P0002 ELECTRICITY AND MAGNETISM
P0003 PUBLISHERS TO THE UNIVERSITY OF Clarendon press Series
P0004 ELECTRICITY AND MAGNETISM
P0005 JAMES CLERK MAXWELL, M.A
P0006 LLD. EDIN., P.E.SS. LONDON AND EDINBURGH HONORARY FELLOW OF TRINITY COLLEGE,
P0007 AND PROFESSOR OF EXPERIMENTAL PHYSICS IN THE UNIVERSITY OF CAMBRIDGE
P0008 AT THE CLARENDON PRESS
P0009 [All rights reserved] v, I
P0010 THE fact that certain bodies, after being rubbed,
P0011 appear to attract other bodies, was known to the ancients. In modern times, a great variety of other phenomena have been observed, and have been found to be related to these phenomena of attraction. They have been classed under the name of Electric phe
P0012 nomena, amber, ?i\eKTpov, having been the substance in which they were first described.
P0013 Other bodies, particularly the loadstone, and pieces of iron and steel which have been subjected to certain
P0014 processes, have also been long known to exhibit phe nomena of action at a distance. These phenomena,
P0015 with others related to them, were found to differ from the electric phenomena, and have been classed under
P0016 the name of Magnetic phenomena, the loadstone, /zayi^?,
P0017 being found in the Thessalian Magnesia.
P0018 These two classes of phenomena have since been found to be related to each other, and the relations between the various phenomena of both classes, so far as they are known, constitute the science of Elec
P0019 tromagnetism. In the following Treatise I propose to describe the
P0020 most important of these phenomena, to shew how they may be subjected to measurement, and to trace the
P0021 mathematical connexions of the quantities measured. Having thus obtained the data for a mathematical
P0022 theory of electromagnetism, and having shewn how this theory may be applied to the calculation of phe
P0023 nomena, I shall endeavour to place in as clear a light as I can the relations between the mathematical form
P0024 of this theory and that of the fundamental science of
P0025 Dynamics, in order that we may be in some degree
P0026 prepared to determine the kind of dynamical pheno
P0027 mena among which we are to look for illustrations or
P0028 explanations of the electromagnetic phenomena. In describing the phenomena, I shall select those
P0029 which most clearly illustrate the fundamental ideas of the theory, omitting others, or reserving them till the reader is more advanced.
P0030 The most important aspect of any phenomenon from a mathematical point of view is that of a measurable
P0031 quantity. I shall therefore consider electrical pheno
P0032 mena chiefly with a view to their measurement, de
P0033 scribing the methods of measurement, and defining the standards on which they depend.
P0034 In the application of mathematics to the calculation of electrical quantities, I shall endeavour in the first place to deduce the most general conclusions from the data at our disposal, and in the next place to apply the results to the simplest cases that can be chosen.
P0035 I shall avoid, as much as I can, those questions which,
P0036 though they have elicited the skill of mathematicians, have not enlarged our knowledge of science. PREFACE. vii
P0037 The internal relations of the different branches of
P0038 the science which we have to study are more numerous
P0039 and complex than those of any other science hitherto developed. Its external relations, on the one hand to dynamics, and on the other to heat, light, chemical action, and the constitution of bodies, seem to indicate the special importance of electrical science as an aid
P0040 to the interpretation of nature. It appears to me, therefore, that the study of elec
P0041 tromagnetism in all its extent has now become of the first importance as a means of promoting the progress
P0042 The mathematical laws of the different classes of
P0043 phenomena have been to a great extent satisfactorily made out.
P0044 The connexions between the different classes of phe nomena have also been investigated, and the proba bility of the rigorous exactness of the experimental laws has been greatly strengthened by a more extended knowledge of their relations to each other.
P0045 Finally, some progress has been made in the re
P0046 duction of electromagnet-ism to a dynamical science,
P0047 by shewing that no electromagnetic phenomenon is contradictory to the supposition that it depends on
P0048 purely dynamical action.
P0049 What has been hitherto done, however, has by no means exhausted the field of electrical research. It
P0050 has rather opened up that field, by pointing out sub jects of enquiry, and furnishing us with means of
P0051 It is hardly necessary to enlarge upon the beneficial viii PREFACE.
P0052 results of magnetic research on navigation, and the importance of a knowledge of the true direction of the compass, and of the effect of the iron in a ship.
P0053 But the labours of those who have endeavoured to render navigation more secure by means of magnetic
P0054 observations have at the same time greatly advanced
P0055 the progress of pure science.
P0056 Gauss, as a member of the German Magnetic Union,
P0057 brought his powerful intellect to bear on the theory of magnetism, and on the methods of observing it, and he not only added greatly to our knowledge of the theory of attractions, but reconstructed the whole of magnetic science as regards the instruments used, the methods of observation, and the calculation of the
P0058 results, so that his memoirs on Terrestrial Magnetism
P0059 may be taken as models of physical research by all those who are engaged in the measurement of any
P0060 of the forces in nature.
P0061 The important applications of electromagnetism to telegraphy have also reacted on pure science by giving a commercial value to accurate electrical measure
P0062 ments, and by affording to electricians the use of apparatus on a scale which greatly transcends that of any ordinary laboratory. The consequences of this demand for electrical knowledge, and of these experi mental opportunities for acquiring it, have been already very great, both in stimulating the energies of ad vanced electricians, and in diffusing among practical
P0063 men a degree of accurate knowledge which is likely
P0064 to conduce to the general scientific progress of the whole engineering profession. PREFACE. ix
P0065 There are several treatises in which electrical and
P0066 magnetic phenomena are described in a popular way.
P0067 These, however, are not what is wanted by those wrho
P0068 have been brought face to face with quantities to be measured, and whose minds do not rest satisfied with
P0069 lecture-room experiments.
P0070 There is also a considerable mass of mathematical
P0071 memoirs which are of great importance in electrical science, but they lie concealed in the bulky Trans actions of learned societies ; they do not form a con nected system ; they are of very unequal merit, and they are for the most part beyond the comprehension of any but professed mathematicians.
P0072 I have therefore thought that a treatise would be useful which should have for its principal object to take up the whole subject in a methodical manner,
P0073 and which should also indicate how each part of the
P0074 subject is brought within the reach of methods of verification by actual measurement.
P0075 The general complexion of the treatise differs con siderably from that of several excellent electrical works, published, most of them, in Germany, and it
P0076 may appear that scant justice is done to the specu lations of several eminent electricians and mathema
P0077 ticians. One reason of this is that before I began the study of electricity I resolved to read no mathe matics on the subject till I had first read through Faraday s Experimental Researches on Electricity. I was aware that there was supposed to be a difference between Faraday s way of conceiving phenomena and
P0078 that of the mathematicians, so that neither he nor x PREFACE.
P0079 they were satisfied with each other s language. I had
P0080 also the conviction that this discrepancy did not arise
P0081 from either party being wrong. I was first convinced of this by Sir William Thomson *, to whose advice and assistance, as well as to his published papers, I owe most of what I have learned on the subject.
P0082 As I proceeded with the study of Faraday, I per ceived that his method of conceiving the phenomena was also a mathematical one, though not exhibited in the conventional form of mathematical symbols. I also found that these methods were capable of being expressed in the ordinary mathematical forms, and thus compared with those of the professed mathema
P0083 For instance, Faraday, in his mind s eye, saw lines of force traversing all space where the mathematicians saw centres of force attracting at a distance : Faraday saw a medium where they saw nothing but distance : Faraday sought the seat of the phenomena in real actions going on in the medium, they were satisfied that they had found it in a power of action at a
P0084 distance impressed on the electric fluids.
P0085 When I had translated what I considered to be
P0086 Faraday s ideas into a mathematical form, I found that in general the results of the two methods coin cided, so that the same phenomena were accounted for, and the same laws of action deduced by both methods, but that Faraday s methods resembled those
P0087 * I take this opportunity of acknowledging my obligations to Sir W. Thomson and to Professor Tait for many valuable suggestions made
P0088 during the printing of this work. PREFACE. xi
P0089 in which we begin with the whole and arrive at the
P0090 parts by analysis, while the ordinary mathematical methods were founded on the principle of beginning with the parts and building up the whole by syn
P0091 I also found that several of the most fertile methods
P0092 of research discovered by the mathematicians could be
P0093 expressed much better in terms of ideas derived from
P0094 Faraday than in their original form. The whole theory, for instance, of the potential, con
P0095 sidered as a quantity which satisfies a certain partial differential equation, belongs essentially to the method which I have called that of Faraday. According to the other method, the potential, if it is to be considered
P0096 at all, must be regarded as the result of a summa
P0097 tion of the electrified particles divided each by its dis
P0098 tance from a given point. Hence many of the mathe
P0099 matical discoveries of Laplace, Poisson, Green and Gauss find their proper place in this treatise, and their appropriate expression in terms of conceptions mainly derived from Faraday.
P0100 Great progress has been made in electrical science, chiefly in Germany, by cultivators of the theory of
P0101 action at a distance. The valuable electrical measure
P0102 ments of W. Weber are interpreted by him according to this theory, and the electromagnetic speculation which was originated by Gauss, and carried on by Weber, Riemann, J. and C. Neumann, Lorenz, &c. is founded on the theory of action at a distance, but depending either directly on the relative velocity of the particles, or on the gradual propagation of something, xii PREFACE.
P0103 whether potential or force, from the one particle to the other. The great success which these eminent
P0104 men have attained in the application of mathematics
P0105 to electrical phenomena gives, as is natural, addi
P0106 tional weight to their theoretical speculations, so that
P0107 those who, as students of electricity, turn to them as
P0108 the greatest authorities in mathematical electricity, would probably imbibe, along with their mathematical
P0109 methods, their physical hypotheses. These physical hypotheses, however, are entirely
P0110 alien from the way of looking at things which I
P0111 adopt, and one object which I have in view is that
P0112 some of those who wish to study electricity may, by reading this treatise, come to see that there is another way of treating the subject, which is no less fitted to
P0113 explain the phenomena, and which, though in some
P0114 parts it may appear less definite, corresponds, as I
P0115 think, more faithfully with our actual knowledge, both in what it affirms and in what it leaves undecided.
P0116 In a philosophical point of view, moreover, it is exceedingly important that two methods should be compared, both of which have succeeded in explaining the principal electromagnetic phenomena, and both of which have attempted to explain the propagation of light as an electromagnetic phenomenon, and have
P0117 actually calculated its velocity, while at the same time the fundamental conceptions of what actually takes place, as well as most of the secondary conceptions of
P0118 the quantities concerned, are radically different.
P0119 I have therefore taken the part of an advocate rather than that of a judge, and have rather exemplified one PREFACE. xiii
P0120 method than attempted to give an impartial description of both. I have no doubt that the method which I
P0121 have called the German one will also find its sup porters, and will be expounded with a skill worthy
P0122 I have not attempted an exhaustive account of elec trical phenomena, experiments, and apparatus. The
P0123 student who desires to read all that is known on these
P0124 subjects will find great assistance from the Traite d Electricite of Professor A. de la Rive, and from several
P0125 German treatises, such as Wiedemann s Galvanismus,
P0126 Riess Reibiingselektricitat, Beer s Einleitung in die Elek
P0127 I have confined myself almost entirely to the ma
P0128 thematical treatment of the subject, but I would recommend the student, after he has learned, experi mentally if possible, what are the phenomena to be observed, to read carefully Faraday s Experimental
P0129 Researches in Electricity. He will there find a strictly contemporary historical account of some of the greatest
P0130 electrical discoveries and investigations, carried on in an order and succession which could hardly have been
P0131 improved if the results had been known from the first, and expressed in the language of a man who devoted much of his attention to the methods of ac
P0132 curately describing scientific operations and their re
P0133 It is of great advantage to the student of any subject to read the original memoirs on that subject, for science is always most completely assimilated when
P0134 * Life and Letters of Faraday, vol. i. p. 395. xiv PREFACE.
P0135 it is in the nascent state, and in the case of Faraday s Researches this is comparatively easy, as they are
P0136 published in a separate form, and may be read con
P0137 secutively. If by anything I have here written I
P0138 may assist any student in understanding Faraday s
P0139 modes of thought and expression, I shall regard it as
P0140 the accomplishment of one of my principal aims to
P0141 communicate to others the same delight which I have found myself in reading Faraday s Researches.
P0142 The description of the phenomena, and the ele mentary parts of the theory of each subject, will be
P0143 found in the earlier chapters of each of the four Parts into which this treatise is divided. The student will
P0144 find in these chapters enough to give him an elementary acquaintance with the whole science.
P0145 The remaining chapters of each Part are occupied with the higher parts of the theory, the processes of numerical calculation, and the instruments and methods
P0146 of experimental research.
P0147 The relations between electromagnetic phenomena and those of radiation, the theory of molecular electric currents, and the results of speculation on the nature
P0148 of action at a distance, are treated of in the last four
P0149 chapters of the second volume.

P0465 ON THE MEASUREMENT OF QUANTITIES.
P0466 1.] EVERY expression of a Quantity consists of two factors or components. One of these is the name of a certain known quan tity of the same kind as the quantity to be expressed, which is taken as a standard of reference. The other component is the number of times the standard is to be taken in order to make up the required quantity. The standard quantity is technically called the Unit, and the number is called the Numerical Value of the
P0467 There must be as many different units as there are different
P0468 kinds of quantities to be measured, but in all dynamical sciences it is possible to define these units in terms of the three funda mental units of Length, Time, and Mass. Thus the units of area and of volume are defined respectively as the square and the cube
P0469 whose sides are the unit of length.
P0470 Sometimes, however, we find several units of the same kind founded on independent considerations. Thus the gallon, or the volume of ten pounds of water, is used as a unit of capacity as well as the cubic foot. The gallon may be a convenient measure in some cases, but it is not a systematic one, since its numerical re lation to the cubic foot is not a round integral number.
P0471 2.] In framing a mathematical system we suppose the funda mental units of length, time, and mass to be given, and deduce all the derivative units from these by the simplest attainable de
P0472 The formulae at which we arrive must be such that a person
P0473 VOL. i. B 2 PRELIMINARY. [3.
P0474 of any nation, by substituting for the different symbols the nu merical value of the quantities as measured by his own national units, would arrive at a true result.
P0475 Hence, in all scientific studies it is of the greatest importance to employ units belonging to a properly defined system, and to
P0476 know the relations of these units to the fundamental units, so that we may be able at once to transform our results from one system to
P0477 This is most conveniently done by ascertaining the dimensions
P0478 of every unit in terms of the three fundamental units. When a
P0479 given unit varies as the ^th power of one of these units, it is said to be of n dimensions as regards that unit.
P0480 For instance, the scientific unit of volume is always the cube whose side is the unit of length. If the unit of length varies, the unit of volume will vary as its third power, and the unit of volume is said to be of three dimensions with respect to the unit of
P0481 A knowledge of the dimensions of units furnishes a test which
P0482 ought to be applied to the equations resulting from any lengthened investigation. The dimensions of every term of such an equa tion, with respect to each of the three fundamental units, must be the same. If not, the equation is absurd, and contains some error, as its interpretation would be different according to the arbi trary system of units which we adopt *.
P0483 The Three Fundamental Units.
P0484 3.] (1) Length. The standard of length for scientific purposes in this country is one foot, which is the third part of the standard yard preserved in the Exchequer Chambers.
P0485 In France, and other countries which have adopted the metric system, it is the metre. The metre is theoretically the ten mil lionth part of the length of a meridian of the earth measured from the pole to the equator ; but practically it is the length of a standard preserved in Paris, which was constructed by Borda to correspond, when at the temperature of melting ice, with the value of the preceding length as measured by Delambre. The metre has not been altered to correspond with new and more accurate measurements of the earth, but the arc of the meridian is estimated in terms of the original metre.
P0486 * The theory of dimensions was first stated by Fourier, Tkeorie de Chaleur, 160. 5-] THE THREE FUNDAMENTAL UNITS. 3
P0487 In astronomy the mean distance of the earth from the sun is
P0488 sometimes taken as a unit of length. In the present state of science the most universal standard of
P0489 length which we could assume would be the wave length in vacuum of a particular kind of light, emitted by some widely diffused sub stance such as sodium, which has well-defined lines in its spectrum. Such a standard would be independent of any changes in the di mensions of the earth, and should be adopted by those who expect their writings to be more permanent than that body.
P0490 In treating of the dimensions of units we shall call the unit of length []. If I is the numerical value of a length, it is under
P0491 stood to be expressed in terms of the concrete unit [_Z/], so that the actual length would be fully expressed by I \L~\.
P0492 4.] (2) Time. The standard unit of time in all civilized coun tries is deduced from the time of rotation of the earth about its
P0493 axis. The sidereal day, or the true period of rotation of the earth, can be ascertained with great exactness by the ordinary observa tions of astronomers ; and the mean solar day can be deduced from this by our knowledge of the length of the year.
P0494 The unit of time adopted in all physical researches is one second of mean solar time.
P0495 In astronomy a year is sometimes used as a unit of time. A
P0496 more universal unit of time might be found by taking the periodic time of vibration of the particular kind of light whose wave length
P0497 is the unit of length.
P0498 We shall call the concrete unit of time \_T~\, and the numerical
P0499 5.] (3) Mass. The standard unit of mass is in this country the avoirdupois pound preserved in the Exchequer Chambers. The grain, which is often used as a unit, is defined to be the 7000th
P0500 part of this pound. In the metrical system it is the gramme, which is theoretically
P0501 the mass of a cubic centimetre of distilled water at standard tem
P0502 perature and pressure, but practically it is the thousandth part of a standard kilogramme preserved in Paris.
P0503 The accuracy with which the masses of bodies can be com pared by weighing is far greater than that hitherto attained in the measurement of lengths, so that all masses ought, if possible, to be compared directly with the standard, and not deduced from experiments on water.
P0504 In descriptive astronomy the mass of the sun or that of the B2 4 PRELIMINARY. [5.
P0505 earth is sometimes taken as a unit, but in the dynamical theory is deduced from the units of time of astronomy the unit of mass and length, combined with the fact of universal gravitation. The astronomical unit of mass is that mass which attracts another
P0506 body placed at the unit of distance so as to produce in that body
P0507 the unit of acceleration.
P0508 In framing a universal system of units we may either deduce
P0509 the unit of mass in this way from those of length and time
P0510 already defined, and this we can do to a rough approximation in
P0511 the present state of science ; or, if we * soon to be able to
P0512 determine the mass of a single molecule of a standard substance,
P0513 we may wait for this determination before fixing a universal
P0514 We shall denote the concrete unit of mass by the symbol \M]
P0515 in treating of the dimensions of other units. The unit of mass
P0516 will be taken as one of the three fundamental units. When, as
P0517 in the French system, a particular substance,, water, is taken as
P0518 a standard of density, then the unit of mass is no longer inde
P0519 pendent, but varies as the unit of volume, or as 3

P0532 6.] The unit of Velocity is that velocity in which unit of length
P0533 is described in unit of time. Its dimensions are 7&quot; 1 [j&T ].
P0534 If we adopt the units of length and time derived from the
P0535 vibrations of light, then the unit of velocity is the velocity of
P0536 The unit of Acceleration is that acceleration in which the velo
P0537 city increases by unity in unit of time. Its dimensions are 2
P0538 The unit of Density is the density of a substance which contains
P0539 unit of mass in unit of volume. Its dimensions are 3
P0540 The unit of Momentum is the momentum of unit of mass moving
P0541 with unit of velocity. Its dimensions are l
P0542 The unit of Force is the force which produces unit of momentum
P0543 in unit of time. Its dimensions are 2
P0544 This is the absolute unit of force, and this definition of it is
P0545 implied in every equation in Dynamics. Nevertheless, in many
P0546 text books in which these equations are given, a different unit of force is adopted, namely, the weight of the national unit of mass ; and then, in order to satisfy the equations, the national unit of mass is itself abandoned, and an artificial unit is adopted as the dynamical
P0547 unit, equal to the national unit divided by the numerical value of the force of gravity at the place. In this way both the unit of force and the unit of mass are made to depend on the value of the force of gravity, which varies from place to place, so that state ments involving these quantities are not complete without a know ledge of the force of gravity in the places where these statements were found to be true.
P0548 The abolition, for all scientific purposes, of this method of mea
P0549 suring forces is mainly due to the introduction of a general system of making observations of magnetic force in countries in which
P0550 the force of gravity is different. All such forces are now measured according to the strictly dynamical method deduced from our definitions, and the numerical results are the same in whatever
P0551 country the experiments are made.
P0552 The unit of Work is the work done by the unit of force acting through the unit of length measured in its own direction. Its dimensions are 2 [J/Z T~ 2].
P0553 The Energy of a system, being its capacity of performing work, is measured by the work which the system is capable of performing by the expenditure of its whole energy. 6 PRELIMINARY. [7.
P0554 The definitions of other quantities, and of the units to which they are referred, will be given when we require them.
P0555 In transforming the values of physical quantities determined in terms of one unit, so as to express them in terms of any other unit
P0556 of the same kind, we have only to remember that every expres sion for the quantity consists of two factors, the unit and the nu merical part which expresses how often the unit is to be taken. Hence the numerical part of the expression varies inversely as the
P0557 magnitude of the unit, that is, inversely as the various powers of the fundamental units which are indicated by the dimensions of the
P0558 On Physical Continuity and Discontinuity.
P0559 7.] A quantity is said to vary continuously when, if it passes
P0560 from one value to another, it assumes all the intermediate values.
P0561 We may obtain the conception of continuity from a consideration
P0562 of the continuous existence of a particle of matter in time and space. Such a particle cannot pass from one position to another without describing a continuous line in space, and the coordinates of its position must be continuous functions of the time.
P0563 In the so-called equation of continuity, as given in treatises on Hydrodynamics, the fact expressed is that matter cannot appear in or disappear from an element of volume without passing in or out
P0564 through the sides of that element.
P0565 A quantity is said to be a continuous function of its variables
P0566 when, if the variables alter continuously, the quantity itself alters
P0567 Thus, if u is a function of x, and if, while x passes continuously from # to fl?1} u passes continuously from n to ult but when x

P0572 the value x = x^ as the limit of the fraction

P0615 Periodic and Multiple Functions.
P0616 9.] If u is a function of x such that its value is the same for
P0617 a?, x + at x + na, and all values of x differing by a, u is called a
P0618 periodic function of x, and a is called its period. If x is considered as a function of u, then, for a given value of
P0619 U, there must be an infinite series of values of x differing by multiples of a. In this case x is called a multiple function of u, and a is called its cyclic constant.

P0622 On the Relation of Physical Quantities to Directions in Space.
P0623 10.] t ln distinguishing the kinds of physical quantities, it is of
P0624 great importance to know how they are related to the directions of those coordinate axes which we usually employ in defining the positions of things. The introduction of coordinate axes into geo metry by Des Cartes was one of the greatest steps in mathematical
P0625 progress, for it reduced the methods of geometry to calculations performed on numerical quantities. The position of a point is made to depend on the length of three lines which are always drawn in determinate directions, and the line joining two points is in like manner considered as the resultant of three lines.
P0626 But for many purposes in physical reasoning, as distinguished II.] VECTORS, OR DIRECTED QUANTITIES. 9
P0627 from calculation, it is desirable to avoid explicitly introducing the Cartesian coordinates, and to fix the mind at once on a point of space instead of its three coordinates, and on the magnitude and direction of a force instead of its three components. This mode of contemplating geometrical and physical quantities is more prim itive and more natural than the other, although the ideas connected
P0628 with it did not receive their full development till Hamilton made the next great step in dealing with space, by the invention of his
P0629 Calculus of Quaternions.
P0630 As the methods of Des Cartes are still the most familiar to
P0631 students of science, and as they are really the most useful for purposes of calculation, we shall express all our results in the
P0632 Cartesian form. I am convinced, however, that the introduction
P0633 of the ideas, as distinguished from the operations and methods of Quaternions, will be of great use to us in the study of all parts of our subject, and especially in electrodynamics, where we have to deal with a number of physical quantities, the relations of which to each other can be expressed far more simply by a few words of Hamilton s, than by the ordinary equations.
P0634 11.] One of the most important features of Hamilton s method is the division of quantities into Scalars and Vectors.
P0635 A Scalar quantity is capable of being completely defined by a
P0636 single numerical specification. Its numerical value does not in
P0637 any way depend on the directions we assume for the coordinate
P0638 A Vector, or Directed quantity, requires for its definition three
P0639 numerical specifications, and these may most simply be understood
P0640 as having reference to the directions of the coordinate axes. Scalar quantities do not involve direction. The volume of a
P0641 geometrical figure, the mass and the energy of a material body, the hydrostatical pressure at a point in a fluid, and the potential at a point in space, are examples of scalar quantities.
P0642 A vector quantity has direction as well as magnitude, and is
P0643 such that a reversal of its direction reverses its sign. The dis placement of a point, represented by a straight line drawn from
P0644 its original to its final position, may be taken as the typical vector quantity, from which indeed the name of Vector is derived.
P0645 The velocity of a body, its momentum, the force acting on it, an electric current, the magnetization of a particle of iron, are
P0646 instances of vector quantities.
P0647 There are physical quantities of another kind which are related 1 PRELIMINARY. [ I 2.
P0648 to directions in space, but which are not vectors. Stresses and strains in solid bodies are examples of these, and the properties of bodies considered in the theory of elasticity and in the theory of double refraction. Quantities of this class require for their definition nine numerical specifications. They are expressed in the language of Quaternions by linear and vector functions of a vector.
P0649 The addition of one vector quantity to another of the same kind is performed according to the rule given in Statics for the com position of forces. In fact, the proof which Poisson gives of the
P0650 parallelogram of forces is applicable to the composition of any quantities such that a reversal of their sign is equivalent to turning them end for end.
P0651 When we wish to denote a vector quantity by a single symbol,
P0652 and to call attention to the fact that it is a vector, so that we must consider its direction as well as its magnitude, we shall denote it by a German capital letter, as fy, 33, &c.
P0653 In the calculus of Quaternions, the position of a point in space is defined by the vector drawn from a fixed point, called the origin,
P0654 to that point. If at that point of space we have to consider any physical quantity whose value depends on the position of the point, that quantity is treated as a function of the vector drawn from the origin. The function may be itself either scalar or vector. The density of a body, its temperature, its hydrostatic pressure, the potential at a point, are examples of scalar functions. The
P0655 resultant force at the point, the velocity of a fluid at that point, the velocity of rotation of an element of the fluid, and the couple
P0656 producing rotation, are examples of vector functions.
P0657 12.] Physical vector quantities may be divided into two classes,
P0658 in one of which the quantity is defined with reference to a line, while in the other the quantity is defined with reference to an
P0659 For instance, the resultant of an attractive force in any direction
P0660 may be measured by finding the work which it would do on a body if the body were moved a short distance in that direction
P0661 and dividing it by that short distance. Here the attractive force is defined with reference to a line.
P0662 On the other hand, the flux of heat in any direction at any point of a solid body may be defined as the quantity of heat which
P0663 crosses a small area drawn perpendicular to that direction divided by that area and by the time. Here the flux is defined with reference to an area. 13.] FORCES AND FLUXES. 11
P0664 There are certain cases in which a quantity may be measured
P0665 with reference to a line as well as with reference to an area.
P0666 Thus, in treating- of the displacements of elastic solids, we may
P0667 direct our attention either to the original and the actual position
P0668 of a particle, in which case the displacement of the particle is
P0669 measured by the line drawn from the first position to the second, a small area fixed in space, and determine or we may consider solid passes across that area during the dis
P0670 what quantity of the
P0671 In the same way the velocity of a fluid may be investigated
P0672 either with respect to the actual velocity of the individual parti cles, or with respect to the quantity of the fluid which flows through
P0673 But in these cases we require to know separately the density of
P0674 the body as well as the displacement or velocity, in order to apply the first method, and whenever we attempt to form a molecular theory we have to use the second method.
P0675 In the case of the flow of electricity we do not know anything of its density or its velocity in the conductor, we only know the
P0676 value of what, on the fluid theory, would correspond to the product of the density and the velocity. Hence in all such cases we must apply the more general method of measurement of the flux across an area.
P0677 In electrical science, electromotive force and magnetic force belong to the first class, being defined with reference to lines.
P0678 When we wish to indicate this fact, we may refer to them as
P0679 On the other hand, electric and magnetic induction, and electric
P0680 currents, belong to the second class, being defined with reference
P0681 to areas. When we wish to indicate this fact, we shall refer to them
P0682 Each of these forces may be considered as producing, or tending
P0683 to produce, its corresponding flux. Thus, electromotive force pro
P0684 duces electric currents in conductors, and tends to produce them in dielectrics. It produces electric induction in dielectrics, and pro bably in conductors also. In the same sense, magnetic force pro duces magnetic induction.
P0685 13.] In some cases the flux is simply proportional to the force and in the same direction, but in other cases we can only affirm that the direction and magnitude of the flux are functions of the direction and magnitude of the force. 12 PRELIM1NAKY. [14.
P0686 The case in which the components of the flux are linear functions of those of the force is discussed in the chapter on the Equations of Conduction, Art. 296. There are in general nine coefficients which determine the relation between the force and the flux. In
P0687 certain cases we have reason to believe that six of these coefficients
P0688 form three pairs of equal quantities. In such cases the relation be tween the line of direction of the force and the normal plane of the flux is of the same kind as that between a diameter of an ellipsoid and its conjugate diametral plane. In Quaternion language, the one vector is said to be a linear and vector function of the other, and
P0689 when there are three pairs of equal coefficients the function is said
P0690 to be self-conjugate.
P0691 In the case of magnetic induction in iron, the flux, (the mag
P0692 netization of the iron,) is not a linear function of the magnetizing force. In all cases, however, the product of the force and the flux resolved in its direction, gives a result of scientific import ance, and this is always a scalar quantity.
P0693 14.] There are two mathematical operations of frequent occur rence which are appropriate to these two classes of vectors, or
P0694 directed quantities.
P0695 In the case of forces, we have to take the integral along a line of the product of an element of the line, and the resolved part of the force along that element. The result of this operation is called the Line-integral of the force. It represents the work done on a body carried along the line. In certain cases in which
P0696 the line-integral does not depend on the form of the line, but only on the position of its extremities, the line-integral is called the Potential.
P0697 In the case of fluxes, we have to take the integral, over a surface, of the flux through every element of the surface. The result of
P0698 this operation is called the Surface-integral of the flux. It repre
P0699 sents the quantity which passes through the surface. There are certain surfaces across which there is no flux. If
P0700 two of these surfaces intersect, their line of intersection is a line of flux. In those cases in which the flux is in the same direction
P0701 as the force, lines of this kind are often called Lines of Force. It
P0702 would be more correct, however, to speak of them in electrostatics and magnetics as Lines of Induction, and in electrokinematics as
P0703 15.] There is another distinction between different kinds of
P0704 directed quantities, which, though very important in a physical 1 6.] LINE-INTEGRALS. 13
P0705 point of view, is not so necessary to be observed for the sake of the mathematical methods. This is the distinction between longi tudinal and rotational properties.
P0706 The direction and magnitude of a quantity may depend upon
P0707 some action or effect which takes place entirely along a certain
P0708 line, or it may depend upon something of the nature of rota
P0709 tion about that line as an axis. The laws of combination of
P0710 directed quantities are the same whether they are longitudinal or rotational, so that there is no difference in the mathematical treat
P0711 ment of the two classes, but there may be physical circumstances which indicate to which class we must refer a particular pheno
P0712 menon. Thus, electrolysis consists of the transfer of certain sub stances along a line in one direction, and of certain other sub stances in the opposite direction, which is evidently a longitudinal phenomenon, and there is no evidence of any rotational effect about the direction of the force. Hence we infer that the electric
P0713 current which causes or accompanies electrolysis is a longitudinal, and not a rotational phenomenon.
P0714 On the other hand, the north and south poles of a magnet do
P0715 not differ as oxygen and hydrogen do, which appear at opposite
P0716 places during electrolysis, so that we have no evidence that mag
P0717 netism is a longitudinal phenomenon, while the effect of magnetism
P0718 in rotating the plane of polarized light distinctly shews that mag
P0719 netism is a rotational phenomenon.

P0797 Let there be p points in space, and let I lines of any form be
P0798 drawn joining these points so that no two lines intersect each
P0799 other, and no point is left isolated. We shall call a figure com
P0800 posed of lines in this way a Diagram. Of these lines, p 1 are
P0801 sufficient to join the p points so as to form a connected system.
P0802 Every new line completes a loop or closed path, or, as we shall
P0803 call it, a Cycle. The number of independent cycles in the diagram
P0804 is therefore K = I jo+1.
P0805 Any closed path drawn along the lines of the diagram is com
P0806 posed of these independent cycles, each being taken any number of
P0807 times and in either direction.
P0808 The existence of cycles is called Cyclosis, and the number of cycles in a diagram is called its Cyclomatic number.
P0809 Cyclosis in Surfaces and Regions.
P0810 Surfaces are either complete or bounded. Complete surfaces are either infinite or closed. Bounded surfaces are limited by one or
P0811 more closed lines, which may in the limiting cases become finite
P0812 A finite region of space is bounded by one or more closed
P0813 surfaces. Of these one is the external surface, the others are
P0814 * Der Census RaumlicTier Complexe, Gott. Abh., Bd. x. S. 97 (1861). 19.] CYCLIC REGIONS. 17
P0815 included in it and exclude each other, and are called internal
P0816 If the region has one bounding- surface, we may suppose that
P0817 surface to contract inwards without breaking its continuity or cutting itself. If the region is one of simple continuity, such as
P0818 a sphere, this process may be continued till it is reduced to a
P0819 point; but if the region is like a ring, the result will be a closed curve; and if the region has multiple connexions, the result will
P0820 be a diagram of lines, and the cyclomatic number of the diagram will be that of the region. The space outside the region has the same cyclomatic number as the region itself. Hence, if the region is bounded by internal as well as external surfaces, its cyclomatic number is the sum of those due to all the surfaces.
P0821 When a region encloses within itself other regions, it is called a
P0822 Periphractic region.
P0823 The number of internal bounding surfaces of a region is called
P0824 its periphractic number. A closed surface is also periphractic, its
P0825 number being unity. The cyclomatic number of a closed surface is twice that of the
P0826 region which it bounds. To find the cyclomatic number of a bounded surface, suppose all the boundaries to contract inwards, without breaking continuity, till they meet. The surface will then be reduced to a point in the case of an acyclic surface, or to a linear diagram in the case of cyclic surfaces. The cyclomatic number of the diagram is that of the surface.

P0873 The condition that Xdx+Ydy -\- Zdz is a complete differential of some function ^ for all points within a certain region, occurs in
P0874 several physical investigations in which the directed quantity and the potential have different physical interpretations.
P0875 In pure kinematics we may suppose X, Y, Z to be the com
P0876 ponents of the displacement of a point of a continuous body whose original coordinates are x, y, z, then the condition expresses that these displacements constitute a non-rotational strain f.

P0879 fluid is irrotational.
P0880 If X, Y, Z represent the components of the force at the point

P0973 Then, if a closed surface S is drawn within the region St the value of its surface-integral will be zero only when this surface /S&quot; does not include any of the enclosed regions S19 S2 , &c. If it includes any of these, the surface-integral is the sum of the surface
P0974 integrals of the different enclosed regions which lie within it.
P0975 For the same reason, the surface-integral taken over a surface
P0976 bounded by a closed curve is the same for such surfaces only bounded
P0977 by the closed curve as are reconcileable with the given surface by
P0978 continuous motion of the surface within the region S.
P0979 When we have to deal with a periphractic region, the first thing
P0980 to be done is to reduce it to an aperiphractic region by drawing lines joining the different bounding surfaces. Each of these lines,
P0981 provided it joins surfaces which were not already in continuous connexion, reduces the periphractic number by unity, so that the whole number of lines to be drawn to remove the periphraxy is equal to the periphractic number, or the number of internal sur
P0982 faces. When these lines have been drawn we may assert that if
P0983 the solenoidal condition is fulfilled in the region S, any closed surface drawn entirely within S, and not cutting any of the lines, has its
P0984 surface-integral zero.
P0985 In drawing these lines we must remember that any line joining
P0986 surfaces which are already connected does not diminish the peri
P0987 phraxy, but introduces cyclosis.
P0988 The most familiar example of a periphractic region within which the solenoidal condition is fulfilled is the region surrounding a mass
P0989 attracting or repelling inversely as the square of the distance.
P0990 In this case we have
P0991 X= m /&amp;gt;* Y= m y* g
P0992 &amp;gt; &amp;gt; Z= m 3 ;
P0993 where m is the mass supposed to be at the origin of coordinates.

P0996 but at the origin these quantities become infinite. For any closed surface not including the origin, the surface-integral is zero. If a closed surface includes the origin, its surface-integral is 4?m.

P1000 On Right-handed and Left-handed Relations in Space.
P1001 23.] In this treatise the motions of translation along any axis and of rotation about that axis, will be assumed to be of the same sign when their directions correspond to those of the translation and rotation of an ordinary or right-handed screw *.
P1002 For instance, if the actual rotation of the earth from west to east
P1003 is taken positive, the direction of the earth s axis from south to
P1004 north will be taken positive, and if a man walks forward in the
P1005 positive direction, the positive rotation is in the order, head, right
P1006 hand, feet, left-hand.
P1007 If we place ourselves on the positive side of a surface, the positive direction along its bounding curve will be opposite to the motion of the hands of a watch with its face towards us.
P1008 This is the right-handed system which is adopted in Thomson and Tait s Natural Philosophy, 243. The opposite, or left-handed system, is adopted in Hamilton s and Tait s Quaternions. The operation of passing from the one system to the other is called, by
P1009 Listing, Perversion.
P1010 The reflexion of an object in a mirror is a perverted image of the
P1011 &quot;When we use the Cartesian axes of %, y, z, we shall draw them
P1012 * The combined action of the muscles of the arm when we turn the upper side of the right-hand outwards, and at the same time thrust the hand forwards, will impress the right-handed screw motion on the memory more firmly than any verbal
P1013 definition. A common corkscrew may be used as a material symbol of the same
P1014 Professor W. H. Miller has suggested to me that as the tendrils of the vine are
P1015 right-handed screws and those of the hop left-handed, the two systems of relations in space might be called those of the vine and the hop respectively.
P1016 The system of the vine, which we adopt, is that of Linnseus, and of screw-makers in all civilized countries except Japan. De Candolle was the first who called the hop-tendril right-handed, and in this he is followed by Listing, and by most writers on the rotatory polarization of light. Screws like the hop-tendril are made for the couplings of railway-carriages, and for the fittings of wheels on the left side of ordinary carriages, but they are always called left-handed screws by those who use them. 24.] LINE-INTEGRAL AND SURFACE-INTEGRAL. 25

P1136 DESCRIPTION OF PHENOMENA.
P1137 Electrification by Friction.
P1138 27.] EXPERIMENT I *&quot;. Let a piece of glass and a piece of resin, neither of which exhibits any electrical properties, be rubbed to gether and left with the rubbed surfaces in contact. They will still exhibit no electrical properties. Let them be separated. They will now attract each other.
P1139 If a second piece of glass be rubbed with a second piece of resin, and if the pieces be then separated and suspended in the
P1140 neighbourhood of the former pieces of glass and resin,, it may be
P1141 observed (1) That the two pieces of glass repel each other. (2) That each piece of glass attracts each piece of resin. (3) That the two pieces of resin repel each other.
P1142 These phenomena of attraction and repulsion are called Elec trical phenomena, and the bodies which exhibit them are said to be electrified, or to be charged with electricity.
P1143 Bodies may be electrified in many other ways, as well as by
P1144 The electrical properties of the two pieces of glass are similar to each other but opposite to those of the two pieces of resin, the glass attracts what the resin repels and repels what the resin
P1145 * See Sir W. Thomson On the Mathematical Theory of Electricity, Cambridge
P1146 and Dublin Mathematical Journal, March, 1848. 28.] ELECTRIFICATION. 31
P1147 If a body electrified in any manner whatever behaves as the glass does, that is, if it repels the glass and attracts the resin, the body is said to be vitreously electrified, and if it attracts the glass and repels the resin it is said to be resinously electrified. All electrified bodies are found to be either vitreously or resinously
P1148 It is the established practice of men of science to call the vitreous
P1149 electrification positive, and the resinous electrification negative. The exactly opposite properties of the two kinds of electrification justify us in indicating them by opposite signs, but the applica tion of the positive sign to one rather than to the other kind must be considered as a matter of arbitrary convention, just as it is a matter of convention in mathematical diagrams to reckon positive distances towards the right hand.
P1150 No force, either of attraction or of repulsion, can be observed
P1151 between an electrified body and a body not electrified. When, in any case, bodies not previously electrified are observed to be acted on by an electrified body, it is because they have become electrified
P1152 Electrification by Induction.
P1153 28.] EXPERIMENT II *. Let a hollow vessel of metal be hung up by white silk threads, and let a similar thread
P1154 be attached to the lid of the vessel so that the vessel
P1155 may be opened or closed without touching it.
P1156 Let the pieces of glass and resin be similarly sus pended and electrified as before.
P1157 Let the vessel be originally unelectrified, then if
P1158 an electrified piece of glass is hung up within it by its thread without touching the vessel, and the lid
P1159 closed, the outside of the vessel will be found to
P1160 be vitreously electrified, and it may be shewn that
P1161 the electrification outside of the vessel is exactly the same in whatever part of the interior space the glass is suspended.
P1162 If the glass is now taken out of the vessel without touching it, the electrification of the glass will be the same as before it was
P1163 put in, and that of the vessel will have disappeared. This electrification of the vessel, which depends on the glass
P1164 * This, and several experiments which follow, are due to Faraday, On Static 1
P1165 Electrical Inductive Action, Phil. Mag., 1843, or Exp. Res., vol. ii. p. 279. 32 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [29.
P1166 being within it, and which vanishes when the glass is removed, is called Electrification by induction.
P1167 Similar effects would be produced if the glass were suspended
P1168 near the vessel on the outside, but in that case we should find
P1169 an electrification vitreous in one part of the outside of the vessel
P1170 and resinous in another. When the glass is inside the vessel
P1171 the whole of the outside is vitreously and the whole of the inside
P1172 resinously electrified.
P1173 Electrification ly Conduction.
P1174 29.] EXPERIMENT III. Let the metal vessel be electrified by induction, as in the last experiment, let a second metallic body be suspended by white silk threads near it, and let a metal wire, similarly suspended, be brought so as to touch simultaneously the electrified vessel and the second body.
P1175 The second body will now be found to be vitreously electrified,
P1176 and the vitreous electrification of the vessel will have diminished.
P1177 The electrical condition has been transferred from the vessel to
P1178 the second body by means of the wire. The wire is called a con ductor of electricity, and the second body is said to be electrified
P1179 Conductors and Insulators.
P1180 EXPERIMENT IV. If a glass rod, a stick of resin or gutta-percha, or a white silk thread, had been used instead of the metal wire, no transfer of electricity would have taken place. Hence these latter substances are called Non-conductors of electricity. Non-conduc tors are used in electrical experiments to support electrified bodies without carrying off their electricity. They are then called In
P1181 The metals are good conductors ; air, glass, resins, gutta-percha, vulcanite, paraffin, &c. are good insulators; but, as we shall see afterwards, all substances resist the passage of electricity, and all substances allow it to pass, though in exceedingly different degrees. This subject will be considered when we come to treat of the Motion of electricity. For the present we shall consider only two classes of bodies, good conductors, and good insulators.
P1182 In Experiment II an electrified body produced electrification in the metal vessel while separated from it by air, a non-conducting medium. Such a medium, considered as transmitting these electrical effects without conduction, has been called by Faraday a Dielectric 31.] SUMMATION OF ELECTRIC EFFECTS. 33
P1183 medium, and the action which takes place through it is called
P1184 In Experiment III the electrified vessel produced electrification
P1185 in the second metallic body through the medium of the wire. Let us suppose the wire removed, and the electrified piece of glass taken out of the vessel without touching it, and removed to a sufficient distance. The second body will still exhibit vitreous electrifica tion, but the vessel, when the glass is removed, will have resinous electrification. If we now bring the wire into contact with both
P1186 bodies, conduction will take place along the wire, and all electri fication will disappear from both bodies, shewing that the elec trification of the two bodies was equal and opposite.
P1187 30.] EXPERIMENT V. In Experiment II it was shewn that if a piece of glass, electrified by rubbing it with resin, is hung up in an insulated metal vessel, the electrification observed outside does
P1188 not depend on the position of the glass. If we now introduce the
P1189 piece of resin with which the glass was rubbed into the same vessel, without touching it or the vessel, it will be found that there is
P1190 no electrification outside the vessel. From this we conclude that
P1191 the electrification of the resin is exactly equal and opposite to that
P1192 of the glass. By putting in any number of bodies, electrified in any way, it may be shewn that the electrification of the outside of the vessel is that due to the algebraic sum of all the electrifica
P1193 tions, those being reckoned negative which are resinous. We have
P1194 thus a practical method of adding the electrical effects of several
P1195 bodies without altering the electrification of each. 31.] EXPERIMENT VI. Let a second insulated metallic vessel, J5,
P1196 be provided, and let the electrified piece of glass be put into the first vessel A, and the electrified piece of resin into the second vessel B. Let the two vessels be then put in communication by the metal wire, as in Experiment III. All signs of electrification will dis
P1197 Next, let the wire be removed, and let the pieces of glass and of
P1198 resin be taken out of the vessels without touching them. It will
P1199 be found that A is electrified resinously and B vitreously. If now the glass and the vessel A be introduced together into a
P1200 larger insulated vessel C, it will be found that there is no elec
P1201 trification outside C. This shews that the electrification of A is
P1202 exactly equal and opposite to that of the piece of glass, and that
P1203 of B may be shewn in the same way to be equal and opposite to that
P1204 of the piece of resin.
P1205 VOL. I. D 34 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [32.
P1206 We have thus obtained a method of charging a vessel with a
P1207 quantity of electricity exactly equal and opposite to that of an
P1208 electrified body without altering the electrification of the latter,
P1209 and we may in this way charge any number of vessels with exactly equal quantities of electricity of either kind, which we may take
P1210 for provisional units.
P1211 32.] EXPERIMENT VII. Let the vessel B, charged with a quan tity of positive electricity, which we shall call, for the present,
P1212 unity, be introduced into the larger insulated vessel C without
P1213 touching it. It will produce a positive electrification on the out
P1214 side of C. Now let B be made to touch the inside of C. No change of the external electrification will be observed. If B is now taken
P1215 out of C without touching it, and removed to a sufficient distance,
P1216 it will be found that B is completely discharged, and that C has
P1217 become charged with a unit of positive electricity.
P1218 We have thus a method of transferring the charge of B to C. Let B be now recharged with a unit of electricity, introduced
P1219 into C already charged, made to touch the inside of C, and re
P1220 moved. It will be found that B is again completely discharged,
P1221 so that the charge of C is doubled.
P1222 If this process is repeated, it will be found that however highly
P1223 C is previously charged, and in whatever way B is charged, when B is first entirely enclosed in C, then made to touch C, and finally removed without touching C, the charge of B is completely trans ferred to C, and B is entirely free from electrification.
P1224 This experiment indicates a method of charging a body with
P1225 any number of units of electricity. We shall find, when we come
P1226 to the mathematical theory of electricity, that the result of this
P1227 experiment affords an accurate test of the truth of the theory.
P1228 33.] Before we proceed to the investigation of the law of electrical force, let us enumerate the facts we have already esta
P1229 By placing any electrified system inside an insulated hollow con
P1230 ducting vessel, and examining the resultant effect on the outside of the vessel, we ascertain the character of the total electrification
P1231 of the system placed inside, without any communication of elec tricity between the different bodies of the system.
P1232 The electrification of the outside of the vessel may be tested with great delicacy by putting it in communication with an elec
P1233 We may suppose the electroscope to consist of a strip of gold 34-] ELECTRICITY AS A QUANTITY. 35
P1234 leaf hanging between two bodies charged, one positively, and the other negatively. If the gold leaf becomes electrified it will incline
P1235 towards the body whose electrification is opposite to its own. By
P1236 increasing the electrification of the two bodies and the delicacy of the suspension, an exceedingly small electrification of the gold leaf
P1237 may be detected. When we come to describe electrometers and multipliers we
P1238 shall find that there are still more delicate methods of detecting electrification and of testing the accuracy of our theorems, but at present we shall suppose the testing to be made by connecting the hollow vessel with a gold leaf electroscope.
P1239 This method was used by Faraday in his very admirable de monstration of the laws of electrical phenomena *.
P1240 34.] I. The total electrification of a body, or system of bodies, remains always the same, except in so far as it receives electrifi cation from or gives electrification to other bodies.
P1241 In all electrical experiments the electrification of bodies is found to change, but it is always found that this change is due to want of perfect insulation, and that as the means of insulation are im
P1242 proved, the loss of electrification becomes less. We may therefore
P1243 assert that the electrification of a body placed in a perfectly in sulating medium would remain perfectly constant.
P1244 II. When one body electrifies another by conduction, the total
P1245 electrification of the two bodies remains the same, that is, the one
P1246 loses as much positive or gains as much negative electrification as
P1247 the other gains of positive or loses of negative electrification. For if the two bodies are enclosed in the hollow vessel, no change
P1248 of the total electrification is observed.
P1249 III. When electrification is produced by friction, or by any
P1250 other known method, equal quantities of positive and negative elec
P1251 trification are produced.
P1252 For the electrification of the whole system may be tested in the hollow vessel, or the process of electrification may be carried
P1253 on within the vessel itself, and however intense the electrification of
P1254 the parts of the system may be, the electrification of the whole,
P1255 as indicated by the gold leaf electroscope, is invariably zero. The electrification of a body is therefore a physical quantity
P1256 capable of measurement, and two or more electrifications can be combined experimentally with a result of the same kind as when
P1257 * On Static Electrical Inductive Action, Phil. Mag., 1843, or Exp. Res., vol. ii.
P1258 p. 249. D2 36 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [35.
P1259 two quantities are added algebraically. We therefore are entitled
P1260 to use language fitted to deal with electrification as a quantity as
P1261 well as a quality, and to speak of any electrified body as (
P1262 with a certain quantity of positive or negative electricity/
P1263 35.] While admitting electricity, as we have now done, to the rank of a physical quantity, we must not too hastily assume that
P1264 it is, or is not, a substance, or that it is, or is not, a form of
P1265 energy, or that it belongs to any known category of physical
P1266 quantities. All that we have hitherto proved is that it cannot
P1267 be created or annihilated, so that if the total quantity of elec
P1268 tricity within a closed surface is increased or diminished, the in
P1269 crease or diminution must have passed in or out through the closed
P1270 This is true of matter, and is expressed by the equation known as
P1271 the Equation of Continuity in Hydrodynamics.
P1272 It is not true of heat, for heat may be increased or diminished
P1273 within a closed surface, without passing in or out through the surface, by the transformation of some other form of energy into heat, or of heat into some other form of energy.
P1274 It is not true even of energy in general if we admit the imme
P1275 diate action of bodies at a distance. For a body outside the closed
P1276 surface may make an exchange of energy with a body within the surface. But if all apparent action at a distance is the
P1277 result of the action between the parts of an intervening medium, and if the nature of this action of the parts of the medium is
P1278 clearly understood, then it is conceivable that in all cases of the
P1279 increase or diminution of the energy within a closed surface we may be able to trace the passage of the energy in or out through
P1280 There is, however, another reason which warrants us in asserting that electricity, as a physical quantity, synonymous with the total
P1281 electrification of a body, is not, like heat, a form of energy. An
P1282 electrified system has a certain amount of energy, and this energy can be calculated by multiplying the quantity of electricity in each of its parts by another physical quantity, called the Potential of that part, and taking half the sum of the products. The quan tities Electricity and Potential, when multiplied together, produce the quantity Energy. It is impossible, therefore, that electricity and energy should be quantities of the same category, for electricity is only one of the factors of energy, the other factor
P1283 being Potential. 36.] THEORIES OF ELECTRICITY. 37

P1293 36.] In most theories on the subject, Electricity is treated as a substance, but inasmuch as there are two kinds of electrification which, being combined, annul each other, and since we cannot conceive of two substances annulling each other, a distinction has been drawn between Free Electricity and Combined Electricity.
P1294 Theory of Two Fluids.
P1295 In the theory called that of Two Fluids, all bodies, in their
P1296 unelectrified state, are supposed to be charged with equal quan
P1297 tities of positive and negative electricity. These quantities are
P1298 supposed to be so great that no process of electrification has ever
P1299 yet deprived a body of all the electricity of either kind. The pro
P1300 cess of electrification, according to this theory, consists in taking
P1301 N a certain quantity P of positive electricity from the body A and communicating it to B, or in taking a quantity of negative
P1302 electricity from B and communicating it to A^ or in some com
P1303 N bination of these processes. units of negative
P1304 The result will be that A will have P +
P1305 electricity over and above its remaining positive electricity, which
P1306 N is supposed to be in a state of combination with an equal quantity
P1307 of negative electricity. This quantity P + is called the Free
P1308 electricity, the rest is called the Combined, Latent, or Fixed elec
P1309 In most expositions of this theory the two electricities are called Fluids, because they are capable of being transferred from one body to another, and are, within conducting bodies, extremely 38 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [36.
P1310 mobile. The other properties of fluids, such as their inertia, weight, and elasticity, are not attributed to them by those who
P1311 have used the theory for merely mathematical purposes ; but the
P1312 use of the word Fluid has been apt to mislead the vulgar, 1
P1313 many men of science who are not natural philosophers, and who
P1314 have seized on the word Fluid as the only term in the statement
P1315 of the theory which seemed intelligible to them.
P1316 We shall see that the mathematical treatment of the subject has
P1317 been greatly developed by writers who express themselves in terms of the Two Fluids theory. . Their results, however, have been
P1318 deduced entirely from data which can be proved by experiment,
P1319 and which must therefore be true, whether we adopt the theory of
P1320 two fluids or not. The experimental verification of the mathe
P1321 matical results therefore is no evidence for or against the peculiar
P1322 doctrines of this theory.
P1323 The introduction of two fluids permits us to consider the negative
P1324 electrification of A and the positive electrification of B as the effect
P1325 of any one of three different processes which would lead to the same
P1326 result. We have already supposed it produced by the transfer of P units of positive electricity from A to B, together with the
P1327 N transfer of units of negative electricity from B to A. But if P+N units of positive electricity had been transferred from A N to B, or if P + units of negative electricity had been transferred
P1328 from B to A, the resulting free electricity on A and on B would
P1329 have been the same as before, but the quantity of combined
P1330 electricity in A would have been less in the second case and greater
P1331 in the third than it was in the first.
P1332 It would appear therefore, according to this theory, that it is possible to alter not only the amount of free electricity in a body, but the amount of combined electricity. But no phenomena have ever been observed in electrified bodies which can be traced to the
P1333 varying amount of their combined electricities. Hence either the combined electricities have no observable properties, or the amount of the combined electricities is incapable of variation. The first -of these alternatives presents no difficulty to the mere mathema tician, who attributes no properties to the fluids except those of .attraction and repulsion, for in this point of view the two fluids simply annul one another, and their combination is a true mathe matical zero. But to those who cannot use the word Fluid without
P1334 thinking of a substance it is difficult to conceive that the com bination of the two fluids shall have no properties at all, so that 37-] THEORIES OF ONE AND OP TWO FLUIDS. 39
P1335 the addition of more or less of the combination to a body shall not in any way affect it, either by increasing- its mass or its weight, or altering some of its other properties. Hence it has been supposed by some, that in every process of electrification exactly equal quan tities of the two fluids are transferred in opposite directions, so that the total quantity of the two fluids in any body taken to
P1336 gether remains always the same. By this new law they contrive
P1337 to save appearances, forgetting that there would have been no need of the law except to reconcile the two fluids theory with facts, and to prevent it from predicting non-existent phenomena.
P1338 Theory of One Fluid.
P1339 37.] In the theory of One Fluid everything is the same as in the theory of Two Fluids except that, instead of supposing the two
P1340 substances equal and opposite in all respects, one of them, gene rally the negative one, has been endowed with the properties and name of Ordinary Matter, while the other retains the name of The Electric Fluid. The particles of the fluid are supposed to repel one another according to the law of the inverse square of the distance, and to attract those of matter according to the same law. Those of matter are supposed to repel each other and attract those of electricity. The attraction, however, between units of the different substances at unit of distance is supposed to be a very little greater than the repulsion between units of the same kind, so that a unit of matter combined with a unit of electricity will exert a force of attraction on a similar combination at a distance, this force, however, being exceedingly small compared with the force between two uncombined units.
P1341 This residual force is supposed to account for the attraction of gravitation. Unelectrified bodies are supposed to be charged with
P1342 as many units of electricity as they contain of ordinary matter.
P1343 When they contain more electricity or less, they are said to be
P1344 positively or negatively electrified. This theory does not, like the Two-Fluid theory, explain too
P1345 much. It requires us, however, to suppose the mass of the electric fluid so small that no attainable positive or negative electrification has yet perceptibly increased or diminished either the mass or the weight of a body, and it has not yet been able to assign sufficient
P1346 reasons why the vitreous rather than the resinous electrification
P1347 should be supposed due to an excess of electricity.
P1348 One objection has sometimes been urged against this theory by 40 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [38.
P1349 men who ought to have reasoned better. It has been said that
P1350 the doctrine that the particles of matter uncombined with elec tricity repel one another, is in direct antagonism with the well established fact that every particle of matter attracts every other particle throughout the universe. If the theory of One Fluid were true we should have the heavenly bodies repelling one another.
P1351 But it is manifest that the heavenly bodies, according to this theory, if they consisted of matter uncombined with electricity, would be in the highest state of negative electrification, and would
P1352 repel each other. We have no reason to believe that they are in
P1353 such a highly electrified state, or could be maintained in that state. The earth and all the bodies whose attraction has been
P1354 observed are rather in an unelectrified state, that is, they contain the normal charge of electricity, and the only action between them
P1355 is the residual force lately mentioned. The artificial manner, how ever, in which this residual force is introduced is a much more
P1356 valid objection to the theory. In the present treatise I propose, at different stages of the in
P1357 vestigation, to test the different theories in the light of additional
P1358 classes of phenomena. For my own part, I look for additional
P1359 light on the nature of electricity from a study of what takes place in the space intervening between the electrified bodies. Such is the essential character of the mode of investigation pursued by Faraday in his Experimental Researches, and as we go on I intend to exhibit
P1360 the results, as developed by Faraday, W. Thomson, &c., in a con nected and mathematical form, so that we may perceive what
P1361 phenomena are explained equally well by all the theories, and what phenomena indicate the peculiar difficulties of each theory.
P1362 Measurement of the Force between Electrified Bodies.
P1363 38.] Forces may be measured in various ways. For instance, one of the bodies may be suspended from one arm of a delicate
P1364 balance, and weights suspended from the other arm, till the body,
P1365 when unelectrified, is in equilibrium. The other body may then be placed at a known distance beneath the first, so that the
P1366 attraction or repulsion of the bodies when electrified may increase
P1367 or diminish the apparent weight of the first. The weight which must be added to or taken from the other arm, when expressed in dynamical measure, will measure the force between the bodies.
P1368 This arrangement was used by Sir W. Snow Harris, and is that adopted in Sir W. Thomson s absolute electrometers. See Art. 217. 39-] MEASUREMENT OF ELECTRIC FORCES. 41
P1369 It is sometimes more convenient to use a torsion-balance in
P1370 which a horizontal arm is suspended by a fine wire or fibre, so as to be capable of vibrating about the vertical wire as an axis, and the body is attached to one end of the arm and acted on by the force in the tangential direction, so as to turn the arm round the vertical axis, and so twist the suspension wire through a certain angle. The torsional rigidity of the wire is found by observing the time of oscillation of the arm, the moment of inertia of the arm being otherwise known, and from the angle of torsion and
P1371 the torsional rigidity the force of attraction or repulsion can be deduced. The torsion-balance was devised by Michell for the de termination of the force of gravitation between small bodies, and was used by Cavendish for this purpose. Coulomb, working in dependently of these philosophers, reinvented it, and successfully applied it to discover the laws of electric and magnetic forces; and the torsion-balance has ever since been used in all researches
P1372 where small forces have to be measured. See Art. 215.

P1431 43.] The experiments of Coulomb with the torsion-balance may
P1432 be considered to have established the law of force with a certain
P1433 approximation to accuracy. Experiments of this kind, however, are rendered difficult, and in some degree uncertain, by several disturbing causes, which must be carefully traced and corrected for.
P1434 In the first place, the two electrified bodies must be of sensible dimensions relative to the distance between them, in order to be capable of carrying charges sufficient to produce measurable forces. The action of each body will then produce an effect on the dis tribution of electricity on the other, so that the charge cannot be considered as evenly distributed over the surface, or collected at the centre of gravity ; but its effect must be calculated by an intricate investigation. This, however, has been done as regards two spheres by Poisson in an extremely able manner, and the
P1435 investigation has been greatly simplified by Sir W. Thomson in
P1436 his Theory of Electrical Images. See Arts. 172-174. Another difficulty arises from the action of the electricity
P1437 induced on the sides of the case containing the instrument. By 4:4: ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [44.
P1438 making the inside of the instrument accurately cylindric, and
P1439 making- its inner surface of metal, this effect can be rendered definite and measurable.
P1440 An independent difficulty arises from the imperfect insulation
P1441 of the bodies, on account of which the charge continually de creases. Coulomb investigated the law of dissipation, and made
P1442 corrections for it in his experiments.
P1443 The methods of insulating charged conductors, and of measuring electrical effects, have been greatly improved since the time of
P1444 Coulomb, particularly by Sir W. Thomson; but the perfect ac
P1445 curacy of Coulomb s law of force is established, not by any direct
P1446 experiments and measurements (which may be used as illustrations
P1447 of the law), but by a mathematical consideration of the pheno menon described as Experiment VII, namely, that an electrified conductor B, if made to touch the inside of a hollow closed con
P1448 ductor C and then withdrawn without touching C, is perfectly dis charged, in whatever manner the outside of C may be electrified. By means of delicate electroscopes it is easy to shew that no
P1449 electricity remains on B after the operation, and by the mathe
P1450 matical theory given at Art. 74, this can only be the case if the
P1451 force varies inversely as the square of the distance, for if the law
P1452 had been of any different form B would have been electrified.

P1473 In a conductor the electrification is distributed exactly as if it were free to move in the conductor according to the same law. P1477 Equipotential Surfaces.
P1478 46.] If a surface described or supposed to be described in the electric field is such that the electric potential is the same at every point of the surface it is called an Equipotential surface.
P1479 An electrified point constrained to rest upon such a surface will

P1481 because the potential is the same at every point. An equipotential
P1482 surface is therefore a surface of equilibrium or a level surface. 43 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [46.
