P1514 electrification. Hence the electricity on any small area of the surface will be acted on by a force tending from the conductor and proportional to the product of the resultant force and the
P1515 density, that is, proportional to the square of the resultant force This force which acts outwards as a tension on every part of
P1516 the conductor will be called electric Tension. It is measured like
P1517 ordinary mechanical tension, by the force exerted on unit of area. The word Tension has been used by electricians in several vague
P1518 senses, and it has been attempted to adopt it in mathematical language as a synonym for Potential ; but on examining the cases in which the word has been used, I think it will be more con
P1519 sistent with usage and with mechanical analogy to understand
P1520 by tension a pulling force of so many pounds per square inch
P1521 exerted on the surface of a conductor or elsewhere. We shall find
P1522 that the conception of Faraday, that this electric tension exists not
P1523 only at the electrified surface but all along the lines of force, leads to a theory of electric action as a phenomenon of stress in a medium.

P1530 Capacity of a Conductor.
P1531 50.] If one conductor is insulated while all the surrounding con
P1532 ductors are kept at the zero potential by being put in commu nication with the earth, and if the conductor, when charged with
P1533 a quantity E of electricity, has a potential F, the ratio of E to V
P1534 is called the Capacity of the conductor. If the conductor is com
P1535 pletely enclosed within a conducting vessel without touching it, then the charge on the inner conductor will be equal and op posite to the charge on the inner surface of the outer conductor, and will be equal to the capacity of the inner conductor multiplied by the difference of the potentials of the two conductors.
P1536 Electric Accumulators.
P1537 A system consisting of two conductors whose opposed surfaces
P1538 are separated from each other by a thin stratum of an insulating medium is called an electric Accumulator. Its capacity is directly proportional to the area of the opposed surfaces and inversely pro
P1539 portional to the thickness of the stratum between them. A Leyden
P1540 jar is an accumulator in which glass is the insulating medium. Accumulators are sometimes called Condensers, but I prefer to restrict the term condenser to an instrument which is used not to
P1541 hold electricity but to increase its superficial density.
P1542 PROPERTIES OF BODIES IN RELATION TO STATICAL ELECTRICITY.
P1543 Resistance to the Passage of Electricity through a Body.
P1544 51.] When a charge of electricity is communicated to any part
P1545 of a mass of metal the electricity is rapidly transferred from places of high to places of low potential till the potential of the whole mass becomes the same. In the case of pieces of metal used in ordinary experiments this process is completed in a time too short to be observed, but in the case of very long and thin wires, such as those used in telegraphs, the potential does not become uniform till after a sensible time, on account of the resistance of the wire
P1546 to the passage of electricity through it. is exceedingly dif The resistance to the passage of electricity from the tables at
P1547 ferent in different substances, as may be seen 51.] ELECTRIC RESISTANCE. 49
P1548 Arts. 362, 366, and 369, which will be explained in treating of
P1549 All the metals are good conductors, though the resistance of lead is 12 times that of copper or silver, that of iron 6 times, and that of mercury 60 times that of copper. The resistance of all metals increases as their temperature rises.
P1550 Selenium in its crystalline state may also he regarded as a con
P1551 ductor, though its resistance is 3.7 x 10 12 times that of a piece of copper of the same dimensions. Its resistance increases as the temperature rises. Selenium in the amorphous form is a good
P1552 insulator, like sulphur.
P1553 Many liquids conduct electricity by electrolysis. This mode of conduction will be considered in Part II. For the present, we may
P1554 regard all liquids containing water and all damp bodies as con
P1555 ductors, far inferior to the metals, but incapable of insulating a
P1556 charge of electricity for a sufficient time to be observed.
P1557 On the other hand, the gases at the atmospheric pressure, whether
P1558 dry or moist, are insulators so nearly perfect when the electric tension
P1559 is small that we have as yet obtained no evidence of electricity passing
P1560 through them by ordinary conduction. The gradual loss of charge
P1561 by electrified bodies may in every case be traced to imperfect insu
P1562 lation in the supports, the electricity either passing through the substance of the support or creeping over its surface. Hence, when
P1563 two charged bodies are hung up near each other, they will preserve
P1564 their charges longer if they are electrified in opposite ways, than if
P1565 they are electrified in the same way. For though the electromotive force tending to make the electricity pass through the air between them is much greater when they are oppositely electrified, no per
P1566 ceptible loss occurs in this way. The actual loss takes place through
P1567 the supports, and the electromotive force through the supports is greatest when the bodies are electrified in the same way. The result
P1568 appears anomalous only when we expect the loss to occur by the
P1569 passage of electricity through the air between the bodies. Certain kinds of glass when cold are marvelously perfect in
P1570 sulators, and Sir W. Thomson has preserved charges of electricity
P1571 for years in bulbs hermetically sealed. The same glass, however,
P1572 becomes a conductor at a temperature below that of boiling water.
P1573 Gutta-percha, caoutchouc, vulcanite, paraffin, and resins are good
P1574 insulators, the resistance of gutta-percha at 75F. being about
P1575 6 x 10 19 times that of copper.
P1576 Ice, crystals, and solidified electrolytes, are also insulators.
P1577 VOL. I. E 50 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [52.
P1578 Certain liquids, such as naphtha, turpentine, and some oils, are insulators, but inferior to most of the solid insulators.
P1579 The resistance of most substances, except the metals, and selenium and carbon, seems to diminish as the temperature rises.
P1580 Specific Inductive Capacity.
P1581 52.] All bodies whose insulating power is such that when they are placed between two conductors at different potentials the elec tromotive force acting on them does not immediately distribute
P1582 their electricity so as to reduce the potential to a constant value, are
P1583 called by Faraday Dielectrics. Faraday discovered that the capacity of an accumulator depends
P1584 on the nature of the insulating medium between the two conductors,
P1585 as well as on the dimensions and relative position of the conductors
P1586 themselves. By substituting other insulating media for air as the
P1587 dielectric of the accumulator, without altering it in any other respect, he found that when air and other gases were employed as the insulating medium the capacity of the accumulator remained the same, but that when shell-lac, sulphur, glass, &c., were substituted for air, the capacity was increased in a ratio which was different for each substance.
P1588 The ratio of the capacity of an accumulator formed of any di electric medium to the capacity of an accumulator of the same form and dimensions filled with air, was named by Faraday the Specific Inductive Capacity of the dielectric medium. It is equal to unity for air and other gases at all pressures, and probably at all tempe ratures, and it is greater than unity for all other liquid or solid dielectrics which have been examined.
P1589 If the dielectric is not a good insulator, it is difficult to mea
P1590 sure its inductive capacity, because the accumulator will not hold a charge for a sufficient time to allow it to be measured ; but it is certain that inductive capacity is a property not confined to good insulators, and it is probable that it exists in all bodies.
P1591 Absorption of Electricity.
P1592 53.] It is found that when an accumulator is formed of certain dielectrics, the following phenomena occur.
P1593 When the accumulator has been for some time electrified and is
P1594 then suddenly discharged and again insulated, it becomes recharged 54-] ELECTRIC ABSORPTION. 51
P1595 in the same sense as at first, but to a smaller degree, so that it may
P1596 be discharged again several times in succession, these discharges
P1597 always diminishing. This phenomenon is called that of the Re
P1598 The instantaneous discharge appears always to be proportional to the difference of potentials at the instant of discharge, and the
P1599 ratio of these quantities is the true capacity of the accumulator; but if the contact of the discharger is prolonged so as to include some of the residual discharge, the apparent capacity of the accu mulator, calculated from such a discharge, will be too great.
P1600 The accumulator if charged and left insulated appears to lose its charge by conduction, but it is found that the proportionate rate of loss is much greater at first than it is afterwards, so that the measure of conductivity, if deduced from what takes place at first, would be too great. Thus, when the insulation of a submarine cable is tested, the insulation appears to improve as the electrifi
P1601 Thermal phenomena of a kind at first sight analogous take place in the case of the conduction of heat when the opposite sides of a body are kept at different temperatures. In the case of heat we know that they depend on the heat taken in and given out by the body itself. Hence, in the case of the electrical phenomena, it has been supposed that electricity is absorbed and emitted by the
P1602 parts of the body. We shall see, however, in Art. 329, that the
P1603 phenomena can be explained without the hypothesis of absorption of electricity, by supposing the dielectric in some degree heterogeneous.
P1604 That the phenomenon called Electric Absorption is not an
P1605 actual absorption of electricity by the substance may be shewn by
P1606 charging the substance in any manner with electricity while it is surrounded by a closed metallic insulated vessel. If, when the substance is charged and insulated, the vessel be instantaneously discharged and then left insulated, no charge is ever communicated to the vessel by the gradual dissipation of the electrification of the charged substance within it.
P1607 54.] This fact is expressed by the statement of Faraday that it is impossible to charge matter with an absolute and independent charge of one kind of electricity *.
P1608 In fact it appears from the result of every experiment which
P1609 has been tried that in whatever way electrical actions may take

P1612 place among a system of bodies surrounded by a metallic vessel, the
P1613 charge on the outside of that vessel is not altered.
P1614 Now if any portion of electricity could be forced into a body
P1615 so as to be absorbed in it, or to become latent, or in any way
P1616 to exist in it, without being connected with an -equal portion of the opposite electricity by lines of induction, or if, after having being absorbed, it could gradually emerge and return to its or dinary mode of action, we should find some change of electrifica tion in the surrounding vessel.
P1617 As this is never found to be the case, Faraday concluded that it is impossible to communicate an absolute charge to matter, and that no portion of matter can by any change of state evolve or
P1618 render latent one kind of electricity or the other. He therefore
P1619 regarded induction as the essential function both in the first development and the consequent phenomena of electricity/ His * induction is (1298) a polarized state of the particles of the dielectric, each particle being positive on one side and negative on the other, the positive and the negative electrification of each

P1621 Disruptive Discharge *.
P1622 55.] If the electromotive force acting at any point of a dielectric is gradually increased, a limit is at length reached at which there is a sudden electrical discharge through the dielectric, generally accompanied with light and sound, and with a temporary or per manent rupture of the dielectric.
P1623 The intensity of the electromotive force when this takes place
P1624 depends on the nature of the dielectric. It is greater, for instance, in dense air than in rare air, and greater in glass than in air, but
P1625 in every case, if the electromotive force be made great enough, the dielectric gives way and its insulating power is destroyed, so
P1626 that a current of electricity takes place through it. It is for this reason that distributions of electricity for which the electric resultant force becomes anywhere infinite cannot exist in nature.
P1627 Thus, when a conductor having a sharp point is electrified,
P1628 the theory, based on the hypothesis that it retains its charge,
P1629 leads to the conclusion that as we approach the point the super
P1630 ficial density of the electricity increases without limit, so that at the point itself the surface-density, and therefore the resultant
P1631 * See Faraday, Exp. Rts., vol. i., series xii. and xiii. 55-] ELECTRIC GLOW. 53
P1632 electrical force, would be infinite. If the air, or other surrounding dielectric, had an invincible insulating power, this result would actually occur ; but the fact is, that as soon as the resultant force in the neighbourhood of the point has reached a certain limit, the insulating power of the air gives way, so that the air close to
P1633 the point becomes a conductor. At a certain distance from the
P1634 point the resultant force is not sufficient to break through the insulation of the air, so that the electric current is checked, and the electricity accumulates in the air round the point.
P1635 The point is thus surrounded by particles of air charged with electricity of the same kind with its own. The effect of this charged air round the point is to relieve the air at the point itself from part of the enormous electromotive force which it would have ex perienced if the conductor alone had been electrified. In fact the surface of the electrified body is no longer pointed, because the point is enveloped by a rounded mass of electrified air, the surface of which, rather than that of the solid conductor, may be regarded
P1636 as the outer electrified surface.
P1637 If this portion of electrified air could be kept still, the elec trified body would retain its charge, if not on itself at least in its
P1638 neighbourhood, but the charged particles of air being free to move under the action of electrical force, tend to move away from the elec trified body because it is charged with the same kind of electricity. The charged particles of air therefore tend to move off in the direc tion of the lines of force and to approach those surrounding bodies
P1639 which are oppositely electrified. When they are gone, other un
P1640 charged particles take their place round the point, and since these cannot shield those next the point itself from the excessive elec tric tension, a new discharge takes place, after which the newly charged particles move off, and so on as long as the body remains
P1641 In this way the following phenomena are produced : At and
P1642 close to the point there is a steady glow, arising from the con stant discharges which are taking place between the point and the air very near it.
P1643 The charged particles of air tend to move off in the same general direction, and thus produce a current of air from the point, con sisting of the charged particles, and probably of others carried along by them. By artificially aiding this current we may increase the glow, and by checking the formation of the current we may pre
P1644 vent the continuance of the glow. 54 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [56.
P1645 The electric wind in the neighbourhood of the point is sometimes very rapid, but it soon loses its velocity, and the air with its charged particles is carried about with the general motions of the atmo
P1646 sphere, and constitutes an invisible electric cloud. When the charged
P1647 particles come near to any conducting surface, such as a wall, they induce on that surface an electrification opposite to their own, and are then attracted towards the wall, but since the electromotive
P1648 force is small they may remain for a long time near the wall without being drawn up to the surface and discharged. They
P1649 thus form an electrified atmosphere clinging to conductors, the pre
P1650 sence of which may sometimes be detected by the electrometer.
P1651 The electrical forces, however, acting between charged portions of air and other bodies are exceedingly feeble compared with the forces which produce winds arising from inequalities of density due to differences of temperature, so that it is very improbable that any observable part of the motion of ordinary thunder clouds arises from electrical causes.
P1652 The passage of electricity from one place to another by the motion of charged particles is called Electrical Convection or Con
P1653 The electrical glow is therefore produced by the constant passage of electricity through a small portion of air in which the tension is very high, so as to charge the surrounding particles of air which are continually swept off by the electric wind, which is an essential part of the phenomenon.
P1654 The glow is more easily formed in rare air than in dense air, and more easily when the point is positive than when it is negative. This and many other differences between positive and negative elec trification must be studied by those who desire to discover some thing about the nature of electricity. They have not, however, been satisfactorily brought to bear upon any existing theory.
P1655 56.] The electric brush is a phenomenon which may be pro duced by electrifying a blunt point or small ball so as to produce
P1656 an electric field in which the tension diminishes, but in a less, rapid
P1657 manner, as we leave the surface. It consists of a succession of
P1658 discharges, ramifying as they diverge from the ball into the air, and terminating either by charging portions of air or by reaching some other conductor. It is accompanied by a sound, the pitch of which depends on the interval between the successive discharges, and there is no current of air as in the case of the glow. 57-] ELECTRIC SPARK. 55
P1659 57.] When the tension in the space between two conductors is
P1660 considerable all the way between them, as in the case of two balls
P1661 whose distance is not great compared with their radii, the discharge, when it occurs, usually takes the form of a spark, by which nearly the whole electrification is discharged at once.
P1662 In this case, when any part of the dielectric has given way,
P1663 the parts on either side of it in the direction of the electric force are put into a state of greater tension so that they also give way, and so the discharge proceeds right through the dielectric, just as when a little rent is made in the edge of a piece of paper a tension applied to the paper in the direction of the edge causes the paper to be torn through, beginning at the rent, but diverging occasionally where there are weak places in the paper. The electric spark in
P1664 the same way begins at the point where the electric tension first
P1665 overcomes the insulation of the dielectric, and proceeds from that point, in an apparently irregular path, so as to take in other weak points, such as particles of dust floating in air.
P1666 0)i the Electric Force required to produce a Spark in Air.
P1667 In the experiments of Sir W. Thomson * the electromotive force
P1668 required to produce a spark across strata of air of various thick
P1669 nesses was measured by means of an electrometer. The sparks were made to pass between two surfaces, one of which
P1670 was plane, and the other only sufficiently convex to make the sparks occur always at the same place.
P1671 The difference of potential required to cause a spark to pass was found to increase with the distance, but in a less rapid ratio, so that the electric force at any point between the surfaces, which is the quotient of the difference of potential divided by the distance, can be raised to a greater value without a discharge when the stratum
P1672 When the stratum of air is very thin, say .00254 of a centimetre,
P1673 the resultant force required to produce a spark was found to be 527.7, in terms of centimetres and grammes. This corresponds to an electric tension of 1 1.29 grammes weight per square centimetre.
P1674 When the distance between the surfaces is about a millimetre
P1675 the electric force is about 130, and the electric tension .68 grammes weight per square centimetre. It is probable that the value for
P1676 * Proc. K. S., I860 ; or, Reprint, chap. xix. 56 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA* [58.
P1677 greater distances is not much less than this. The ordinary pressure of the atmosphere is about 1032 grammes per square centimetre.
P1678 It is difficult to explain why a thin stratum of air should require
P1679 a greater force to produce a disruptive discharge across it than a thicker stratum. Is it possible that the air very near to the sur face of dense bodies is condensed, so as to become a better insu lator ? or does the potential of an electrified conductor differ from
P1680 that of the air in contact with it by a quantity having a maximum
P1681 value just before discharge, so that the observed difference of potential of the conductors is in every case greater than the dif
P1682 ference of potentials on the two sides of the stratum of air by a constant quantity equivalent to the addition of about .005 of an inch to the thickness of the stratum ? See Art. 370.
P1683 All these phenomena differ considerably in different gases, and in the same gas at different densities. Some of the forms of electrical discharge through rare gases are exceedingly remarkable. In some cases there is a regular alternation of luminous and dark strata, so
P1684 that if the electricity, for example, is passing along a tube contain
P1685 ing a very small quantity of gas, a number of luminous disks will
P1686 be seen arranged transversely at nearly equal intervals along the
P1687 axis of the tube and separated by dark strata. If the strength of the current be increased a new disk will start into existence, and
P1688 it and the old disks will arrange themselves in closer order. In a tube described by Mr. Gassiot&quot;* the light of each of the disks is bluish on the negative and reddish on the positive side, and bright red in the central stratum.
P1689 These, and many other phenomena of electrical discharge, are exceedingly important, and when they are better understood they
P1690 will probably throw great light on the nature of electricity as well
P1691 as on the nature of gases and of the medium pervading space. At present, however, they must be considered as outside the domain of
P1692 the mathematical theory of electricity.
P1693 Electric Phenomena of Tourmaline.
P1694 58.] Certain crystals of tourmaline, and of other minerals, possess
P1695 what may be called Electric Polarity. Suppose a crystal of tour
P1696 maline to be at a uniform temperature, and apparently free from
P1697 electrification on its surface. Let its temperature be now raised, the crystal remaining insulated. One end will be found positively
P1698 * Intellectual Observer, March, 1866. 59-] ELECTRIFICATION OF TOURMALINE. 57
P1699 and the other end negatively electrified. Let the surface be de prived of this apparent electrification by means of a flame or other wise, then if the crystal be made still hotter, electrification of the same kind as before will appear, but if the crystal be cooled the end which was positive when the crystal was heated will become
P1700 negative. These electrifications are observed at the extremities of the crys
P1701 tallographic axis. Some crystals are terminated by a six-sided pyramid at one end and by a three- sided pyramid at the other. In these the end having the six-sided pyramid becomes positive when the crystal is heated.
P1702 Sir W. Thomson supposes every portion of these and other hemi
P1703 hedral crystals to have a definite electric polarity, the intensity
P1704 of which depends on the temperature. When the surface is passed
P1705 through a flame, every part of the surface becomes electrified to such an extent as to exactly neutralize, for all external points, the effect of the internal polarity. The crystal then has no ex ternal electrical action, nor any tendency to change its mode of electrification. But if it be heated or cooled the interior polariza tion of each particle of the crystal is altered, and can no longer be balanced by the superficial electrification, so that there is a
P1706 resultant external action.
P1707 Plan of this Treatise.
P1708 59.] In the following treatise I propose first to explain the ordinary theory of electrical action, which considers it as depending only on the electrified bodies and on their relative position, without
P1709 taking account of any phenomena which may take place in the surrounding media. In this way we shall establish the law of the
P1710 inverse square, the theory of the potential, and the equations of
P1711 Laplace and Poisson. We shall next consider the charges and
P1712 potentials of a system of electrified conductors as connected by
P1713 a system of equations, the coefficients of which may be supposed
P1714 to be determined by experiment in those cases in which our present mathematical methods are not applicable, and from these we shall determine the mechanical forces acting between the different elec
P1715 We shall then investigate certain general theorems by which
P1716 Green, Gauss, and Thomson have indicated the conditions of so lution of problems in the distribution of electricity. One result of these theorems is, that if Poisson s equation is satisfied by any 58 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [59.
P1717 function, and if at the surface of every conductor the function has the value of the potential of that conductor, then the func
P1718 tion expresses the actual potential of the system at every point. We
P1719 also deduce a method of finding problems capable of exact solution. In Thomson s theorem, the total energy of the system is ex
P1720 pressed in the form of the integral of a certain quantity extended over the whole space between the electrified bodies, and also in the form of an integral extended over the electrified surfaces only.
P1721 The equation between these two expressions may be thus inter
P1722 preted physically. We may conceive the relation into which the
P1723 electrified bodies are thrown, either as the result of the state of
P1724 the intervening medium, or as the result of a direct action between the electrified bodies at a distance. If we adopt the latter con
P1725 ception, we may determine the law of the action, but we can go
P1726 no further in speculating on its cause. If, on the other hand, we adopt the conception of action through a medium, we are led to enquire into the nature of that action in each part of the medium.
P1727 It appears from the theorem, that if we are to look for the seat of the electric energy in the different parts of the dielectric me dium, the amount of energy in any small part must depend on
P1728 the square of the intensity of the resultant electromotive force at
P1729 that place multiplied by a coefficient called the specific inductive capacity of the medium.
P1730 It is better, however, in considering the theory of dielectrics in the most general point of view, to distinguish between the elec tromotive force at any point and the electric polarization of the
P1731 medium at that point, since these directed quantities, though re lated to one another, are not, in some solid substances, in the same direction. The most general expression for the electric energy of the medium per unit of volume is half the product of the electro motive force and the electric polarization multiplied by the cosine
P1732 of the angle between their directions. In all fluid dielectrics the electromotive force and the electric
P1733 polarization are in the same direction and in a constant ratio. If we calculate on this hypothesis the total energy residing
P1734 in the medium, we shall find it equal to the energy due to the electrification of the conductors on the hypothesis of direct action at a distance. Hence the two hypotheses are mathematically equi
P1735 If we now proceed to investigate the mechanical state of the medium on the hypothesis that the mechanical action observed 6O.] STRESS IN DIELECTRICS. 59
P1736 between electrified bodies is exerted through and by means of the medium, as in the familiar instances of the action of one body on another by means of the tension of a rope or the pressure of a rod, we find that the medium must be in a state of mechanical
P1737 The nature of this stress is, as Faraday pointed out *, a tension along the lines of force combined with an equal pressure in all directions at right angles to these lines. The magnitude of these stresses is proportional to the energy of the electrification, or, in other words, to the square of the resultant electromotive force mul tiplied by the specific inductive capacity of the medium.
P1738 This distribution of stress is the only one consistent with the observed mechanical action on the electrified bodies, and also with
P1739 the observed equilibrium of the fluid dielectric which surrounds them. I have therefore thought it a warrantable step in scientific procedure to assume the actual existence of this state of stress, and to follow the assumption into its consequences. Finding the phrase electric tension used in several vague senses, I have attempted to confine it to what I conceive to have been in the mind of some
P1740 of those who have used it, namely, the state of stress in the dielectric medium which causes motion of the electrified bodies, and leads, when continually augmented, to disruptive discharge.
P1741 Electric tension, in this sense, is a tension of exactly the same kind, and measured in the same way, as the tension of a rope, and the dielectric medium, which can support a certain tension
P1742 and no more, may be said to have a certain strength in exactly
P1743 the same sense as the rope is said to have a certain strength. Thus, for example, Thomson has found that air at the ordinary pressure and temperature can support an electric tension of 9600 grains weight per square foot before a spark passes.
P1744 60.] From the hypothesis that electric action is not a direct action between bodies at a distance, but is exerted by means of the medium between the bodies, we have deduced that this medium
P1745 must be in a state of stress. We have also ascertained the cha
P1746 racter of the stress, and compared it with the stresses which may
P1747 occur in solid bodies. Along the lines of force there is tension,
P1748 and perpendicular to them there is pressure, the numerical mag
P1749 nitude of these forces being equal, and each proportional to the square of the resultant force at the point. Having established these results, we are prepared to take another step, and to form
P1750 * Exp. Bes., series xi. 1297. 60 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [60.
P1751 an idea of the nature of the electric polarization of the dielectric
P1752 An elementary portion of a body may be said to be polarized
P1753 when it acquires equal and opposite properties on two opposite sides. The idea of internal polarity may be studied to the greatest advantage as exemplified in permanent magnets, and it will be explained at greater length when we come to treat of magnetism.
P1754 The electric polarization of an elementary portion of a dielectric is a forced state into which the medium is thrown by the action of electromotive force, and which disappears when that force is
P1755 removed. We may conceive it to consist in what we may call
P1756 an electrical displacement, produced by the electromotive force.
P1757 When the electromotive force acts on a conducting medium it
P1758 produces a current through it, but if the medium is a non-con ductor or dielectric, the current cannot flow through the medium, but the electricity is displaced within the medium in the direction
P1759 of the electromotive force, the extent of this displacement de pending on the magnitude of the electromotive force, so that if the electromotive force increases or diminishes the electric displace
P1760 ment increases and diminishes in the same ratio.
P1761 The amount of the displacement is measured by the quantity of electricity which crosses unit of area, while the displacement increases from zero to its actual amount. This, therefore, is the
P1762 measure of the electric polarization. The analogy between the action of electromotive force in pro
P1763 ducing electric displacement and of ordinary mechanical force in producing the displacement of an elastic body is so obvious that I have ventured to call the ratio of the electromotive force to the
P1764 corresponding electric displacement the coefficient of electric elasticity of the medium. This coefficient is different in different media, and varies inversely as the specific inductive capacity of each medium.
P1765 The variations of electric displacement evidently constitute electric currents. These currents, however, can only exist during the variation of the displacement, and therefore, since the displace ment cannot exceed a certain value without causing disruptive discharge, they cannot be continued indefinitely in the same direc tion, like the currents through conductors.
P1766 In tourmaline, and other pyro-electric crystals, it is probable that a state of electric polarization exists, which depends upon tem perature, and does not require an external electromotive force to produce it If the interior of a body were in a state of permanent 60.] ELECTRIC DISPLACEMENT. 61
P1767 electric polarization, the outside would gradually become charged in such a manner as to neutralize the action of the internal elec
P1768 trification for all points outside the body. This external superficial
P1769 charge could not be detected by any of the ordinary tests, and could not be removed by any of the ordinary methods for dis charging superficial electrification. The internal polarization of
P1770 the substance would therefore never be discovered unless by some means, such as change of temperature, the amount of the internal
P1771 polarization could be increased or diminished. The external elec
P1772 trification would then be no longer capable of neutralizing the
P1773 external effect of the internal polarization, and an apparent elec
P1774 trification would be observed, as in the case of tourmaline.

P1828 61.] We are thus led to a very remarkable consequence of the
P1829 theory which we are examining, namely, that the motions of elec
P1830 tricity are like those of an incompressible fluid, so that the total
P1831 quantity within an imaginary fixed closed surface remains always the same. This result appears at first sight in direct contradiction to the fact that we can charge a conductor and then introduce 62.] THEORY PROPOSED. 63
P1832 it into the closed space, and so alter the quantity of electricity within that space. But we must remember that the ordinary theory takes no account of the electric displacement in the substance of dielectrics which we have been investigating, but confines its attention to the electrification at the bounding surfaces of the conductors and dielectrics. In the case of the charOged conductor let us suppose the charge to be positive, then if the surrounding dielectric extends on all sides beyond the closed surface there will be electric polarization, accompanied with displacement from within outwards all over the closed surface, and the surface-integral of the displacement taken over the surface will be equal to the charge on the conductor within.
P1833 Thus when the charged conductor is introduced into the closed
P1834 space there is immediately a displacement of a quantity of elec tricity equal to the charge through the surface from within out wards, and the whole quantity within the surface remains the same.

P1846 That electromotive force acting on a diele^iicr produces what
P1847 we have called electric displacement, the relation between the force
P1848 and the displacement being in the most general case of a kind
P1849 to be afterwards investigated in treating of conduction, but in
P1850 the most important cases the force is in the same direction as
P1851 the displacement, and is numerically equal to the displacement
P1852 multiplied by a quantity which we have called the coefficient of
P1853 electric elasticity of the dielectric.
P1854 That the energy per unit of volume of the dielectric arising from
P1855 the electric polarization is half the product of the electromotive 64 ELECTROSTATIC PHENOMENA. [62.
P1856 force and the electric displacement multiplied, if necessary, by the cosine of the angle between their directions.
P1857 That in fluid dielectrics the electric polarization is accompanied by a tension in the direction of the lines of force combined with an equal pressure in all directions at right angles to the lines of force, the amount of the tension or pressure per unit of area being numerically equal to the energy per unit of volume at the same place.
P1858 That the surfaces of any elementary portion into which we may
P1859 conceive the volume of the dielectric divided must be conceived
P1860 to be electrified, so that the surface- density at any point of the surface is equal in magnitude to the displacement through that point of the surface reckoned inwards, so that if the displacement is in the positive direction, the surface of the element will be elec trified negatively on the positive side and positively on the negative side. These superficial electrifications will in general destroy one another when consecutive elements are considered, except where the dielectric has an internal charge, or at the surface of the
P1861 That whatever electricity may be, and whatever we may under stand by the movement of electricity, the phenomenon which we have called electric displacement is a movement of electricity in the
P1862 same sense as the transference of a definite quantity of electricity through a wire is a movement of electricity, the only difference being that in the dielectric there is a force which we have called electric elasticity which acts against the electric displacement, and forces the electricity back when the electromotive force is removed ; whereas in the conducting wire the electric elasticity is continually giving way, so that a current of true conduction is set up, and the resistance depends, not on the total quantity of electricity dis placed from its position of equilibrium, but on the quantity which crosses a section of the conductor in a given time.
P1863 That in every case the motion of electricity is subject to the same condition as that of an incompressible fluid, namely, that
P1864 at every instant as much must flow out of any given closed space
P1865 It follows from this that every electric current must form a closed circuit. The importance of this result will be seen when we
P1866 investigate the laws of electro-magnetism.
P1867 Since, as we have seen, the theory of direct action at a distance is mathematically identical with that of action by means of a 62.] METHOD OF THIS WORK. 65
P1868 medium, the actual phenomena may be explained by the one
P1869 theory as well as by the other, provided suitable hypotheses be introduced when any difficulty occurs. Thus, Mossotti has deduced the mathematical theory of dielectrics from the ordinary theoiy of attraction by merely giving an electric instead of a magnetic interpretation to the symbols in the investigation by which Poisson has deduced the theory of magnetic induction from the theory of
P1870 magnetic fluids. He assumes the existence within the dielectric of
P1871 small conducting elements, capable of having their opposite surfaces oppositely electrified by induction,, but not capable of losing or gaining electricity on the whole, owing to their being insulated from each other by a non-conducting medium. This theory of
P1872 dielectrics is consistent with the laws of electricity, and may be
P1873 actually true. If it is true, the specific inductive capacity of a
P1874 dielectric may be greater, but cannot be less, than that of air or vacuum. No instance has yet been found of a dielectric having
P1875 an inductive capacity less than that of air, but if such should be discovered, Mossotti s theory must be abandoned, although his formulae would all remain exact, and would only require us to alter the sign of a coefficient.
P1876 In the theory which I propose to develope, the mathematical methods are founded upon the smallest possible amount of hypo thesis, and thus equations of the same form are found applicable to phenomena which are certainly of quite different natures, as, for
P1877 instance, electric induction through dielectrics ; conduction through conductors, and magnetic induction. In all these cases the re lation between the force and the effect produced is expressed by
P1878 a set of equations of the same kind, so that when a problem in one of these subjects is solved, the problem and its solution may be translated into the language of the other subjects and the results in their new form will also be true.
P1879 ELEMENTARY MATHEMATICAL THEORY OF STATICAL
P1880 Definition of Electricity as a Mathematical Quantity.
P1881 63.] We have seen that the actions of electrified bodies are such
P1882 that the electrification of one body may be equal to that of another, or to the sum of the electrifications of two bodies, and that when
P1883 two bodies are equally and oppositely electrified they have no elec trical effect on external bodies when placed together within a closed
P1884 insulated conducting vessel. We may express all these results in
P1885 a concise and consistent manner by describing an electrified body as
P1886 charged with a certain quantity of electricity, which we may denote by e. When the electrification is positive, that is, according to the usual convention, vitreous, e will be a positive quantity. When the
P1887 electrification is negative or resinous, e will be negative, and the
P1888 quantity e may be interpreted either as a negative quantity of
P1889 vitreous electricity or as a positive quantity of resinous electricity.
P1890 The effect of adding together two equal and opposite charges of
P1891 electricity, +e and e, is to produce a state of no electrification
P1892 expressed by zero. We may therefore regard a body not electrified
P1893 as virtually charged with equal and opposite charges of indefinite magnitude, and an electrified body as virtually charged with un equal quantities of positive and negative electricity, the algebraic
P1894 sum of these charges constituting the observed electrification. It is manifest, however, that this way of regarding an electrified body is entirely artificial, and may be compared to the conception of the velocity of a body as compounded of two or more different velo
P1895 cities, no one of which is the actual velocity of the body. When
P1896 we speak therefore of a body being charged with a quantity e of electricity we mean simply that the body is electrified, and that
P1897 the electrification is vitreous or resinous according as e is positive or negative. 64.] ELECTRIC DENSITY. 67

P2096 the probable error of an experiment with the torsion-balance is
P2097 considerable. As an argument that the attraction is really, and

P2101 In that experiment a conductor B, charged in any manner, was enclosed in a hollow conducting vessel C, which completely sur
P2102 rounded it. C was also electrified in any manner.
P2103 B was then placed in electric communication with C, and was then
P2104 again insulated and removed from C without touching it, and ex
P2105 amined by means of an electroscope. In this way it was shewn that a conductor, if made to touch the inside of a conducting vessel
P2106 which completely encloses it, becomes completely discharged, so that no trace of electrification can be discovered by the most delicate electrometer, however strongly the conductor or the vessel has been previously electrified.
P2107 The methods of detecting the electrification of a body are so
P2108 delicate that a millionth part of the original electrification of B
P2109 could be observed if it existed. No experiments involving the direct
P2110 measurement of forces can be brought to such a degree of accuracy. It follows from this experiment that a non-electrified body in the
P2111 inside of a hollow conductor is at the same potential as the hollow conductor, in whatever way that conductor is charged. For if it were not at the same potential, then, if it were put in electric connexion with the vessel, either by touching it or by means of a wire, electricity would pass from the one body to the other, and the conductor, when removed from the vessel, would be found to be electrified positively or negatively, which, as we have already stated, is not the case.
P2112 Hence the whole space inside a hollow conductor is at the same potential as the conductor if no electrified body is placed within it. If the law of the inverse square is true, this will be the case what ever be the form of the hollow conductor. Our object at present, however, is to ascertain from this fact the form of the law of

P2311 sense that Faraday uses his conception of lines of force to indicate not only the direction but the amount of the force at any place in the field.
P2312 We have used the phrase Lines of Force because it has been used
P2313 by Faraday and others. In strictness, however, these lines should be called Lines of Electric Induction.
P2314 In the ordinary cases the lines of induction indicate the direction and magnitude of the resultant electromotive force at every point, because the force and the induction are in the same direction and
P2315 in a constant ratio. There are other cases, however, in which it is important to remember that these lines indicate the induction, and that the force is indicated by the equipotential surfaces, being normal to these surfaces and inversely proportional to the distances
P2316 of consecutive surfaces.

P2464 For consider the lines of force issuing from the charged con
P2465 ductor Ar . The charge is measured by the excess of the number
P2466 of lines which issue from it over those which terminate in it.
P2467 Hence, if the conductor has no charge, the number of lines which enter the conductor must be equal to the number which issue from it. The lines which enter the conductor come from places of greater potential, and those which issue from it go to places of less poten tial. Hence the potential of an uncharged conductor must be intermediate between the highest and lowest potentials in the field, and therefore the highest and lowest potentials cannot belong to
P2468 any of the uncharged bodies.

P2570 95.] IN the preceding chapter we have calculated the potential function and investigated its properties on the hypothesis that
P2571 there is a direct action at a distance between electrified bodies, which is the resultant of the direct actions between the various electrified parts of the bodies.
P2572 If we call this the direct method of investigation, the inverse method will consist in assuming that the potential is a function characterised by properties the same as those which we have already
P2573 established, and investigating the form of the function. In the direct method the potential is calculated from the dis
P2574 tribution of electricity by a process of integration, and is found to satisfy certain partial differential equations. In the inverse method the partial differential equations are supposed given, and we have to find the potential and the distribution of electricity.
P2575 It is only in problems in which the distribution of electricity
P2576 is given that the direct method can be used. When we have to
P2577 find the distribution on a conductor we must make use of the
P2578 &quot;We have now to shew that the inverse method leads in every

P2580 The mathematical ideas expressed by this equation are of a
P2581 different kind from those expressed by the equation

P2583 In the differential equation we express that the values of the
P2584 second derivatives of V in the neighbourhood of any point, and 96.] CHARACTERISTICS OF THE POTENTIAL. 99
P2585 the density at that point are related to each other in a certain
P2586 manner, and no relation is expressed between the value of V at
P2587 that point and the value of p at any point at a sensible distance from it.
P2588 In the second expression, on the other hand, the distance between
P2589 the point (x 3 y , z ) at which p exists from the point (#, y, z] at
P2590 which V exists is denoted by r, and is distinctly recognised in the
P2591 expression to be integrated. The integral, therefore, is the appropriate mathematical expression
P2592 for a theory of action between particles at a distance, whereas the
P2593 differential equation is the appropriate expression for a theory of
P2594 action exerted between contiguous parts of a medium.
P2595 We have seen that the result of the integration satisfies the differential equation. We have now to shew that it is the only
P2596 solution of that equation fulfilling certain conditions.
P2597 We shall in this way not only establish the mathematical equi
P2598 valence of the two expressions, but prepare our minds to pass from
P2599 the theory of direct action at a distance to that of action between contiguous parts of a medium.

P2800 electricity in an electrified system, we must make K = 1 throughout the space occupied by air, and K=& throughout the space occupied
P2801 by conductors. If any part of the space is occupied by dielectrics
P2802 whose inductive capacity differs from that of air, we must make K
P2803 in that part of the space equal to the specific inductive capacity.
P2804 The value of F, determined so as to fulfil these conditions, will
P2805 be the only possible value of the potential in the given system.
P2806 Green s Theorem shews that the quantity Q, when it has its minimum value corresponding to a given distribution of electricity,
P2807 represents the potential energy of that distribution of electricity.
P2808 See Art. 100, equation (11).
P2809 In the form in which Q is expressed as the result of integration
P2810 over every part of the field, it indicates that the energy due to the
P2811 electrification of the bodies in the field may be considered as the
P2812 result of the summation of a certain quantity which exists in every part of the field where electrical force is in action, whether elec trification be present or not in that part of the field.
P2813 The mathematical method, therefore, in which Q, the symbol of electrical energy, is made an object of study, instead of p, the symbol of electricity itself, corresponds to the method of physical speculation, in which we look for the seat of electrical action in 108 GENERAL THEOREMS. [lOO.
P2814 every part of the field, instead of confining our attention to the electrified bodies.
P2815 The fact that Q attains a minimum value when the components
P2816 of the electric force are expressed in terms of the first derivatives of a potential, shews that, if it were possible for the electric force to be distributed in any other manner, a mechanical force would be brought into play tending to bring the distribution of force
P2817 into its actual state. The actual state of the electric field is
P2818 therefore a state of stable equilibrium, considered with reference to all variations of that state consistent with the actual distribution of free electricity.

P2918 Green s Theorem, on the other hand, belongs essentially to what we have called the inverse method. P2922 integral which we must endeavour to interpret.

P3167 105.] According to the theory of action at a distance the action
P3168 between E^ and E2 is really made up of a system of forces acting in straight lines between the electricity in El and that in H29 and the
P3169 actual mechanical effect is in complete accordance with this theory.
P3170 There is, however, another point of view from which we may
P3171 examine the action between El and E2 . When we see one body
P3172 acting on another at a distance, before we assume that the one acts directly on the other we generally inquire whether there is any material connexion between the two bodies, and if we find
P3173 strings, or rods, or framework of any kind, capable of accounting for the observed action between the bodies, we prefer to explain
P3174 the action by means of the intermediate connexions, rather than admit the notion of direct action at a distance.
P3175 Thus when two particles are connected by a straight or curved
P3176 rod, the action between the particles is always along the line joining
P3177 them, but we account for this action by means of a system of 106.] INTERNAL FORCES. 123
P3178 internal forces in the substance of the rod. The existence of these
P3179 internal forces is deduced entirely from observation of the effect of external forces on the rod, and the internal forces themselves are generally assumed to be the resultants of forces which act between particles of the rod. Thus the observed action between two distant particles is, in this instance, removed from the class of direct actions at a distance by referring it to the intervention of the rod ; the action of the rod is explained by the existence of internal forces in its substance ; and the internal forces are
P3180 explained by means of forces assumed to act between the particles of which the rod is composed, that is, between bodies at distances which though small must be finite.
P3181 The observed action at a considerable distance is therefore ex
P3182 plained by means of a great number of forces acting between bodies at very small distances, for which we are as little able to account as for the action at any distance however great.
P3183 Nevertheless, the consideration of the phenomenon, as explained in this way, leads us to investigate the properties of the rod, and to form a theory of elasticity \\hich we should have overlooked if we had been satisfied with the explanation by action at a distance,
P3184 106.] Let us now examine the consequence of assuming that the action between electrified bodies can be explained by the inter mediate action of the medium between them, and let us ascertain
P3185 what properties of the medium will account for the observed action.
P3186 We have first to determine the internal forces in the medium,
P3187 and afterwards to account for them if possible. In order to determine the internal forces in any case we proceed

P3391 appear at variance with the established principle that at any point in a fluid the pressures in all directions are equal. But in the deduction of this principle from a consideration of the mobility and equilibrium of the parts of the fluid it is taken for granted
P3392 that no action such as that which we here suppose to take place along- the lines of force exists in the fluid. The state of stress which we have been studying is perfectly consistent with the mobility and equilibrium of the fluid, for we have seen that, if
P3393 any portion of the fluid is devoid of electric charge, it experi ences no resultant force from the stresses on its surface, however
P3394 intense these may be. It is only when a portion of the fluid
P3395 becomes charged, that its equilibrium is disturbed by the stresses on its surface, and we know that in this case it actually tends to move. Hence the supposed state of stress is not inconsistent with
P3396 the equilibrium of a fluid dielectric.

P3408 I. Electric Displacement. When induction takes place in a to a dielectric a phenomenon takes place which is equivalent For displacement of electricity in the direction of the induction. III.] ELECTRIC POLARIZATION. 133
P3409 instance, in a Leyden jar, of which the inner coating is charged positively and the outer coating negatively, the displacement in the substance of the glass is from within outwards.
P3410 Any increase of this displacement is equivalent, during the time
P3411 of increase, to a current of positive electricity from within outwards, and any diminution of the displacement is equivalent to a current in the opposite direction.
P3412 The whole quantity of electricity displaced through any area of a surface fixed in the dielectric is measured by the quantity which we have already investigated (Art. 75) as the surface-integral of
P3413 induction through that area, multiplied by K, where K is the
P3414 specific inductive capacity of the dielectric. II. Superficial Electrification of the Particles of the Dielectric.
P3415 Conceive any portion of the dielectric, large or small, to be separated (in imagination) from the rest by a closed surface, then we must suppose that on every elementary portion of this surface there is an electrification measured by the total displacement of electricity through that element of surface reckoned Inwards.
P3416 In the case of the Leyden jar of which the inner coating is charged positively, any portion of the glass will have its inner side charged positively and its outer side negatively. If this portion be entirely in the interior of the glass, its superficial elec trification will be neutralized by the opposite electrification of the parts in contact with it, but if it be in contact with a conducting body which is incapable of maintaining in itself the inductive state, the superficial electrification will not be neutralized, but will con stitute that apparent electrification which is commonly called the
P3417 Electrification of the Conductor.
P3418 The electrification therefore at the bounding surface of a con ductor and the surrounding dielectric, which on the old theory was called the electrification of the conductor, must be called in the theory of induction the superficial electrification of the surrounding
P3419 According to this theory, all electrification is the residual effect of the polarization of the dielectric. This polarization exists throughout the interior of the substance, but it is there neutralized by the juxtaposition of oppositely electrified parts, so that it is only at the surface of the dielectric that the effects of the electrification
P3420 become apparent. The theory completely accounts for the theorem of Art. 7 7, that 134 ELECTRIC ATTRACTION. [ill.
P3421 the total induction through a closed surface is equal to the total quantity of electricity within the surface multiplied by 4-n&quot;. For
P3422 what we have called the induction through the surface is simply the electric displacement multiplied by 47r, and the total displace ment outwards is necessarily equal to the total electrification within
P3423 The theory also accounts for the impossibility of communicating an absolute charge to matter. For every particle of the dielectric is electrified with equal and opposite charges on its opposite sides, if it would not be more correct to say that these electrifications are only the manifestations of a single phenomenon, which we may call
P3424 Electric Polarization.
P3425 A dielectric medium, when thus polarized, is the seat of electrical
P3426 energy, and the energy in unit of volume of the medium is nu
P3427 merically equal to the electric tension on unit of area, both quan tities being equal to half the product of the displacement and the
P3428 resultant electromotive force, or
P3429 K where p is the electric tension, & the displacement, &amp;lt; the electro
P3430 motive force, and the specific inductive capacity.
P3431 If the medium is not a perfect insulator, the state of constraint,
P3432 which we call electric polarization, is continually giving way. The
P3433 medium yields to the electromotive force, the electric stress is
P3434 relaxed, and the potential energy of the state of constraint is con
P3435 verted into heat. The rate at which this decay of the state of
P3436 polarization takes place depends on the nature of the medium.
P3437 In some kinds of glass, days or years may elapse before the polar
P3438 ization sinks to half its original value. In copper, this change
P3439 may occupy less than the billionth of a second.
P3440 We have supposed the medium after being polarized to be simply
P3441 left to itself. In the phenomenon called the electric current the
P3442 constant passage of electricity through the medium tends to restore
P3443 the state of polarization as fast as the conductivity of the medium
P3444 allows it to decay. Thus the external agency which maintains the
P3445 current is always doing work in restoring the polarization of the
P3446 medium, which is continually becoming relaxed, and the potential
P3447 energy of this polarization is continually becoming transformed
P3448 into heat, so that the final result of the energy expended in main
P3449 taining the current is to raise the temperature of the conductor. CHAPTER VI.

P3456 At such a point, therefore, the value of V is a maximum, or

P3461 surface electrified positively or negatively. If, therefore, a point
P3462 of equilibrium occurs in an unelectrified part of the field it must
P3463 be a stationary point, and not a maximum or a minimum.
P3464 In fact, the first condition of a maximum or minimum is that
P3465 must be all negative or all positive, if they have finite values. Now, by Laplace s equation, at a point where there is no elec
P3466 trification, the sum of these three quantities is zero, and therefore
P3467 this condition cannot be fulfilled.

P3493 clude only the electrified point or conductor of lowest potential,

P3521 fills all space, it loses every degree of cyclosis it has acquired, and

P3631 117.] WE have seen that the determination of the distribution
P3632 of electricity on the surface of conductors may be made to depend
P3633 on the solution of Laplace s equation
P3634 + ^+ _= ~d^ df dz* a function of x, y^ and V being which vanishes at an z, which is always finite and con infinite distance, and which has tinuous,
P3635 a given constant value at the surface of each conductor.
P3636 It is not in general possible by known mathematical methods
P3637 to solve this equation so as to fulfil arbitrarily given conditions,
P3638 but it is always possible to assign various forms to the function
P3639 V which shall satisfy the equation, and to determine in each case the forms of the conducting surfaces, so that the function V shall
P3640 be the true solution.
P3641 It appears, therefore, that what we should naturally call the inverse problem of determining the forms of the conductors from the potential is more manageable than the direct problem of de termining the potential when the form of the conductors is given.
P3642 In fact, every electrical problem of which we know the solution has been constructed by an inverse process. It is therefore of great importance to the electrician that he should know what results have been obtained in this way, since the only method by which he can expect to solve a new problem is by reducing it
P3643 to one of the cases in which a similar problem has been con
P3644 structed by the inverse process. This historical knowledge of results can be turned to account in
P3645 two ways. If we are required to devise an instrument for making electrical measurements with the greatest accuracy, we may select
P3646 those forms for the electrified surfaces which correspond to cases
P3647 of which we know the accurate solution. If, on the other hand, Jl8.] USE OF DIAGRAMS. 143
P3648 we are required to estimate what will be the electrification of bodies whose forms are given, we may begin with some case in which one of the equipotential surfaces takes a form somewhat resembling the given form,, and then by a tentative method we may modify the pro blem till it more nearly corresponds to the given case. This method
P3649 is evidently very imperfect considered from a mathematical point
P3650 of view, but it is the only one we have, and if we are not allowed to choose our conditions, we can make only an approximate cal culation of the electrification. It appears, therefore, that what we want is a knowledge of the forms of equipotential surfaces and lines of induction in as many different cases as we can collect
P3651 together and remember. In certain classes of cases, such as those
P3652 relating to spheres, we may proceed by mathematical methods. In other cases we cannot afford to despise the humbler method of
P3653 actually drawing tentative figures on paper, and selecting that which appears least unlike the figure we require.
P3654 This latter method I think may be of some use, even in cases in
P3655 which the exact solution has been obtained, for I find that an eye
P3656 knowledge of the forms of the equipotential surfaces often leads to a right selection of a mathematical method of solution.
P3657 I have therefore drawn several diagrams of systems of equipotential
P3658 surfaces and lines of force, so that the student may make himself
P3659 familiar with the forms of the lines. The methods by which such
P3660 diagrams may be drawn will be explained as we go on, as they
P3661 belong to questions of different kinds.

P3728 122.] I am anxious that these diagrams should be studied as
P3729 illustrations of the language of Faraday in speaking of lines of force/ the forces of an electrified body, &c.
P3730 In strict mathematical language the word Force is used to signify the supposed cause of the tendency which a material body is found to have towards alteration in its state of rest or motion. It is
P3731 indifferent whether we speak of this observed tendency or of its
P3732 immediate cause, since the cause is simply inferred from the effect, and has no other evidence to support it.
P3733 Since, however, we are ourselves in the practice of directing the motion of our own bodies, and of moving other things in this way, we have acquired a copious store of remembered sensations relating to these actions, and therefore our ideas of force are connected in
P3734 our minds with ideas of conscious power, of exertion, and of fatigue, and of overcoming or yielding to pressure. These ideas, which give a colouring and vividness to the purely abstract idea of force, do not in mathematically trained minds lead to any practical error.
P3735 But in the vulgar language of the time when dynamical science was unknown, all the words relating to exertion, such as force, 123.] AND ^INES OF INDUCTION. 147
P3736 energy, power, &c., were confounded with each other, though some of the schoolmen endeavoured to introduce a greater precision into
P3737 The cultivation and popularization of correct dynamical ideas since the time of Galileo and Newton has effected an immense
P3738 change in the language and ideas of common life, but it is only within recent times, and in consequence of the increasing im
P3739 portance of machinery, that the ideas of force, energy, and power have become accurately distinguished from each other. Very few, however, even of scientific men, are careful to observe these dis tinctions ; hence we often hear of the force of a cannon-ball when
P3740 either its energy or its momentum is meant, and of the force of an electrified body when the quantity of its electrification is meant.
P3741 Now the quantity of electricity in a body is measured, according
P3742 to Faraday s ideas, by the number of lines of force, or rather of induction, which proceed from it. These lines of force must all terminate somewhere, either on bodies in the neighbourhood, or on the walls and roof of the room, or on the earth, or on the heavenly
P3743 bodies, and wherever they terminate there is a quantity of elec tricity exactly equal and opposite to that on the part of the body
P3744 from which they proceeded. By examining the diagrams this will
P3745 be seen to be the case. There is therefore no contradiction between
P3746 Faraday s views and the mathematical results of the old theor}^, but, on the contrary, the idea of lines of force throws great light on these results, and seems to afford the means of rising by a con tinuous process from the somewhat rigid conceptions of the old
P3747 theory to notions which may be capable of greater expansion, so as to provide room for the increase of our knowledge by further

P4317 142.] The spherical harmonics which I have described are those of integral degrees. To enter on the consideration of harmonics
P4318 of fractional, irrational, or impossible degrees is beyond my purpose,
P4319 which is to give as clear an idea as I can of what these harmonics are. I have done so by referring the harmonic, not to a system of polar coordinates of latitude and longitude, or to Cartesian coordinates, but to a number of points on the sphere, which I have called the Poles of the harmonic. Whatever be the type of a harmonic of the degree i, it is always mathematically possible to find i points on the sphere which are its poles. The actual calculation of the position of these poles would in general involve the solution of a system of 2i equations of the degree i. The conception of the general harmonic, with its poles placed in any manner on the sphere^ is useful rather in fixing our ideas than in making calculations. For the latter purpose it is more convenient to consider the harmonic as the sum of 2i-\- 1 conjugate harmonics of selected types, and the ordinary symmetrical system, in which polar coordinates are used, is the most convenient. In this system

P4657 THEORY OF ELECTRIC IMAGES AND ELECTRIC INVERSION.
P4658 155.] We have already shewn that when a conducting sphere
P4659 is under the influence of a known distribution of electricity, the
P4660 distribution of electricity on the surface of the sphere can be determined by the method of spherical harmonics.
P4661 For this purpose we require to expand the potential of the in
P4662 fluencing system in a series of solid harmonics of positive degree,
P4663 having the centre of the sphere as origin, and we then find a corresponding series of solid harmonics of negative degree, which
P4664 express the potential due to the electrification of the sphere.
P4665 By the use of this very powerful method of analysis, Poisson
P4666 determined the electrification of a sphere under the influence of a given electrical system, and he also solved the more difficult problem to determine the distribution of electricity on two con ducting spheres in presence of each other. These investigations
P4667 have been pursued at great length by Plana and others, who have
P4668 confirmed the accuracy of Poisson.
P4669 In applying this method to the most elementary case of a sphere under the influence of a single electrified point, we require to expand the potential due to the electrified point in a series of solid har monics, and to determine a second series of solid harmonics which express the potential, due to the electrification of the sphere, in the
P4670 It does not appear that any of these mathematicians observed that this second series expresses the potential due to an imaginary electrified point, which has no physical existence as an electrified
P4671 point, but which may be called an electrical image, because the
P4672 action of the surface on external points is the same as that which would be produced by the imaginary electrified point if the spherical surface were removed. 192 ELECTKIC IMAGES. [156.
P4673 This discovery seems to have been reserved for Sir W. Thomson,
P4674 who has developed it into a method of great power for the solution of electrical problems, and at the same time capable of being pre
P4675 sented in an elementary geometrical form. His original investigations, which are contained in the Cambridge
P4676 and Dublin Mathematical Journal, 1848, are expressed in terms of the ordinary theory of attraction at a distance, and make no use of the method of potentials and of the general theorems of Chapter IV, though they were probably discovered by these methods. Instead, however, of following the method of the author, I shall make free use of the idea of the potential and of equipotential surfaces, when ever the investigation can be rendered more intelligible by such means.
P4677 Theory of Electric Images.

P4714 157.] Definition of an Electrical Image. An electrical a surface
P4715 an electrified point or system of points on one side of the same which would produce on the other side of that surface
P4716 electrical action which the actual electrification of that surface
P4717 really does produce.
P4718 In Optics a point or s}Tstem of points on one side of a mirror or lens which if it existed would emit the system of rays which
P4719 actually exists on the other side of the mirror or lens, is called a
P4720 Electrical images correspond to virtual images in optics in being
P4721 related to the space on the other side of the surface. They do not
P4722 correspond to them in actual position, or in the merely approximate
P4723 character of optical foci.
P4724 There are no real electrical images, that is, imaginary electrified points which would produce, in the region on the same side of the
P4725 electrified surface, an effect equivalent to that of the electrified surface.
P4726 For if the potential in any region of space is equal to that due to a certain electrification in the same region it must be actually
P4727 produced by that electrification. In fact, the electrification at any
P4728 point may be found from the potential near that point by the
P4729 application of Poisson s equation.

P5350 Application of Electrical Inversion to the case of a Spherical Bowl.
P5351 176.] One of the most remarkable illustrations of the power of
P5352 Sir W. Thomson s method of Electrical Images is furnished by his
P5353 investigation of the distribution of electricity on a portion of a spherical surface bounded by a small circle. The results of this
P5354 investigation, without proof, were communicated to M. Liouville and published in his Journal in 1847. The complete investigation is given in the reprint of Thomson s Electrical Papers, Article XV. I am not aware that a solution of the problem of the distribution
P5355 of electricity on a finite portion of any curved surface has been given by any other mathematician.
P5356 As I wish to explain the method rather than to verify the calculation, I shall not enter at length into either the geometry
P5357 or the integration, but refer my readers to Thomson s work.
P5358 Distribution of Electricity on an Ellipsoid. 177.] It is shewn by a well-known method* that the attraction of a shell bounded by two similar and similarly situated and concentric ellipsoids is such that there is no resultant attraction on any point within the shell. If we suppose the thickness of the shell to diminish indefinitely while its density increases, we ultimately arrive at the conception of a surface-density varying as the perpendicular from the centre on the tangent plane, and since the resultant attraction of this superficial distribution on any point within the ellipsoid is zero, electricity, if so distributed on the surface,, will be in equilibrium. Hence, the surface-density at any point of an ellipsoid undis turbed by external influence varies as the distance of the tangent plane from the centre.
P5359 * Thomson and Tait s Natural Philosophy, 520, or Art. 150 of this book. 222 ELECTRIC IMAGES.

P5451 182.] THE number of independent cases in which the problem of electrical equilibrium has been solved is very small. The method
P5452 of spherical harmonics has been employed for spherical conductors, and the methods of electrical images and of inversion are still more powerful in the cases to which they can be applied. The case of surfaces of the second degree is the only one, as far as I know, in which both the equipotential surfaces and the lines of force are
P5453 known when the lines of force are not plane curves. But there is an important class of problems in the theory of
P5454 electrical equilibrium, and in that of the conduction of currents,
P5455 in which we have to consider space of two dimensions only.
P5456 For instance, if throughout the part of the electric field under consideration, and for a considerable distance beyond it, the surfaces of all the conductors are generated by the motion of straight lines parallel to the axis of z, and if the part of the field where this ceases to be the case is so far from the part considered that the
P5457 electrical action of the distant part on the field may be neglected,
P5458 then the electricity will be uniformly distributed along each gene
P5459 rating line, and if we consider a part of the field bounded by two planes perpendicular to the axis of z and at distance unity, the
P5460 potential and the distribution of electricity will be functions of x and y only.

P5696 density is directly as the square of the distance from the edge. In reality, in all cases in which the density becomes infinite at

P5976 203.] In many electrical instruments a wire grating is used to
P5977 prevent certain parts of the apparatus from being electrified by
P5978 induction. We know that if a conductor be entirely surrounded
P5979 by a metallic vessel at the same potential with itself, no electricity
P5980 can be induced on the surface of the conductor by any electrified
P5981 body outside the vessel. The conductor, however, when completely
P5982 surrounded by metal, cannot be seen, and therefore, in certain cases,
P5983 an aperture is left which is covered with a grating of fine wire.
P5984 Let us investigate the effect of this grating in diminishing the
P5985 effect of electrical induction. We shall suppose the grating to
P5986 consist of a series of parallel wires in one plane and at equal
P5987 intervals, the diameter of the wires being small compared with the
P5988 distance between them, while the nearest portions of the electrified
P5989 bodies on the one side and of the protected conductor on the other
P5990 are at distances from the plane of the screen, which are considerable
P5991 compared with the distance between consecutive wires.
