P6109 ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS.
P6110 On Electrostatic Instruments.
P6111 THE instruments which we have to consider at present may be
P6112 divided into the following classes :
P6113 (1) Electrical machines for the production and augmentation of
P6114 (2) Multipliers, for increasing electrification in a known ratio. (3) Electrometers, for the measurement of electric potentials and
P6115 charges. (4) Accumulators, for holding large electrical charges.
P6116 Electrical Machines.
P6117 207.] In the common electrical machine a plate or cylinder of glass is made to revolve so as to rub against a surface of leather, on which is spread an amalgam of zinc and mercury. The surface
P6118 of the glass becomes electrified positively and that of the rubber negatively. As the electrified surface of the glass moves away from the negative electrification of the rubber it acquires a high positive potential. It then comes opposite to a set of sharp metal points in connexion with the conductor of the machine. The posi tive electrification of the glass induces a negative electrification of the points, which is the more intense the sharper the points and the nearer they are to the glass.
P6119 When the machine works properly there is a discharge through
P6120 the air between the glass and the points, the glass loses part of its positive charge, which is transferred to the points and so to the insulated prime conductor of the machine, and to any other body with which it is in electric communication.
P6121 The portion of the glass which is advancing towards the rubber has thus a smaller positive charge than that which is leaving it at the same time, so that the rubber, and the conductors in com munication with it, become negatively electrified. 208.] ELECTROPHORUS. 255
P6122 The highly positive surface of the glass where it leaves the rubber is more attracted by the negative charge of the rubber than the partially discharged surface wnich is advancing towards the rubber. The electrical forces therefore act as a resistance to the force
P6123 employed in turning the machine. The work done in turning the machine is therefore greater than that spent in overcoming ordinary friction and other resistances, and the excess is employed in pro ducing a state of electrification whose energy is equivalent to this
P6124 The work done in overcoming friction is at once converted into
P6125 heat in the bodies rubbed together. The electrical energy may
P6126 be also converted either into mechanical energy or into heat. If the machine does not store up mechanical energy, all the
P6127 energy will be converted into heat, and the only difference between the heat due to friction and that due to electrical action is that the
P6128 former is generated at the rubbing surfaces while the latter may be
P6129 generated in conductors at a distance *.
P6130 We have seen that the electrical charge on the surface of the
P6131 glass is attracted by the rubber. If this attraction were sufficiently intense there would be a discharge between the glass and the rubber, instead of between the glass and the collecting points. To prevent this, flaps of silk are attached to the rubber. These become negatively electrified and adhere to the glass, and so diminish the
P6132 potential near the rubber.
P6133 The potential therefore increases more gradually as the glass moves away from the rubber, and therefore at any one point there is less attraction of the charge on the glass towards the rubber, and consequently less danger of direct discharge to the rubber.
P6134 In some electrical machines the moving part is of ebonite instead of glass, and the rubbers of wool or fur. The rubber is then elec trified positively and the prime conductor negatively.
P6135 The Electrophorus of Volta.
P6136 208.] The electrophorus consists of a plate of resin or of ebonite
P6137 backed with metal, and a plate of metal of the same size. An
P6138 insulating handle can be screwed to the back of either of these plates. The ebonite plate has a metal pin which connects the metal
P6139 * It is probable that in many cases where dynamical energy is converted into heat by friction, part of the energy may be first transformed into electrical energy and
P6140 then converted into heat as the electrical energy is spent in maintaining currents of
P6141 short circuit close to the rubbing surfaces. See SirW. Thomson. On the Electro
P6142 dynamic Qualities of Metals. Phil. Trans., 1856, p. 650. 256 ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS. [209.
P6143 plate with the metal back of the ebonite plate when the two plates
P6144 The ebonite plate is electrified negatively by rubbing it with wool or cat s skin. The metal plate is then brought near the
P6145 ebonite by means of the insulating handle. No direct discharge
P6146 passes between the ebonite and the metal plate, but the potential of the metal plate is rendered negative by induction, so that when it comes within a certain distance of the metal pin a spark passes,
P6147 and if the metal plate be now carried to a distance it is found to have a positive charge which may be communicated to a con ductor. The metal at the back of the ebonite plate is found to
P6148 have a negative charge equal and opposite to the charge of the metal
P6149 In using the instrument to charge a condenser or accumulator one of the plates is laid on a conductor in communication with the earth, and the other is first laid on it, then removed and applied to the electrode of the condenser, then laid on the fixed plate and the process repeated. If the ebonite plate is fixed the condenser will be charged positively. If the metal plate is fixed the condenser will be charged negatively.
P6150 The work done by the hand in separating the plates is always greater than the work done by the electrical attraction during the
P6151 approach of the plates, so that the operation of charging the con denser involves the expenditure of work. Part of this work is accounted for by the energy of the charged condenser, part is spent in producing the noise and heat of the sparks, and the rest in overcoming other resistances to the motion.
P6152 On Machines producing Electrification by Mechanical Work.
P6153 209.] In the ordinary frictional electrical machine the work done in overcoming friction is far greater than that done in increasing the electrification. Hence any arrangement by which the elec
P6154 trification may be produced entirely by mechanical work against
P6155 the electrical forces is of scientific importance if not of practical
P6156 value. The first machine of this kind seems to have been Nicholson s
P6157 Revolving Doubler, described in the Philosophical Transactions for
P6158 1788 as ( an instrument which by the turning of a Winch produces
P6159 the two states of Electricity without friction or communication with the Earth/
P6160 210.] It was by means of the revolving doubler that Volta succeeded in developing from the electrification of the pile an 210.] THE REVOLTING DOUBLER. 257
P6161 electrification capable of affecting- his electrometer. Instruments
P6162 on the same principle have been invented independently by Mr. C. F. Varley*, and SirW, Thomson.
P6163 These instruments consist essentially of insulated conductors of
P6164 various forms, some fixed and others moveable. The moveable
P6165 conductors are called Carriers, and the fixed ones may be called
P6166 Inductors, Receivers, and Regenerators. The inductors and receivers are so formed that when the carriers arrive at certain points in
P6167 their revolution they are almost completely surrounded by a con ducting body. As the inductors and receivers cannot completely surround the carrier and at the same time allow it to move freely
P6168 in and out without a complicated arrangement of moveable pieces,
P6169 the instrument is not theoretically perfect without a pair of re
P6170 generators, which store up the small amount of electricity which the carriers retain when they emerge from the receivers.
P6171 For the present, however, we may suppose the inductors and receivers to surround the carrier completely when it is within them, in which case the theory is much simplified.
P6172 We shall suppose the machine to consist of two inductors A and C, and of two receivers B and D, with two carriers F and G.
P6173 Suppose the inductor A to be positively electrified so that its
P6174 potential is A, and that the carrier F is within it and is at potential
P6175 F. Then, if Q is the coefficient of induction (taken positive) between
P6176 A and F3 the quantity of electricity on the carrier will be Q (FA}.
P6177 If the carrier, while within the inductor, is put in connexion with
P6178 the earth, then F = 0, and the charge on the carrier will be QA,
P6179 a negative quantity. Let the carrier be carried round till it is within the receiver B, and let it then come in contact with a spring
P6180 so as to be in electrical connexion with B. It will then, as was
P6181 shewn in Art. 32, become completely discharged, and will com
P6182 municate its whole negative charge to the receiver B.
P6183 The carrier will next enter the inductor (7, which we shall suppose
P6184 charged negatively. While within C it is put in connexion with
P6185 the earth and thus acquires a positive charge, which it carries off
P6186 and communicates to the receiver D, and so on.
P6187 In this way, if the potentials of the inductors remain always
P6188 constant, the receivers B and D receive successive charges, which
P6189 are the same for every revolution of the carrier, and thus every
P6190 revolution produces an equal increment of electricity in the re

P6212 Instruments of this kind may be used for various purposes.
P6213 For producing* a copious supply of electricity at a high potential, as is done by means of Mr. Varley s large machine.
P6214 For adjusting the charge of a condenser, as in the case of Thomson s electrometer, the charge of which can be increased or diminished by a few turns of a veiy small machine of this kind, which is then called a Replenishes
P6215 For multiplying small differences of potential. The inductors
P6216 may be charged at first to an exceedingly small potential, as, for
P6217 instance, that due to a thermo-electric pair, then, by turning the
P6218 machine, the difference of potentials may be continually multiplied
P6219 till it becomes capable of measurement by an ordinary electrometer.
P6220 By determining by experiment the ratio of increase of this difference
P6221 due to each turn of the machine, the original electromotive force
P6222 with which the inductors were charged may be deduced from the number of turns and the final electrification.
P6223 In most of these instruments the carriers are made to revolve
P6224 about an axis and to come into the proper positions with respect to the inductors by turning an axle. The connexions are made by means of springs so placed that the carriers come in contact with them at the proper instants.
P6225 211.] Sir AY. Thomson*, however, has constructed a machine for multiplying electrical charges in which the carriers are drops of water falling out of the inside of an inductor into an insulated receiver. The receiver is thus continually supplied with electricity
P6226 of opposite sign to that of the inductor. If the inductor is electrified positively, the receiver will receive a continually increasing charge of negative electricity.
P6227 The water is made to escape from the receiver by means of a funnel, the nozzle of which is almost surrounded by the metal of the receiver. The drops falling from this nozzle are therefore nearly free from electrification. Another inductor and receiver of the same construction are arranged so that the inductor of the one system is in connexion with the receiver of the other. The rate of increase of charge of the receivers is thus no longer constant,
P6228 but increases in a geometrical progression with the time, the charges of the two receivers being of opposite signs. This increase goes on till the falling drops are so diverted from their course by
P6229 * Proc. E. S., June 20, 1867.
P6230 S3 260 ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS. [212.
P6231 the electrical action that they fall outside of the receiver or even strike the inductor.
P6232 In this instrument the energy of the electrification is drawn
P6233 from that of the falling drops. 212.] Several other electrical machines have been constructed
P6234 in which the principle of electric induction is employed. Of these the most remarkable is that of Holtz, in which the carrier is a glass
P6235 plate varnished with gum-lac and the inductors are pieces of pasteboard. Sparks are prevented from passing between the parts of the apparatus by means of two glass plates, one on each side of the revolving carrier plate. This machine is found to be very
P6236 effective, and not to be much affected by the state of the atmo sphere. The principle is the same as in the revolving doubler and
P6237 the instruments developed out of the same idea, but as the carrier is an insulating plate and the inductors are imperfect conductors, the complete explanation of the action is. more difficult than in
P6238 the case where the carriers are good conductors of known form and are charged and discharged at definite points.
P6239 213.] In the electrical machines already described sparks occur whenever the carrier comes in
P6240 contact with a conductor at a
P6241 different potential from its own.

P6244 a loss of energy, and therefore the whole work employed in turning the machine is not con verted into electrification in an
P6245 available form, but part is spent

P6252 to prevent loss of work.

P6305 On Electrometers and Electroscopes.
P6306 214.] An electrometer is an instrument by means of which electrical charges or electrical potentials may be measured. In
P6307 struments by means of which the existence of electric charges or
P6308 of differences of potential may be indicated, but which are not
P6309 capable of affording numerical measures, are called Electroscopes.
P6310 An electroscope if sufficiently sensible may be used in electrical
P6311 measurements, provided we can make the measurement depend on 2 1 5.] COULOMB S TORSION BALANCE. 263
P6312 the absence of electrification. For instance, if we have two charged
P6313 bodies A and we may use the method described in Chapter I to determine which body has the greater charge. Let the body A
P6314 be carried by an insulating support into the interior of an insulated
P6315 closed vessel C. Let C be connected to earth and again insulated. There will then be no external electrification on C. Now let A
P6316 be removed, and B introduced into the interior of C, and the elec trification of C tested by an electroscope. If the charge of B is
P6317 equal to that of A there will be no electrification, but if it is greater
P6318 or less there will be electrification of the same kind as that of B, or
P6319 Methods of this kind, in which the thing to be observed is the non-existence of some phenomenon, are called null or zero methods. They require only an instrument capable of detecting the existence of the phenomenon.
P6320 In another class of instruments for the registration of phe
P6321 nomena the instrument may be depended upon to give always the
P6322 same indication for the same value of the quantity to be registered, but the readings of the scale of the instrument are not proportional to the values of the quantity, and the relation between these readings and the corresponding value is unknown, except that the one is some continuous function of the other. Several electrometers
P6323 depending on the mutual repulsion of parts of the instrument which are similarly electrified are of this class. The use of such
P6324 instruments is to register phenomena, not to measure them. Instead of the true values of the quantity to be measured, a series of
P6325 numbers is obtained, which may be used afterwards to determine these values when the scale of the instrument has been properly
P6326 investigated and tabulated. In a still higher class of instruments the scale readings are
P6327 proportional to the quantity to be measured, so that all that is
P6328 required for the complete measurement of the quantity is a know ledge of the coefficient by which the scale readings must be
P6329 multiplied to obtain the true value of the quantity. Instruments so constructed that they contain within themselves
P6330 the means of independently determining the true values of quan tities are called Absolute Instruments.
P6331 215.] Coulomb s Torsion Balance.
P6332 A great number of the experiments by which Coulomb 264 ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS. [215.
P6333 established the fundamental laws of electricity were made by mea suring the force between two small spheres charged with electricity, one of which was fixed while the other was held in equilibrium by two forces, the electrical action between the spheres, and the
P6334 torsional elasticity of a glass fibre or metal wire. See Art. 38.
P6335 The balance of torsion consists of a horizontal arm of gum-lac, suspended by a fine wire or glass fibre, and carrying at one end a little sphere of elder pith, smoothly gilt. The suspension wire is fastened above to the vertical axis of an arm which can be moved
P6336 round a horizontal graduated circle, so as to twist the upper end
P6337 of the wire about its own axis any number of degrees. The whole of this apparatus is enclosed in a case. Another little
P6338 sphere is so mounted on an insulating stem that it can be charged
P6339 and introduced into the case through a hole, and brought so that
P6340 its centre coincides with a definite point in the horizontal circle
P6341 described by the suspended sphere. The position of the suspended
P6342 sphere is ascertained by means of a graduated circle engraved on
P6343 the cylindrical glass case of the instrument.
P6344 Now suppose both spheres charged, and the suspended sphere
P6345 in equilibrium in a known position such that the torsion-arm makes
P6346 an angle with the radius through the centre of the fixed sphere.
P6347 The distance of the centres is then 2 a sin \ 0, where a is the radius
P6348 of the torsion-arm, and if F is the force between the spheres the
P6349 moment of this force about the axis of torsion is Fa cos i 0.
P6350 Let both spheres be completely discharged, and let the torsion
P6351 arm now be in equilibrium at an angle $ with the radius through
P6352 M Then the angle through which the electrical force twisted the torsion-arm must have been and if is the moment of
P6353 the torsional elasticity of the fibre, we shall have the equation
P6354 Fa cos ^0 = M(0-4&amp;gt;).
P6355 Hence, if we can ascertain M, we can determine F} the actual
P6356 force between the spheres at the distance 2 a sin \ 0.
P6357 To find My the moment of torsion, let / be the moment of inertia

P6379 Electrometersfor the Measurement of Potentials.
P6380 216.] In all electrometers the moveable part is a body charged with electricity, and its potential is different from that of certain of the fixed parts round it. When, as in Coulomb s method, an
P6381 insulated body having a certain charge is used, it is the charge
P6382 which is the direct object of measurement. We may, however,
P6383 connect the balls of Coulomb s electrometer, by means of fine wires, with different conductors. The charges of the balls will then depend on the values of the potentials of these conductors and on the potential of the case of the instrument. The charge on each
P6384 ball will be approximately equal to its radius multiplied by the
P6385 excess of its potential over that of the case of the instrument, provided the radii of the balls are small compared with their
P6386 distances from each other and from the sides or opening of the
P6387 Coulomb s form of apparatus, however, is not well adapted for measurements of this kind, owing to the smallness of the force between spheres at the proper distances when the difference of po
P6388 tentials is small. A more convenient form is that of the Attracted
P6389 Disk Electrometer. The first electrometers on this principle were
P6390 constructed by Sir W. Snow Harris*. They have since been
P6391 brought to great perfection, both in theory and construction, by SirW. Thomson f.
P6392 When two disks at different potentials are brought face to face
P6393 with a small interval between them there will be a nearly uniform electrification on the opposite faces and very little electrification on the backs of the disks, provided there are no other conductors or electrified bodies in the neighbourhood. The charge on the positive disk will be approximately proportional to its area, and to the difference of potentials of the disks, and inversely as the distance between them. Hence, by making the areas of the disks large

P6396 British Association, Dundee, 1867. PRINCIPLE OF THE GUARD-RING. 267
P6397 and the distance between them small, a small difference of potential
P6398 may give rise to a measurable force of attraction.
P6399 The mathematical theory of the distribution of electricity over two disks thus arranged is given at Art. 202, but since it is im
P6400 possible to make the case of the apparatus so large that we may
P6401 suppose the disks insulated in an infinite space, the indications of the instrument in this form are not easily interpreted numerically.
P6402 217.] The addition of the guard-ring to the attracted disk is one
P6403 of the chief improvements which Sir W. Thomson has made on the
P6404 Instead of suspending the whole of one of the disks and determ ining the force acting upon it, a central portion of the disk is separated from the rest to form the attracted disk, and the outer ring forming the remainder of the disk is fixed. In this way the force is measured only on that part of the disk where it is most regular, and the want of uniformity of the electrification near the
P6405 edge is of no importance, as it occurs on the guard-ring and not on the suspended part of the disk.
P6406 Besides this, by connecting the guard-ring with a metal case surrounding the back of the attracted disk and all its suspending apparatus, the electrification of the back of the disk is rendered 268 ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS. [2 I/.
P6407 impossible, for it is part of the inner surface of a closed hollow conductor all at the same potential.
P6408 Thomson s Absolute Electrometer therefore consists essentially of two parallel plates at different potentials, one of which is made so that a certain area, no part of which is near the edge of the plate, is moveable under the action of electric force. To fix our ideas we may suppose the attracted disk and guard-ring uppermost. The fixed disk is horizontal, and is mounted on an insulating stem which has a measurable vertical motion given to it by means of a micrometer screw. The guard-ring is at least as large as the fixed disk ; its lower surface is truly plane and parallel to the fixed
P6409 disk. A delicate balance is erected on the guard-ring to which

Its position with respect to the guard ring is then ascertained by means of a system of fiducial marks.

P6505 to increase the area of its surface opposed to B.

P6622 222.] To measure the potential of a conductor without touching it, we may measure the potential of the air at any point in the neighbourhood of the conductor, and calculate that of the conductor from the result. If there be a hollow nearly surrounded by the conductor, then the potential at any point of the air in this hollow
P6623 witt be very nearly that of the conductor.
P6624 In this way it has been ascertained by Sir W. Thomson that if
P6625 two hollow conductors, one of copper and the other of zinc, are in metallic contact, then the potential of the air in the hollow surrounded by zinc is positive with reference to that of the air in the hollow surrounded by copper.

P6627 -small bodies to detach themselves from the end of the electrode,

P6629 rounding air. This may be done by causing shot, filings, sand, or

P6633 electrode. The potential is very soon made equal to that of the air at the burning end of the match. Even a fine metallic point is sufficient to create a discharge by means of the particles of the air when the difference of potentials is considerable, but if we wish to reduce this difference to zero, we must use one of the methods stated above.
P6634 If we only wish to ascertain the sign of the difference of the potentials at two places, and not its numerical value, we may cause drops or filings to be discharged at one of the places from a nozzle connected with the other place, and catch the drops or filings in an insulated vessel. Each drop as it falls is charged with a certain amount of electricity, and it is completely discharged into the vessel. The charge of the vessel therefore is continually ac cumulating, and after a sufficient number of drops have fallen, the charge of the vessel may be tested by the roughest methods. The
P6635 sign of the charge is positive if the potential of the nozzle is positive relatively to that of the surrounding air.
P6636 MEASUREMENT OF SURFACE-DENSITY OF ELECTRIFICATION.
P6637 Theory of the Proof Plane.
P6638 223.] In testing the results of the mathematical theory of the distribution of electricity on the surface of conductors, it is necessary to be able to measure the surface-density at different points of the conductor. For this purpose Coulomb employed a small disk
P6639 of gilt paper fastened to an insulating stem of gum-lac. He ap
P6640 plied this disk to various points of the conductor by placing it so as to coincide as nearly as possible with the surface of the
P6641 conductor. He then removed it by means of the insulating stem,
P6642 and measured the charge of the disk by means of his electrometer. Since the surface of the disk, when applied to the conductor,
P6643 nearly coincided with that of the conductor, he concluded that the surface-density on the outer surface of the disk was nearly equal to that on the surface of the conductor at that place, and that
P6644 the charge on the disk when removed was nearly equal to that
P6645 on an area of the surface of the conductor equal to that of one side of the disk. This disk, when employed in this way, is called Coulomb s Proof Plane.
P6646 As objections have been raised to Coulombs use of the proof plane, I shall make some remarks on the theory of the experiment. 278 ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS. [224.
P6647 The experiment consists in bringing- a small conducting body into contact with the surface of the conductor at the point where the density is to be measured, and then removing the body and
P6648 determining its charge.
P6649 &quot;We have first to shew that the charge on the small body when
P6650 in contact with the conductor is proportional to the surface density which existed at the point of contact before the small body was placed there.
P6651 We shall suppose that all the dimensions of the small body, and
P6652 especially its dimension in the direction of the normal at the point
P6653 of contact, are small compared with either of the radii of curvature of the conductor at the point of contact. Hence the variation of the resultant force due to the conductor supposed rigidly electrified
P6654 within the space occupied by the small body may be neglected, and we may treat the surface of the conductor near the small body
P6655 Now the charge which the small body will take by contact with
P6656 a plane surface will be proportional to the resultant force normal
P6657 to the surface, that is, to the surface-density. We shall ascertain
P6658 the amount of the charge for particular forms of the body.
P6659 We have next to shew that when the small body is removed no
P6660 spark will pass between it and the conductor, so that it will carry
P6661 its charge with it. This is evident, because when the bodies are
P6662 in contact their potentials are the same, and therefore the density on the parts nearest to the point of contact is extremely small.
P6663 When the small body is removed to a very short distance from
P6664 the conductor, which we shall suppose to be electrified positively, then the electrification at the point nearest to the small body is no longer zero but positive, but, since the charge of the small body is positive, the positive electrification close to the small body will
P6665 be less than at other neighbouring points of the surface. Now
P6666 the passage of a spark depends in general on the magnitude of the
P6667 resultant force, and this on the surface-density. Hence, since we
P6668 suppose that the conductor is not so highly electrified as to be discharging electricity from the other parts of its surface, it will not discharge a spark to the small body from a part of its surface
P6669 which we have shewn to have a smaller surface-density.
P6670 224.] We shall now consider various forms of the small body.
P6671 Suppose it to be a small hemisphere applied to the conductor so as to touch it at the centre of its flat side.

P6734 On Electric Accumulators and the Measurement of Capacity.
P6735 226.] An Accumulator or Condenser is an apparatus consisting
P6736 of two conducting surfaces separated by an insulating dielectric medium.
P6737 A Leyden jar is an accumulator in which an inside coating of
P6738 tinfoil is separated from the outside coating by the glass of which the jar is made. The original Leyden phial was a glass vessel containing- water which was separated by the glass from the hand which held it.
P6739 The outer surface of any insulated conductor may be considered
P6740 as one of the surfaces of an accumulator, the other being the earth or the walls of the room in which it is placed, and the intervening air being the dielectric medium.
P6741 The capacity of an accumulator is measured by the quantity of electricity with which the inner surface must be charged to make the difference between the potentials of the surfaces unity.
P6742 Since every electrical potential is the sum of a number of parts found by dividing each electrical element by its distance from a point, the ratio of a quantity of electricity to a potential must have the dimensions of a line. Hence electrostatic capacity is a linear quantity, or we may measure it in feet or metres without
P6743 In electrical researches accumulators are used for two principal purposes, for receiving and retaining large quantities of electricity in as small a compass as possible, and for measuring definite quan tities of electricity by means of the potential to which they raise
P6744 the accumulator. 282 ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS. [ 22 7
P6745 For the retention of electrical charges nothing has been devised more perfect than the Leyden jar. The principal part of the loss
P6746 arises from the electricity creeping along the damp uncoated surface of the glass from the one coating to the other. This may be checked in a great degree by artificially drying the air within the jar, and by varnishing the surface of the glass where it is exposed to the
P6747 atmosphere. In Sir W. Thomsons electroscopes there is a very
P6748 small percentage of loss from day to day, and I believe that none of this loss can be traced to direct conduction either through air or through glass when the glass is good, but that it arises chiefly from superficial conduction along the various insulating stems and glass surfaces of the instrument.
P6749 In fact, the same electrician has communicated a charge to sulphuric acid in a large bulb with a long neck, and has then her metically sealed the neck by fusing it, so that the charge was com pletely surrounded by glass, and after some years the charge was found still to be retained.
P6750 It is only, however, when cold, that glass insulates in this
P6751 way, for the charge escapes at once if the glass is heated to
P6752 a temperature below 100C.
P6753 When it is desired to obtain great capacity in small compass,
P6754 accumulators in which the dielectric is sheet caoutchouc, mica, or
P6755 paper impregnated with paraffin are convenient. 227.] For accumulators of the second class, intended for the
P6756 measurement of quantities of electricity, all solid dielectrics must be employed with great caution on account of the property which they
P6757 possess called Electric Absorption.
P6758 The only safe dielectric for such accumulators is air, which has this inconvenience, that if any dust or dirt gets into the narrow space between the opposed surfaces, which ought to be occupied only by air, it not only alters the thickness of the stratum of air, but may establish a connexion between the opposed surfaces, in which
P6759 case the accumulator will not hold a charge.
P6760 To determine in absolute measure, that is to say in feet or metres, the capacity of an accumulator, we must either first ascertain its form and size, and then solve the problem of the distribution of electricity on its opposed surfaces, or we must compare its capacity with that of another accumulator, for which this problem has been
P6761 As the problem is a very difficult one, it is best to begin with an accumulator constructed of a form for which the solution is known. 228.] MEASUREMENT OF CAPACITY. 283
P6762 Thus the capacity of an insulated sphere in an unlimited space is known to be measured by the radius of the sphere.
P6763 A sphere suspended in a room was actually used by MM. Kohl
P6764 ran sch and Weber, as an absolute standard with which they com
P6765 pared the capacity of other accumulators.
P6766 The capacity, however, of a sphere of moderate size is so small when compared with the capacities of the accumulators in common
P6767 use that the sphere is not a convenient standard measure.
P6768 Its capacity might be greatly increased by surrounding the sphere with a hollow concentric spherical surface of somewhat greater radius. The capacity of the inner surface is then a fourth proportional to the thickness of the stratum of air and the radii of the two surfaces.
P6769 Sir W. Thomson has employed this arrangement as a standard of
P6770 capacity, but the difficulties of working the surfaces truly spherical, of making them truly concentric, and of measuring their distance and their radii with sufficient accuracy, are considerable.
P6771 We are therefore led to prefer for an absolute measure of capacity
P6772 a form in which the opposed surfaces are parallel planes. The accuracy of the surface of the planes can be easily tested,
P6773 and their distance can be measured by a micrometer screw, and
P6774 may be made capable of continuous variation, which is a most
P6775 important property of a measuring instrument. The only difficulty remaining arises from the fact that the planes
P6776 must necessarily be bounded, and that the distribution of electricity near the boundaries of the planes has not been rigidly calculated. It is true that if we make them equal circular disks, whose radius
P6777 is large compared with the distance between them, we may treat
P6778 the edges of the disks as if they were straight lines, and calculate the distribution of electricity by the method due to Helmholtz, and described at Art. 202. But it will be noticed that in this case
P6779 part of the electricity is distributed on the back of each disk, and that in the calculation it has been supposed that there are no conductors in the neighbourhood, which is not and cannot be the
P6780 case in a small instrument.
P6781 228.] We therefore prefer the following arrangement, due to
P6782 Sir W. Thomson, which we may call the Guard-ring arrangement,
P6783 by means of which the quantity of electricity on an insulated disk
P6784 may be exactly determined in terms of its potential. 284: ELECTROSTATIC INSTRUMENTS. [228. 229.] COMPARISON OF CAPACITIES. 285
P6785 Now let the vessel Bb be put in connexion with the earth. The charge on the disk A will no longer be uniformly distributed, but it will remain the same in quantity, and if we now discharge A we
P6786 shall obtain a quantity of electricity, the value of which we know
P6787 R in terms of V, the original difference of potentials and the measur
P6788 able quantities E, and A.
P6789 On the Comparison of the Capacity of Accumulators. 229.] The form of accumulator which is best fitted to have its
P6790 capacity determined in absolute measure from the form and dimen
P6791 sions of its parts is not generally the most suitable for electrical experiments. It is desirable that the measures of capacity in actual use should be accumulators having only two conducting surfaces, one of which is as nearly as possible surrounded by the other. The guard-ring accumulator, on the other hand, has three independent conducting portions which must be charged and discharged in a certain order. Hence it is desirable to be able to compare the capacities of two accumulators by an electrical process, so as to test
P6792 accumulators which may afterwards serve as secondary standards. I shall first shew how to test the equality of the capacity of two
P6793 guard-ring accumulators.

P6842 THE ELECTRIC CURRENT.
P6843 230.] WE have seen, in Art. 45, that when a conductor is in
P6844 electrical equilibrium the potential at every point of the conductor
P6845 If two conductors A and B are charged with electricity so that
P6846 the potential of A is higher than that of B, then, if they are put in communication by means of a metallic wire C touching both of them, part of the charge of A will be transferred to B, and the
P6847 potentials of A and B will become in a very short time equalized.
P6848 231.] During this process certain phenomena are observed in the wire C, which are called the phenomena of the electric conflict
P6849 The first of these phenomena is the transference of positive
P6850 electrification from A to B and of negative electrification from B
P6851 to A. This transference may be also effected in a slower manner
P6852 by bringing a small insulated body into contact with A and B
P6853 alternately. By this process, which we may call electrical con
P6854 vection, successive small portions of the electrification of each body are transferred to the other. In either case a certain quantity of electricity, or of the state of electrification, passes from one place to another along a certain path in the space between the bodies.
P6855 Whatever therefore may be our opinion of the nature of elec tricity, we must admit that the process which we have described constitutes a current of electricity. This current may be described 232.] THE VOLTAIC BATTERY. 289
P6856 as a current of positive electricity from A to .5, or a current of negative electricity from B to A, or as a combination of these two
P6857 According to Fechner s and Weber s theory it is a combination
P6858 of a current of positive electricity with an exactly equal current of negative electricity in the opposite direction through the same substance. It is necessary to remember this exceedingly artificial hypothesis regarding the constitution of the current in order to
P6859 understand the statement of some of Weber s most valuable ex
P6860 N If, as in Art. 36, we suppose P units of positive electricity
P6861 transferred from A to B , and units of negative electricity trans ferred from B to A in unit of time, then, according to Weber s
P6862 theory, P=N, and P or N is to be taken as the numerical measure
P6863 We, on the contrary, make no assumption as to the relation
P6864 between P and N, but attend only to the result of the current,
P6865 N namely, the transference of P + Ar of positive electrification from A
P6866 to j5, and we shall consider P-f the true measure of the current. The current, therefore, which Weber would call 1 we shall call 2.
P6867 232.] In the case of the current between two insulated con ductors at different potentials the operation is soon brought to an end by the equalization of the potentials of the two bodies, and the current is therefore essentially a Transient current.
P6868 But there are methods by which the difference of potentials of
P6869 the conductors may be maintained constant, in which case the
P6870 current will continue to flow with uniform strength as a Steady Current.
P6871 The Voltaic Battery.
P6872 The most convenient method of producing a steady current is by means of the Voltaic Battery.
P6873 For the sake of distinctness we shall describe Daniell s Constant
P6874 A solution of sulphate of zinc is placed in a cell of porous earth
P6875 enware, and this cell is placed in a vessel containing a saturated
P6876 solution of sulphate of copper. A piece of zinc is dipped into the
P6877 sulphate of zinc, and a piece of copper is dipped into the sulphate
P6878 of copper. Wires are soldered to the zinc and to the copper above
P6879 VOL. i. u 290 THE ELECTRIC CURRENT. [ 2 33*
P6880 the surface of the liquid. This combination is called a cell or element of Daniell s battery. See Art. 272.
P6881 233.] If the cell is insulated by being placed on a non-con ducting stand, and if the wire connected with the copper is put in contact with an insulated conductor A, and the wire connected
P6882 with the zinc is put in contact with J3, another insulated conductor
P6883 of the same metal as A, then it may be shewn by means of a delicate
P6884 electrometer that the potential of A exceeds that of B by a certain
P6885 quantity. This difference of potentials is called the Electromotive
P6886 Force of the Daniell s Cell.
P6887 If A and B are now disconnected from the cell and put in
P6888 communication by means of a wire, a transient current passes
P6889 through the wire from A to J3, and the potentials of A and B become equal. A and B may then be charged again by the cell,
P6890 and the process repeated as long as the cell will work. But if
P6891 A and B be connected by means of the wire C, and at the same
P6892 time connected with the battery as before, then the cell will main tain a constant current through C, and also a constant difference
P6893 of potentials between A and B. This difference will not, as we
P6894 shall see, be equal to the whole electromotive force of the cell, for
P6895 part of this force is spent in maintaining the current through the
P6896 A number of cells placed in series so that the zinc of the first
P6897 cell is connected by metal with the copper of the second, and so on, is called a Voltaic Battery. The electromotive force of such a battery is the sum of the electromotive forces of the cells of which it is composed. If the battery is insulated it may be charged with electricity as a whole, but the potential of the copper end will always exceed that of the zinc end by the electromotive force of the battery, whatever the absolute value of either of these
P6898 potentials may be. The cells of the battery may be of very various
P6899 construction, containing different chemical substances and different
P6900 metals, provided they are such that chemical action does not go on when no current passes.
P6901 234.] Let us now consider a voltaic battery with its ends insulated from each other. The copper end will be positively or vitreously electrified, and the zinc end will be negatively or resinously electrified.
P6902 Let the two ends of the battery be now connected by means
P6903 of a wire. An electric current will commence, and will in a very
P6904 short time attain a constant value. It is then said to be a Steady Current. 236.] ELECTKOLYSIS. 291
P6905 Properties of the Current.
P6906 235.] The current forms a closed circuit in the direction from copper to zinc through the wires, and from zinc to copper through
P6907 If the circuit be broken by cutting any of the wires which connect the copper of one cell with the zinc of the next in order, the current will be stopped, and the potential of the end of the wire in connexion with the copper will be found to exceed that of the end of the wire in connexion with the zinc by a constant quantity, namely, the total electromotive force of the circuit.
P6908 Electrolytic Action of the Current.
P6909 236.] As long as the circuit is broken no chemical action goes
P6910 on in the cells, but as soon as the circuit is completed, zinc is dissolved from the zinc in each of the Daniell s cells, and copper is deposited on the copper.
P6911 The quantity of sulphate of zinc increases, and the quantity of sulphate of copper diminishes unless more is constantly supplied.
P6912 The quantity of zinc dissolved and also that of copper deposited is the same in each of the Daniell s cells throughout the circuit, what ever the size of the plates of the cell, and if any of the cells be of a different construction, the amount of chemical action in it bears a constant proportion to the action in the Daniell s cell. For instance, if one of the cells consists of two platinum plates dipped into sulphuric acid diluted with water, oxygen will be given off at the surface of the plate where the current enters the liquid, namely, the plate in metallic connexion with the copper of Daniell s cell, and hydrogen at the surface of the plate where the current leaves the liquid, namely, the plate connected with the zinc of
P6913 The volume of the hydrogen is exactly twice the volume of the oxygen given off in the same time, and the weight of the oxygen is exactly eight times the weight of the hydrogen.
P6914 In every cell of the circuit the weight of each substance dissolved, deposited, or decomposed is equal to a certain quantity called the electrochemical equivalent of that substance, multiplied by the strength of the current and by the time during which it has been flowing.
P6915 For the experiments which established this principle, see the seventh and eighth series of Faraday s Experimental Researches;
P6916 T: 2 292 THE ELECTRIC CURRENT. [237.
P6917 and for an investigation of the apparent exceptions to the rule, see
P6918 Miller s Chemical Physics and Wiedemann s Galvanismus. 237.] Substances which are decomposed in this way are called
P6919 Electrolytes. The process is called Electrolysis. The places where the current enters and leaves the electrolyte are called Electrodes. Of these the electrode by which the current enters is called the Anode, and that by which it leaves the electrolyte is called the Cathode. The components into which the electrolyte is resolved are called Ions : that which appears at the anode is called the Anion, and that which appears at the cathode is called the Cation.
P6920 Of these terms, which were, I believe, invented by Faraday with
P6921 the help of Dr. Whewell, the first three, namely, electrode, elec trolysis, and electrolyte have been generally adopted, and the mode of conduction of the current in which this kind of decomposition and transfer of the components takes place is called Electrolytic Conduction.
P6922 If a homogeneous electrolyte is placed in a tube of variable section, and if the electrodes are placed at the ends of this tube, it is found that when the current passes, the anion appears at the anode and the cation at the cathode, the quantities of these ions being electrochemically equivalent, and such as to be together equivalent to a certain quantity of the electrolyte. In the other parts of the tube, whether the section be large or small, uniform or varying, the composition of the electrolyte remains unaltered. Hence the amount of electrolysis which takes place across every
P6923 section of the tube is the same. Where the section is small the
P6924 action must therefore be more intense than where the section is
P6925 large, but the total amount of each ion which crosses any complete section of the electrolyte in a given time is the same for all sections.
P6926 The strength of the current may therefore be measured by the amount of electrolysis in a given time. An instrument by which
P6927 the quantity of the electrolytic products can be readily measured is called a Voltameter.
P6928 The strength of the current, as thus measured, is the same at every part of the circuit, and the total quantity of the elec trolytic products in the voltameter after any given time is pro portional to the amount of electricity which passes any section in the same time.
P6929 238.] If we introduce a voltameter at one part of the circuit of a voltaic battery, and break the circuit at another part, we may suppose the measurement of the current to be conducted thus. 239-] MAGNETIC ACTION. 293
P6930 Let the ends of the broken circuit be A and H, and let A be the
P6931 anode and B the cathode. Let an insulated ball be made to touch A and B alternately, it will carry from A to B a certain measurable
P6932 quantity of electricity at each journey. This quantity may be measured by an electrometer, or it may be calculated by mul
P6933 tiplying the electromotive force of the circuit by the electrostatic
P6934 capacity of the ball. Electricity is thus carried from A to B on the
P6935 insulated ball by a process which may be called Convection. At
P6936 the same time electrolysis goes on in the voltameter and in the
P6937 c^lls of the battery, and the amount of electrolysis in each cell may
P6938 be compared with the amount of electricity carried across by the insulated ball. The quantity of a substance which is electrolysed by one unit of electricity is called an Electrochemical equivalent
P6939 This experiment would be an extremely tedious and troublesome one if conducted in this way with a ball of ordinary magnitude and a manageable battery, for an enormous number of journeys would have to be made before an appreciable quantity of the electro lyte was decomposed. The experiment must therefore be considered as a mere illustration, the actual measurements of electrochemical
P6940 equivalents being conducted in a different way. But the experi ment may be considered as an illustration of the process of elec trolysis itself, for if we regard electrolytic conduction as a species of convection in which an electrochemical equivalent of the anion
P6941 travels with negative electricity in the direction of the anode, while an equivalent of the cation travels with positive electricity in the direction of the cathode, the whole amount of transfer of elec
P6942 tricity being one unit, we shall have an idea of the process of electrolysis, which, so far as I know, is not inconsistent with known
P6943 facts, though, on account of our ignorance of the nature of electricity
P6944 and of chemical compounds, it may be a very imperfect repre
P6945 sentation of what really takes place.
P6946 Magnetic Action of the Current.
P6947 239.] Oersted discovered that a magnet placed near a straight
P6948 electric current tends to place itself at right angles to the plane
P6949 passing through the magnet and the current. See Art. 475.
P6950 If a man were to place his body in the line of the current so
P6951 that the current from copper through the wire to zinc should flow from his head to his feet, and if he were to direct his face towards
P6952 the centre of the magnet, then that end of the magnet which tends 294 THE ELECTEIC CUR11ENT. [240.
P6953 to point to the north would, when the current flows, tend to point towards the man s right hand.
P6954 The nature and laws of this electromagnetic action will be dis
P6955 cussed when we come to the fourth part of this treatise. What we are concerned with at present is the fact that the electric
P6956 current has a magnetic action which is exerted outside the current, and by which its existence can be ascertained and its intensity measured without breaking the circuit or introducing anything into
P6957 The amount of the magnetic action has been ascertained to be strictly proportional to the strength of the current as measured by the products of electrolysis in the voltameter, and to be quite independent of the nature of the conductor in which the current is flowing, whether it be a metal or an electrolyte.
P6958 240.] An instrument which indicates the strength of an electric
P6959 current by its magnetic effects is called a Galvanometer. Galvanometers in general consist of one or more coils of silk
P6960 covered wire within which a magnet is suspended with its axis
P6961 horizontal. When a current is passed through the wire the magnet
P6962 tends to set itself with its axis perpendicular to the plane of the
P6963 coils. If we suppose the plane of the coils to be placed parallel to the plane of the earth s equator, and the current to flow round the coil from east to west in the direction of the apparent motion of the sun, then the magnet within will tend to set itself with its magnetization in the same direction as that of the earth con
P6964 sidered as a great magnet, the north pole of the earth being similar to that end of the compass needle which points south.

P6968 the instrument for our fourth part. When therefore we say that

P6989 242.] We have seen that when an electromotive force causes
P6990 a current to flow through a conductor, electricity is transferred from a place of higher to a place of lower potential. If the transfer had been made by convection, that is, by carrying successive charges on a ball from the one place to the other, work would have been done by the electrical forces on the ball, and this might have been turned to account. It is actually turned to account in a partial manner in those dry pile circuits where the electrodes have the form of bells, and the carrier ball is made to swing like a pendulum between the two bells and strike them alternately. In
P6991 this way the electrical action is made to keep up the swinging of the pendulum and to propagate the sound of the bells to a distance. In the case of the conducting wire we have the same
P6992 transfer of electricity from a place of high to a place of low potential without any external work being done. The principle of the Con servation of Energy therefore leads us to look for internal work in the conductor. In an electrolyte this internal work consists partly of the separation of its components. In other conductors it is entirely converted into heat.

P7006 243.] The analogy between the theory of the conduction of electricity and that of the conduction of heat is at first sight almost complete. If we take two systems geometrically similar, and such that the conductivity for heat at any part of the first is proportional
P7007 to the conductivity for electricity at the corresponding part of the

P7010 Thus, in the illustration we have given, in which flow of elec tricity corresponds to flow of heat, and electric potential to tem
P7011 perature, electricity tends to flow from places of high to places of low potential, exactly as heat tends to flow from places of high to places of low temperature.
P7012 244.] The theory of potential and that of temperature may therefore be made to illustrate one another ; there is, however, one
P7013 remarkable difference between the phenomena of electricity and
P7014 Suspend a conducting body within a closed conducting vessel by a silk thread, and charge the vessel with electricity. The potential
P7015 * PhU. Mag., Dec. 1851. 298 CONDUCTION AND RESISTANCE. [ 2 45
P7016 of the vessel and of all within it will be instantly raised, but however long and however powerfully the vessel be electrified, and whether the body within be allowed to come in contact with the vessel or not, no signs of electrification will appear within the vessel, nor will the body within shew any electrical effect when taken out.
P7017 But if the vessel is raised to a high temperature, the body within will rise to the same temperature, but only after a con siderable time, and if it is then taken out it will be found hot, and will remain so till it has continued to emit heat for some time.
P7018 The difference between the phenomena consists in the fact that bodies are capable of absorbing and emitting heat, whereas they
P7019 have no corresponding property with respect to electricity. A body
P7020 cannot be made hot without a certain amount of heat being supplied to it, depending on the mass and specific heat of the body, but the electric potential of a body may be raised to any extent in the way already described without communicatiDg any electricity
P7021 to the body. 245.] Again, suppose a body first heated and then placed inside
P7022 the closed vessel. The outside of the vessel will be at first at the
P7023 temperature of surrounding bodies, but it will soon get hot, and will remain hot till the heat of the interior body has escaped.
P7024 It is impossible to perform a corresponding electrical experiment. It is impossible so to electrify a body, and so to place it in a hollow vessel, that the outside of the vessel shall at first shew no
P7025 signs of electrification but shall afterwards become electrified. It was for some phenomenon of this kind that Faraday sought in vain under the name of an absolute charge of electricity.
P7026 Heat may be hidden in the interior of a body so as to have no
P7027 external action, but it is impossible to isolate a quantity of elec tricity so as to prevent it from being constantly in inductive relation with an equal quantity of electricity of the opposite kind.
P7028 There is nothing therefore among electric phenomena which corresponds to the capacity of a body for heat. This follows at
P7029 once from the doctrine which is asserted in this treatise, that electricity obeys the same condition of continuity as an incom pressible fluid. It is therefore impossible to give a bodily charge of electricity to any substance by forcing an additional quantity of electricity into it. See Arts. 61, 111, 329, 334. CHAPTER III.

P7032 246.] IF we define the potential of a hollow conducting vessel as the potential of the air inside the vessel, we may ascertain this potential by means of an electrometer as described in Part I,

P7034 then test the potential of the air inside each vessel, the potential of the air inside the zinc vessel will be positive as compared with that inside the copper vessel. The difference of potentials depends on the nature of the surface of the insides of the vessels, being
P7035 greatest when the zinc is bright and when the copper is coated
P7036 It appears from this that when two different metals are in contact there is in general an electromotive force acting from the one to the other, so as to make the potential of the one exceed that of the other by a certain quantity. This is Volta s theory of
P7037 Contact Electricity.
P7038 If we take a certain metal, say copper, as the standard, then
P7039 if the potential of iron in contact with copper at the zero potential is /, and that of zinc in contact with copper at zero is Z, then
P7040 the potential of zinc in contact with iron at zero will be ZI.
P7041 It appears from this result, which is true of any three metals, that the differences of potential of any two metals at the same temperature in contact is equal to the difference of their potentials when in contact with a third metal, so that if a circuit be formed of any number of metals at the same temperature there will be electrical equilibrium as soon as they have acquired their proper potentials, and there will be no current kept up in the circuit. 300 CONTACT FOKCE. [ 2 47
P7042 247.] If, however, the circuit consist of two metals and an elec trolyte, the electrolyte, according to Volta s theory, tends to reduce the potentials of the metals in contact with it to equality, so that the electromotive force at the metallic junction is no longer balanced, and a continuous current is kept up. The energy of this current is supplied by the chemical action which takes place between the electrolyte and the metals.
P7043 248.] The electric effect may. however, be produced without chemical action if by any other means we can produce an equali zation of the potentials of two metals in contact. Thus, in an
P7044 experiment due to Sir W. Thomson*, a copper funnel is placed in
P7045 contact with a vertical zinc cylinder, so that when copper filings
P7046 are allowed to pass through the funnel, they separate from each other and from the funnel near the middle of the zinc cylinder, and then fall into an insulated receiver placed below. The receiver is then found to be charged negatively, and the charge increases
P7047 as the filings continue to pour into it. At the same time the zinc cylinder with the copper funnel in it becomes charged more and more positively.
P7048 If now the zinc cylinder were connected with the receiver by a
P7049 wire, there would be a positive current in the wire from the cylinder to the receiver. The stream of copper filings, each filing charged negatively by induction, constitutes a negative current from the funnel to the receiver, or, in other words, a positive current from the receiver to the copper funnel. The positive current, therefore, passes through the air (by the filings) from zinc to copper, and through the metallic junction from copper to zinc, just as in the ordinary voltaic arrangement, but in this case the force which keeps up the current is not chemical action but gravity, which causes the filings to fall, in spite of the electrical attraction between the positively charged funnel and the negatively charged filings.

P7129 served when both junctions are below T.

P7133 253.] From this fact Thomson reasoned as follows :

P7138 must disappear somewhere in the circuit. Now at the tempera ture T iron and copper are neutral to each other, so that no
P7139 reversible thermal effect is produced at the hot junction, and at the cold junction there is, by Peltier s principle, an evolution of heat. Hence the only place where the heat can disappear is in the copper or iron portions of the circuit, so that either a current in iron from hot to cold must cool the iron, or a current in copper

P7176 Electrolytic Conduction.
P7177 255.] I HAVE already stated that when an electric current in any part of its circuit passes through certain compound substances called Electrolytes, the passage of the current is accompanied by
P7178 a certain chemical process called Electrolysis, in which the substance is resolved into two components called Ions, of which one, called the Anion, or the electronegative component, appears at the Anode, or place where the current enters the electrolyte, and the other, called the Cation, appears at the Cathode, or the place where the current leaves the electrolyte.
P7179 The complete investigation of Electrolysis belongs quite as much
P7180 to Chemistry as to Electricity. We shall consider it from an
P7181 electrical point of view, without discussing its application to the theory of the constitution of chemical compounds.
P7182 Of all electrical phenomena electrolysis appears the most likely
P7183 to furnish us with a real insight into the true nature of the electric
P7184 current, because we find currents of ordinary matter and currents of electricity forming essential parts of the same phenomenon.
P7185 It is probably for this very reason that, in the present imperfectly formed state of our ideas about electricity, the theories of electro lysis are so unsatisfactory.
P7186 The fundamental law of electrolysis, which was established by Faraday, and confirmed by the experiments of Beetz, Hittorf, and others down to the present time, is as follows :
P7187 The number of electrochemical equivalents of an electrolyte which are decomposed by the passage of an electric current during a given time is equal to the number of units of electricity which are trans ferred by the current in the same time.
P7188 The electrochemical equivalent of a substance is that quantity X 2, 308 ELECTROLYSIS. [255.
P7189 of the substance which is electrolysed by a unit current passing through the substance for a unit of time, or, in other words, by the
P7190 passage of a unit of electricity. When the unit of electricity is
P7191 denned in absolute measure the absolute value of the electro
P7192 chemical equivalent of each substance can be determined in grains
P7193 or in grammes. The electrochemical equivalents of different substances are pro
P7194 portional to their ordinary chemical equivalents. The ordinary chemical equivalents, however, are the mere numerical ratios in which the substances combine, whereas the electrochemical equi valents are quantities of matter of a determinate magnitude, de pending on the definition of the unit of electricity.
P7195 Every electrolyte consists of two components, which, during the electrolysis, appear where the current enters and leaves the elec trolyte, and nowhere else. Hence, if we conceive a surface described within the substance of the electrolyte, the amount of electrolysis which takes place through this surface, as measured by the elec trochemical equivalents of the components transferred across it in opposite directions, will be proportional to the total electric current through the surface.
P7196 The actual transfer of the ions through the substance of the electrolyte in opposite directions is therefore part of the phenomenon of the conduction of an electric current through an electrolyte. At every point of the electrolyte through which an electric current is passing there are also two opposite material currents of the anion and the cation, which have the same lines of flow with the electric
P7197 current, and are proportional to it in magnitude. It is therefore extremely natural to suppose that the currents of
P7198 the ions are convection currents of electricity, and, in particular, that every molecule of the cation is charged with a certain fixed quantity of positive electricity, which is the same for the molecules of all cations, and that every molecule of the anion is charged with an equal quantity of negative electricity.
P7199 The opposite motion of the ions through the electrolyte would
P7200 then be a complete physical representation of the electric current.
P7201 We may compare this motion of the ions with the motion of gases
P7202 and liquids through each other during the process of diffusion, there being this difference between the two processes, that, in diffusion, the different substances are only mixed together and the mixture is not homogeneous, whereas in electrolysis they are chemi cally combined and the electrolyte is homogeneous. In diffusion 257-] THEORY OP CLAUSIUS. 309
P7203 the determining cause of the motion of a substance in a given direction is a diminution of the quantity of that substance per unit of volume in that direction, whereas in electrolysis the motion of each ion is due to the electromotive force acting on the charged
P7204 256.] Clausius *, who has bestowed much study on the theory
P7205 of the molecular agitation of bodies, supposes that the molecules of all bodies are in a state of constant agitation, but that in solid bodies each molecule never passes beyond a certain distance from
P7206 its original position, whereas in fluids a molecule, after moving a certain distance from its original position, is just as likely to move still farther from it as to move back again. Hence the molecules of a fluid apparently at rest are continually changing their positions, and passing irregularly from one part of the fluid to another. In a compound fluid he supposes that not only the compound molecules travel about in this way, but that, in the collisions which occur between the compound molecules, the mole cules of which they are composed are often separated and change partners, so that the same individual atom is at one time associated with one atom of the opposite kind, and at another time with another. This process Clausius supposes to go on in the liquid at all times, but when an electromotive force acts on the liquid the motions of the molecules, which before were indifferently in all directions, are now influenced by the electromotive force, so that the positively charged molecules have a greater tendency towards the cathode than towards the anode, and the negatively charged molecules have a greater tendency to move in the opposite direction. Hence the molecules
P7207 of the cation will during their intervals of freedom struggle towards
P7208 the cathode, but will continually be checked in their course by pairing for a time with molecules of the anion, which are also
P7209 struggling through the crowd, but in the opposite direction.
P7210 257.] This theory of Clausius enables us to understand how it is,
P7211 that whereas the actual decomposition of an electrolyte requires an
P7212 electromotive force of finite magnitude, the conduction of the
P7213 current in the electrolyte obeys the law of Ohm, so that every
P7214 electromotive force within the electrolyte, even the feeblest, produces a current of proportionate magnitude.
P7215 According to the theory of Clausius, the decomposition and recomposition of the electrolyte is continually going on even when there is no current, and the veiy feeblest electromotive force is
P7216 * Fogg. Ann. bd. ci. s. 338 (1857). 310 ELECTROLYSIS. [25$.
P7217 sufficient to give this process a certain degree of direction, and so to produce the currents of the ions and the electric current, which is part of the same phenomenon. Within the electrolyte, however, the ions are never set free in finite quantity, and it is this liberation of the ions which requires a finite electromotive force. At the electrodes the ions accumulate, for the successive portions of the ions, as they arrive at the electrodes, instead of finding molecules of the opposite ion ready to combine with them, are forced into com
P7218 pany with molecules of their own kind, with which they cannot combine. The electromotive force required to produce this effect
P7219 is of finite magnitude, and forms an opposing electromotive force which produces a reversed current when other electromotive forces
P7220 are removed. When this reversed electromotive force, owing to
P7221 the accumulation of the ions at the electrode, is observed, the
P7222 electrodes are said to be Polarized.
P7223 258.] , One of the best methods of determining whether a body
P7224 is or is not an electrolyte is to place it between platinum electrodes and to pass a current through it for some time, and then, dis engaging the electrodes from the voltaic battery, and connecting
P7225 them with a galvanometer, to observe whether a reverse current, due to polarization of the electrodes, passes through the galvano meter. Such a current, being due to accumulation of different substances on the two electrodes, is a proof that the substance has been electrolytically decomposed by the original current from the battery. This method can often be applied where it is difficult, by direct chemical methods, to detect the presence of the products
P7226 of decomposition at the electrodes. See Art. 271.
P7227 259.] So far as we have gone the theory of electrolysis appears
P7228 very satisfactory. It explains the electric current, the nature of
P7229 which we do not understand, by means of the currents of the
P7230 material components of the electrolyte, the motion of which, though not visible to the eye, is easily demonstrated. It gives a
P7231 clear explanation, as Faraday has shewn, why an electrolyte which conducts in the liquid state is a non-conductor when solidified, for
P7232 unless the molecules can pass from one part to another no elec trolytic conduction can take place, so that the substance must be in a liquid state, either by fusion or by solution, in order to be a conductor.
P7233 But if we go on, and assume that the molecules of the ions
P7234 within the electrolyte are actually charged with certain definite quantities of electricity, positive and negative, so that the elec 260.] MOLECULAR CHARGE. 311
P7235 trolytic current is simply a current of convection, we find that this
P7236 tempting hypothesis leads us into very difficult ground.
P7237 In the first place, we must assume that in every electrolyte each molecule of the cation, as it is liberated at the cathode, commu
P7238 nicates to the cathode a charge of positive electricity, the amount of which is the same for every molecule, not only of that cation but of all other cations. In the same way each molecule of the anion when liberated, communicates to the anode a charge of negative electricity, the numerical magnitude of which is the same as that of the positive charge due to a molecule of a cation, but
P7239 If, instead of a single molecule, we consider an assemblage of molecules, constituting an electrochemical equivalent of the ion, then the total charge of all the molecules is, as we have seen, one
P7240 unit of electricity, positive or negative.
P7241 260.] We do not as yet know how many molecules there are

P7243 theory of chemistry, which is corroborated by many physical con siderations, supposes that the number of molecules in an elec
P7244 trochemical equivalent is the same for all substances. We may
P7245 therefore, in molecular speculations, assume that the number of molecules in an electrochemical equivalent is N, a number unknown at present, but which we may hereafter find means to determine *.
P7246 Each molecule, therefore, on being liberated from the state of
P7247 combination, parts with a charge whose magnitude is -~ , and is
P7248 positive for the cation and negative for the anion. This definite quantity of electricity we shall call the molecular charge. If it
P7249 were known it would be the most natural unit of electricity. Hitherto we have only increased the precision of our ideas by
P7250 exercising our imagination in tracing the electrification of molecules and the discharge of that electrification.
P7251 The liberation of the ions and the passage of positive electricity from the anode and into the cathode are simultaneous facts. The

P7293 equivalents of hydrogen. It would at first sight seem as if, according to the old theory

P7312 The dynamical theory of gases leads us to suppose that in perfect gases equal volumes always contain an equal number of molecules, and that the principal part of the specific heat, that, namely, which depends on the motion of agitation of the molecules among each other, is the same for equal numbers of molecules of all gases. Hence we are led to prefer a chemical system in which equal volumes of oxygen and of hydrogen are regarded as equivalent, and in which water is regarded as a compound of two equivalents of hydrogen and one of oxygen, and therefore probably not capable
P7313 of direct electrolysis. 262.] DYNAMICAL THEORY. 315
P7314 While electrolysis fully establishes the close relationship between electrical phenomena and those of chemical combination, the fact that every chemical compound is not an electrolyte shews that chemical combination is a process of a higher order of complexity than any purely electrical phenomenon. Thus the combinations of the metals with each other, though they are good conductors, and
P7315 their components stand at different points of the scale of electri
P7316 fication by contact, are not, even when in a fluid state,, decomposed by the current. Most of the combinations of the substances which act as anions are not conductors, and therefore are not electrolytes. Besides these we have many compounds, containing the same com ponents as electrolytes, but not in equivalent proportions, and these are also non-conductors, and therefore not electrolytes.
