P7318 262.] Consider any voltaic circuit consisting partly of a battery, partly of a wire, and partly of an electrolytic cell.
P7319 During the passage of unit of electricity through any section of the circuit, one electrochemical equivalent of each of the substances in the cells, whether voltaic or electrolytic, is electrolysed.
P7320 The amount of mechanical energy equivalent to any given chemical process can be ascertained by converting the whole energy due to the process into heat, and then expressing the heat in dynamical measure by multiplying the number of thermal units by
P7321 Joule s mechanical equivalent of heat.
P7322 Where this direct method is not applicable, if we can estimate the heat given out by the substances taken first in the state before
P7323 the process and then in the state after the process during their reduction to a final state, which is the same in both cases, then the thermal equivalent of the process is the difference of the two quan
P7324 In the case in which the chemical action maintains a voltaic
P7325 circuit, Joule found that the heat developed in the voltaic cells is less than that due to the chemical process within the cell, and that the remainder of the heat is developed in the connecting wire, or,
P7326 when there is an electromagnetic engine in the circuit, part of the heat may be accounted for by the mechanical work of the engine.
P7327 For instance, if the electrodes of the voltaic cell are first con nected by a short thick wire, and afterwards by a long thin wire, the heat developed in the cell for each grain of zinc dissolved is greater in the first case than the second, but the heat developed 316 ELECTROLYSIS.
P7328 in the wire is greater in the second case than in the first. The sum of the heat developed in the cell and in the wire for each grain of zinc dissolved is the same in both cases. This has been esta
P7329 blished by Joule by direct experiment. The ratio of the heat generated in the cell to that generated
P7330 in the wire is that of the resistance of the cell to that of the wire,
P7331 so that if the wire were made of sufficient resistance nearly the whole of the heat would be generated in the wire, and if it were made of sufficient conducting power nearly the whole of the heat woula be generated in the cell.
P7332 Let the wire be made so as to have great resistance, then the
P7333 heat generated in it is equal in dynamical measure to the product of the quantity of electricity which is transmitted, multiplied by the electromotive force under which it is made to pass through the wire.
P7334 263.] Now during the time in which an electrochemical equi
P7335 valent of the substance in the cell undergoes the chemical process which gives rise to the current, one unit of electricity passes through the wire. Hence, the heat developed by the passage of one unit of electricity is in this case measured by the electro
P7336 motive force. But this heat is that which one electrochemical
P7337 equivalent of the substance generates, whether in the cell or in the wire, while undergoing the given chemical process.
P7338 Hence the following important theorem, first proved by Thomson (Phil. Mag. Dec. 1851):
P7339 The electromotive force of an electrochemical apparatus is in absolute measure equal to the mechanical equivalent of the chemical action on one electrochemical equivalent of the substance/
P7340 The thermal equivalents of many chemical actions have been
P7341 determined by Andrews, Hess, Favre and Silbermann, &c., and from these their mechanical equivalents can be deduced by multiplication by the mechanical equivalent of heat.
P7342 This theorem not only enables us to calculate from purely thermal data the electromotive force of different voltaic arrangements, and the electromotive force required to effect electrolysis in different cases, but affords the means of actually measuring chemical affinity.
P7343 It has long been known that chemical affinity, or the tendency
P7344 which exists towards the going on of a certain chemical change, is stronger in some cases than in others, but no proper measure of this tendency could be made till it was shewn that this tendency in certain cases is exactly equivalent to a certain electromotive 263-] CALCULATION OF ELECTROMOTIVE FORCE. 317
P7345 force, and can therefore be measured according to the very same principles used in the measurement of electromotive forces.
P7346 Chemical affinity being therefore, in certain cases, reduced to the form of a measurable quantity, the whole theory of chemical processes, of the rate at which they go on, of the displacement of
P7347 one substance by another, &c., becomes much more intelligible than when chemical affinity was regarded as a quality sui generis, and
P7348 irreducible to numerical measurement.

P7354 When the products of electrolysis are liquid or solid the quantity

P7362 264.] WHEN an electric current is passed through an electrolyte
P7363 bounded by metal electrodes, the accumulation of the ions at the electrodes produces the phenomenon called Polarization, which consists in an electromotive force acting in the opposite direction to the current, and producing an apparent increase of the resistance.
P7364 When a continuous current is employed, the resistance appears
P7365 to increase rapidly from the commencement of the current, and
P7366 at last reaches a value nearly constant. If the form of the vessel
P7367 in which the electrolyte is contained is changed, the resistance is altered in the same way as a similar change of form of a metallic conductor would alter its resistance, but an additional apparent resistance, depending on the nature of the electrodes, has always to be added to the true resistance of the electrolyte.
P7368 265.] These phenomena have led some to suppose that there is a finite electromotive force required for a current to pass through an electrolyte. It has been shewn, however, by the researches of Lenz, Neumann, Beetz, Wiedemann*, Paalzowf, and recently by
P7369 those of MM. F. Kohlrausch and W. A. NippoldtJ, that the con duction in the electrolyte itself obeys Ohm s Law with the same
P7370 precision as in metallic conductors, and that the apparent resistance at the bounding surface of the electrolyte and the electrodes is entirely due to polarization.
P7371 266.] The phenomenon called polarization manifests itself in the case of a continuous current by a diminution in the current,
P7372 indicating a force opposed to the current. Resistance is also per
P7373 ceived as a force opposed to the current, but we can distinguish
P7374 * Galvanismus, bd. i. t Berlin Monatsbericht, July, 1868.
P7375 Pogg, Ann. bd. cxxxviii. s. 286 (October, 1869). 267.] DISTINGUISHED FROM RESISTANCE. 319
P7376 between the two phenomena by instantaneously removing or re
P7377 versing the electromotive force.
P7378 The resisting force is always opposite in direction to the current, and the external electromotive force required to overcome it is proportional to the strength of the current, and changes its direc tion when the direction of the current is changed. If the external electromotive force becomes zero the current simply stops.
P7379 The electromotive force due to polarization, on the other hand, is in a fixed direction, opposed to the current which produced it. If the electromotive force which produced the current is removed,
P7380 the polarization produces a current in the opposite direction.
P7381 The difference between the two phenomena may be compared
P7382 with the difference between forcing a current of water through a long capillary tube, and forcing water through a tube of moderate
P7383 length up into a cistern. In the first case if we remove the pressure which produces the flow the current will simply stop. In the second case, if we remove the pressure the water will begin to flow down again from the cistern.
P7384 To make the mechanical illustration more complete, we have only to suppose that the cistern is of moderate depth, so that when a certain amount of water is raised into it, it begins to overflow.
P7385 This will represent the fact that the total electromotive force due
P7386 to polarization has a maximum limit.
P7387 267.] The cause of polarization appears to be the existence at
P7388 the electrodes of the products of the electrolytic decomposition of
P7389 the fluid between them. The surfaces of the electrodes are thus
P7390 rendered electrically different, and an electromotive force between them is called into action, the direction of which is opposite to that of the current which caused the polarization.
P7391 The ions, which by their presence at the electrodes produce the phenomena of polarization, are not in a perfectly free state, but are in a condition in which they adhere to the surface of the electrodes with considerable force.
P7392 The electromotive force due to polarization depends upon the density with which the electrode is covered with the ion, but it
P7393 is not proportional to this density, for the electromotive force does not increase so rapidly as this density.
P7394 This deposit of the ion is constantly tending to become free, and either to diffuse into the liquid, to escape as a gas, or to be
P7395 precipitated as a solid.
P7396 The rate of this dissipation of the polarization is exceedingly 320 ELECTROLYTIC POLARIZATION. [268.
P7397 small for slight degrees of polarization, and exceedingly rapid near
P7398 the limiting value of polarization.

P7464 271.] Hence we may compare the action of this apparatus, which
P7465 is a form of Ritter s Secondary Pile, with that of a Leyden jar. Both the secondary pile and the Leyden jar are capable of being
P7466 charged with a certain amount of electricity, and of being after wards discharged. During the discharge a quantity of electricity nearly equal to the charge passes in the opposite direction. The difference between the charge and the discharge arises partly from dissipation, a process which in the case of small charges is very slow, but which, when the charge exceeds a certain limit, becomes exceedingly rapid. Another part of the difference between the charge and the discharge arises from the fact that after the electrodes have been connected for a time sufficient to produce an apparently
P7467 complete discharge, so that the current has completely disappeared,
P7468 if we separate the electrodes for a time, and afterwards connect them, we obtain a second discharge in the same direction as the
P7469 original discharge. This is called the residual discharge, and is a phenomenon of the Leyden jar as well as of the secondary pile.
P7470 The secondary pile may therefore be compared in several respects

P7476 The capacity of the jar depends on the area of the opposed surfaces, on the distance between them, and on the nature of the substance between them, but not on the nature of the metallic surfaces themselves. The capacity of the secondary pile depends on the area of the surfaces of the electrodes, but not on the distance between them, and it depends on the nature of the surface of the electrodes, as well as on that of the fluid between them. P7482 On the other hand, the superficial density of the charge in the
P7483 secondary pile is immensely greater than the utmost superficial density of the charge which can be accumulated on the surfaces of a Leyden jar, insomuch that Mr. C. F. Varley *, in describing the construction of a condenser of great capacity, recommends a series of gold or platinum plates immersed in dilute acid as prefer able in point of cheapness to induction plates of tinfoil separated by insulating material.
P7484 The form in which the energy of a Leyden jar is stored up is the state of constraint of the dielectric between the conducting surfaces, a state which I have already described under the name of electric polarization, pointing out those phenomena attending this state which are at present known, and indicating the im perfect state of our knowledge of what really takes place. See
P7485 The form in which the energy of the secondary pile is stored up is the chemical condition of the material stratum at the surface of the electrodes, consisting of the ions of the electrolyte and the substance of the electrodes in a relation varying from chemical combination to superficial condensation, mechanical adherence, or
P7486 simple juxtaposition.
P7487 The seat of this energy is close to the surfaces of the electrodes,
P7488 * Specification of C. F. Varley, Electric Telegraphs, &c., Jan. 1860.
P7489 Y2 324 ELECTROLYTIC POLARIZATION.
P7490 and not throughout the substance of the electrolyte, and the form
P7491 in which it exists may be called electrolytic polarization.
P7492 After studying- the secondary pile in connexion with the Leyden
P7493 jar, the student should again compare the voltaic battery with
P7494 some form of the electrical machine, such as that described in
P7495 Mr. Varley has * found that the capacity of one square
P7496 inch is from 175 to 542 microfarads and upwards for platinum
P7497 plates in dilute sulphuric acid, and that the capacity increases with
P7498 the electromotive force, being about 175 for 0.02 of a Daniell s
P7499 cell, and 542 for 1.6 Daniell s cells.
P7500 But the comparison between the Leyden jar and the secondary
P7501 pile may be carried still farther, as in the following experiment, due to Bufff. It is only when the glass of the jar is cold that it is capable of retaining a charge. At a temperature below 100C
P7502 the glass becomes a conductor. If a test-tube containing mercury is placed in a vessel of mercury, and if a pair of electrodes are
P7503 connected, one with the inner and the other with the outer portion
P7504 of mercury, the arrangement constitutes a Leyden jar which will
P7505 hold a charge at ordinary temperatures. If the electrodes are con
P7506 nected with those of a voltaic battery, no current will pass as long as the glass is cold, but if the apparatus is gradually heated a current will begin to pass, and will increase rapidly in intensity as
P7507 the temperature rises, though the glass remains apparently as hard
P7508 This current is manifestly electrolytic, for if the electrodes are disconnected from the battery, and connected with a galvanometer, a considerable reverse current passes, due to polarization of the surfaces of the glass.
P7509 If, while the battery is in action the apparatus is cooled, the
P7510 current is stopped by the cold glass as before, but the polarization
P7511 of the surfaces remains. The mercury may be removed, the surfaces may be washed with nitric acid and with water, and fresh mercury
P7512 introduced. If the apparatus is then heated, the current of polar
P7513 ization appears as soon as the glass is sufficiently warm to conduct it.
P7514 We may therefore regard glass at 100C, though apparently a
P7515 solid body, as an electrolyte, and there is considerable reason to believe that in most instances in which a dielectric has a
P7516 slight degree of conductivity the conduction is electrolytic. The
P7517 * Proc. R. 8., Jan. 12, 1871.
P7518 t Annalen der Chemie und Pharmacie, bd. xc. 257 (1854). 272.] CONSTANT VOLTAIC ELEMENTS. 325
P7519 existence of polarization may be regarded as conclusive evidence of
P7520 electrolysis, and if the conductivity of a substance increases as the
P7521 temperature rises, we have good grounds for suspecting that it is
P7522 On Constant Voltaic Elements.
P7523 272.] When a series of experiments is made with a voltaic
P7524 battery in which polarization occurs, the polarization diminishes during the time that the current is not flowing, so that when it begins to flow again the current is stronger than after it has flowed for some time. If, on the other hand, the resistance of the
P7525 circuit is diminished by allowing the current to flow through a short shunt, then, when the current is again made to flow through the ordinary circuit, it is at first weaker than its normal strength on account of the great polarization produced by the use of the
P7526 To get rid of these irregularities in the current, which are exceedingly troublesome in experiments involving exact measure
P7527 ments, it is necessary to get rid of the polarization, or at least
P7528 to reduce it as much as possible. It does not appear that there is much polarization at the surface
P7529 of the zinc plate when immersed in a solution of sulphate of zinc or in dilute sulphuric acid. The principal seat of polarization is
P7530 at the surface of the negative metal. When the fluid in which
P7531 the negative metal is immersed is dilute sulphuric acid, it is seen to become covered with bubbles of hydrogen gas, arising from the
P7532 electrolytic decomposition of the fluid. Of course these bubbles, by preventing the fluid from touching the metal, diminish the surface of contact and increase the resistance of the circuit. But
P7533 besides the visible bubbles it is certain that there is a thin coating of hydrogen, probably not in a free state, adhering to the metal,
P7534 and as we have seen that this coating is able to produce an elec tromotive force in the reverse direction, it must necessarily diminish
P7535 the electromotive force of the battery. Various plans have been adopted to get rid of this coating of
P7536 hydrogen. It may be diminished to some extent by mechanical
P7537 means, such as stirring the liquid, or rubbing the surface of the
P7538 negative plate. In Smee s battery the negative plates are vertical, and covered with finely divided platinum from which the bubbles of hydrogen easily escape, and in their ascent produce a current of liquid which helps to brush off other bubbles as they are formed.
P7539 A far more efficacious method, however, is to employ chemical 326 ELECTROLYTIC POLARIZATION.
P7540 means. These are of two kinds. In the batteries of Grove and
P7541 Bunsen the negative plate is immersed in a fluid rich in oxygen, and the hydrogen, instead of forming a coating on the plate, combines with this substance. In Grove s battery the plate is of platinum immersed in strong nitric acid. In Bunsen s first battery it is of carbon in the same acid. Chromic acid is also used for the same purpose, and has the advantage of being free from the acid fumes produced by the reduction of nitric acid.
P7542 A different mode of getting rid of the hydrogen is by using
P7543 copper as the negative metal, and covering the surface with a coat of oxide. This, however, rapidly disappears when it is used as
P7544 the negative electrode. To renew it Joule has proposed to make the copper plates in the form of disks, half immersed in the liquid, and to rotate them slowly, so that the air may act on the parts
P7545 exposed to it in turn.
P7546 The other method is by using as the liquid an electrolyte, the cation of which is a metal highly negative to zinc.
P7547 In Daniell s battery a copper plate is immersed in a saturated
P7548 solution of sulphate of copper. When the current flows through
P7549 the solution from the zinc to the copper no hydrogen appears on
P7550 the copper plate, but copper is deposited on it. When the solution
P7551 is saturated, and the current is not too strong, the copper appears
P7552 to act as a true cation, the anion S O4 travelling towards the zinc. When these conditions are not fulfilled hydrogen is evolved at
P7553 the cathode, but immediately acts on the solution, throwing down
P7554 copper, and uniting with S O4 to form oil of vitriol. When this
P7555 is the case, the sulphate of copper next the copper plate is replaced
P7556 by oil of vitriol, the liquid becomes colourless, arid polarization by hydrogen gas again takes place. The copper deposited in this way is of a looser and more friable structure than that deposited by true
P7557 To ensure that the liquid in contact with the copper shall be saturated with sulphate of copper, crystals of this substance must be placed in the liquid close to the copper, so that when the solution is made weak by the deposition of the copper, more of the crystals may be dissolved.
P7558 We have seen that it is necessary that the liquid next the copper
P7559 should be saturated with sulphate of copper. It is still more necessary that the liquid in which the zinc is immersed should be free from sulphate of copper. If any of this salt makes its way to the surface of the zinc it is reduced, and copper is deposited 272.] THOMSON S FORM OF DANIELL S CELL. 327
P7560 on the zinc. The zinc, copper, and fluid then form a little circuit
P7561 in which rapid electrolytic action goes on, and the zinc is eaten
P7562 away by an action which contributes nothing to the useful effect
P7563 To prevent this, the zinc is immersed either in dilute sulphuric acid or in a solution of sulphate of zinc, and to prevent the solution of sulphate of copper from mixing with this liquid, the two liquids are separated by a division consisting of bladder or porous earthen ware, which allows electrolysis to take place through it, but
P7564 effectually prevents mixture of the fluids by visible currents. The In some batteries sawdust is used to prevent currents.
P7565 experiments of Graham, however, shew that the process of diffusion goes on nearly as rapidly when two liquids are separated by a division of this kind as when they are in direct contact, provided
P7566 there are no visible currents, and it is probable that if a septum
P7567 is employed which diminishes the diffusion, it will increase in exactly the same ratio the resistance of the element, because elec trolytic conduction is a process the mathematical laws of which have the same form as those of diffusion, and whatever interferes
P7568 with one must interfere equally with the other. The only differ
P7569 ence is that diffusion is always going on, while the current flows
P7570 only when the battery is in action. In all forms of DanielFs battery the final result is that the
P7571 sulphate of copper finds its way to the zinc and spoils the battery.
P7572 To retard this result indefinitely, Sir W. Thomson * has constructed
P7573 Darnell s battery in the following form.
P7574 LEVEL Cf SIPHON ZWSO+ 1 Cu SO*
P7575 In each cell the copper plate is placed horizontally at the bottom
P7576 * Proc. R. 8., Jan. 19, 1871. 328 ELECTROLYTIC POLARIZATION. [272.
P7577 and a saturated solution of sulphate of zinc is poured over it. The zinc is in the form of a grating and is placed horizontally near the
P7578 surface of the solution. A glass tube is placed vertically in the
P7579 solution with its lower end just above the surface of the copper plate. Crystals of sulphate of copper are dropped down this tube, and, dissolving in the liquid, form a solution of greater density than that of sulphate of zinc alone, so that it cannot get to the zinc except by diffusion. To retard this process of diffusion, a siphon, consisting of a glass tube stuffed with cotton wick, is
P7580 placed with one extremity midway between the zinc and copper, and the other in a vessel outside the cell, so that the liquid is very slowly drawn off near the middle of its depth. To supply its place, water, or a weak solution of sulphate of zinc, is added above when required. In this way the greater part of the sulphate of copper rising through the liquid by diffusion is drawn off by the siphon before it reaches the zinc, and the zinc is surrounded by liquid nearly free from sulphate of copper, and having a very slow downward motion in the cell, which still further retards the upward motion of the sulphate of copper. During the action of the battery
P7581 copper is deposited on the copper plate, and SO4 travels slowly
P7582 through the liquid to the zinc with which it combines, forming sulphate of zinc. Thus the liquid at the bottom becomes less dense by the deposition of the copper, and the liquid at the top becomes more dense by the addition of the zinc. To prevent this action from changing the order of density of the strata, and so producing instability and visible currents in the vessel, care must be taken to keep the tube well supplied with crystals of sulphate of copper, and to feed the cell above with a solution of sulphate of zinc suffi ciently dilute to be lighter than any other stratum of the liquid
P7583 Daniel! s battery is by no means the most powerful in common use. The electromotive force of Grove s cell is 192,000,000, of DanielPs 107,900,000 and that of Bunsen s 188,000,000.
P7584 The resistance of Daniell s cell is in general greater than that of Grove s or Bunsen s of the same size.
P7585 These defects, however, are more than counterbalanced in all cases where exact measurements are required, by the fact that Daniell s cell exceeds every other known arrangement in constancy of electromotive force. It has also the advantage of continuing in working order for a long time, and of emitting no gas. CHAPTER VI.

P7654 Now we know that in the electrostatic system of measurement
P7655 the ratio of a quantity of electricity to the potential of the con ductor on which it is spread is the capacity of the conductor, and
P7656 is measured by a line. If the conductor is a sphere placed in an unlimited field, this line is the radius of the sphere. The ratio of a quantity of electricity to an electromotive force is therefore a line, but the ratio of a quantity of electricity to a current is the time during which the current flows to transmit that quantity. Hence the ratio of a current to an electromotive force is that of a
P7657 line to a time, or in other words, it is a velocity.

P7911 The corresponding equation in Hydrodynamics is called the Equation of Continuity. The continuity which it expresses is
P7912 the continuity of existence, that is, the fact that a material sub stance cannot leave one part of space and arrive at another, without going through the space between. It cannot simply vanish in the 295-] EQUATION OF CONTINUITY. 343
P7913 one place and appear in the other, but it must travel along a con
P7914 tinuous path, so that if a closed surface be drawn, including the one place and excluding the other, a material substance in passing from the one place to the other must go through the closed surface. The most general form of the equation in hydrodynamics is
P7915 ~W d(pu) d(pv) d(pw) dp , }
P7916 where p signifies the ratio of the quantity of the substance to the
P7917 volume it occupies, that volume being in this case the differential
P7918 element of volume, and (pu}} (pv), and (pw) signify the ratio of the
P7919 quantity of the substance which crosses an element of area in unit
P7920 of time to that area, these areas being normal to the axes of #, ^, and
P7921 z respectively. Thus understood, the equation is applicable to any
P7922 material substance, solid or fluid, whether the motion be continuous
P7923 or discontinuous, provided the existence of the parts of that sub
P7924 stance is continuous. If anything, though not a substance, is subject to the condition of continuous existence in time and space, the equation will express this condition. In other parts of Physical Science, as, for instance, in the theory of electric and magnetic
P7925 quantities, equations of a similar form occur. We shall call such
P7926 equations equations of continuity to indicate their form, though
P7927 we may not attribute to these quantities the properties of matter,
P7928 or even continuous existence in time and space.
P7929 The equation (17), which we have arrived at in the case of
P7930 electric currents, is identical with (18) if we make p = 1, that is,
P7931 if we suppose the substance homogeneous and incompressible. The
P7932 equation, in the case of fluids, may also be established by either
P7933 of the modes of proof given in treatises on Hydrodynamics. In one of these we trace the course and the deformation of a certain
P7934 element of the fluid as it moves along. In the other, we fix our attention on an element of space, and take account of all that enters or leaves it. The former of these methods cannot be applied to electric currents, as we do not know the velocity with which the electricity passes through the body, or even whether it moves in the positive or the negative direction of the current. All that we know is the algebraical value of the quantity which crosses unit
P7935 of area in unit of time, a quantity corresponding to (pu) in the
P7936 equation (18). We have no means of ascertaining the value of
P7937 either of the factors p or u, and therefore we cannot follow a par
P7938 ticular portion of electricity in its course through the body. The other method of investigation, in which we consider what passes 344 CONDUCTION IN THREE DIMENSIONS. [296.
P7939 through the walls of an element of volume, is applicable to electric currents, and is perhaps preferable in point of form to that which
P7940 we have given, but as it may be found in any treatise on Hydro dynamics we need not repeat it here.

P8090 305.] As this proposition is of great importance in the theory of electricity, it may be useful to present the following proof of the most general case in a form free from analytical operations.
P8091 Let us consider the propagation of electricity through a conductor
P8092 of any form, homogeneous* or heterogeneous.
P8093 (1) If we draw a line along the path and in the direction of the electric current, the line must pass from places of high potential
P8094 to places of low potential.
P8095 (2) If the potential at every point of the system be altered in
P8096 a given uniform ratio, the currents will be altered in the same ratio,
P8097 according to Ohm s Law.
P8098 (3) If a certain distribution of potential gives rise to a certain distribution of currents, and a second distribution of potential gives rise to a second distribution of currents, then a third distribution in
P8099 which the potential is the sum or difference of those in the first
P8100 and second will give rise to a third distribution of currents, such
P8101 that the total current passing through a given finite surface in the
P8102 third case is the sum or difference of the currents passing through
P8103 it in the first and second cases. For, by Ohm s Law, the additional
P8104 current due to an alteration of potentials is independent of the original current due to the original distribution of potentials.
P8105 (4) If the potential is constant over the whole of a closed surface, 352 RESISTANCE AND CONDUCTIVITY. [3O5
P8106 and if there are no electrodes or intrinsic electromotive forces
P8107 within it, then there will be no currents within the closed surface, and the potential at any point within it will be equal to that at the
P8108 If there are currents within the closed surface they must either be closed curves, or they must begin and end either within the
P8109 closed surface or at the surface itself.
P8110 But since the current must pass from places of high to places of low potential, it cannot flow in a closed curve. the current surface Since there are no electrodes within the
P8111 cannot begin or end within the closed surface, and since the potential at all points of the surface is the same, there can be no current along lines passing from one point of the surface to
P8112 Hence there are no currents within the surface, and therefore there can be no difference of potential, as such a difference would produce currents, and therefore the potential within the closed surface is everywhere the same as at the surface.
P8113 (5) If there is no electric current through any part of a closed surface, and no electrodes or intrinsic electromotive forces within the surface, there will be no currents within the surface, and the
P8114 potential will be uniform.
P8115 We have seen that the currents cannot form closed curves, or
P8116 begin or terminate within the surface, and since by the hypothesis they do not pass through the surface, there can be no currents, and
P8117 therefore the potential is constant. (6) If the potential is uniform over part of a closed surface, and
P8118 if there is no current through the remainder of the surface, the potential within the surface will be uniform for the same reasons.

P8130 306.] The conductor here considered has its surface divided into three portions. Over one of these portions the potential is main tained at a constant value. Over a second portion the potential has a constant value different from the first. The whole of the remainder
P8131 of the surface is impervious to electricity. &quot;We may suppose the
P8132 conditions of the first and second portions to be fulfilled by applying to the conductor two electrodes of perfectly conducting material, and that of the remainder of the surface by coating it with per
P8133 fectly non-conducting material.
P8134 Under these circumstances the current in every part of the conductor is simply proportional to the difference between the
P8135 potentials of the electrodes. Calling this difference the electro motive force, the total current from the one electrode to the other
P8136 is the product of the electromotive force by the conductivity of the conductor as a whole, and the resistance of the conductor is the
P8137 reciprocal of the conductivity.
P8138 It is only when a conductor is approximately in the circumstances
P8139 above defined that it can be said to have a definite resistance, or
P8140 conductivity as a whole. A resistance coil, consisting of a thin
P8141 wire terminating in large masses of copper, approximately satisfies these conditions, for the potential in the massive electrodes is nearly constant, and any differences of potential in different points of the
P8142 same electrode may be neglected in comparison with the difference
P8143 of the potentials of the two electrodes.
P8144 A very useful method of calculating the resistance of such con
P8145 ductors has been given, so far as I know, for the first time, by
P8146 the Hon. J. W. Strutt, in a paper on the Theory of Resonance*.
P8147 It is founded on the following considerations. If the specific resistance of any portion of the conductor be changed, that of the remainder being unchanged, the resistance of
P8148 * Phil. Trans., 1871, p. 77. See Art. 102.
P8149 VOL. I. Aa 354 RESISTANCE AND CONDUCTIVITY. [306.
P8150 the whole conductor will be increased if that of the portion is increased, and diminished if that of the portion be diminished.
P8151 This principle may be regarded as self-evident, but it may easily
P8152 be shewn that the value of the expression for the resistance of a system of conductors between two points selected as electrodes, increases as the resistance of each member of the system in
P8153 It follows from this that if a surface of any form be described in the substance of the conductor, and if we further suppose this surface to be an infinitely thin sheet of a perfectly conducting substance, the resistance of the conductor as a whole will be diminished unless the surface is one of the equipotential surfaces in the natural state of the conductor, in which case no effect will
P8154 be produced by making it a perfect conductor, as it is already in
P8155 electrical equilibrium.
P8156 If therefore we draw within the conductor a series of surfaces, the first of which coincides with the first electrode, and the last with the second, while the intermediate surfaces are bounded by the non-conducting surface and do not intersect each other, and if we suppose each of these surfaces to be an infinitely thin sheet of perfectly conducting matter, we shall have obtained a system the resistance of which is certainly not greater than that of the original conductor, and is equal to it only when the surfaces we
P8157 have chosen are the natural equipotential surfaces. To calculate the resistance of the artificial system is an operation
P8158 of much less difficulty than the original problem. For the resist ance of the whole is the sum of the resistances of all the strata
P8159 contained between the consecutive surfaces, and the resistance of
P8160 each stratum can be found thus :

P8279 310.] THERE are two conditions which the distribution of currents must fulfil in general, the condition that the potential must be continuous, and the condition of continuity of the electric currents.
P8280 At the surface of separation between two media the first of these conditions requires that the potentials at two points on opposite
P8281 sides of the surface, but infinitely near each other, shall be equal. The potentials are here understood to be measured by an elec trometer put in connexion with the given point by means of an electrode of a given metal. If the potentials are measured by the method described in Arts. 222,, 246, where the electrode terminates
P8282 in a cavity of the conductor filled with air, then the potentials at
P8283 contiguous points of different metals measured in this way will differ by a quantity depending on the temperature and on the nature of the two metals.
P8284 The other condition at the surface is that the current through any element of the surface is the same when measured in either medium.

P8736 It by no means follows that every substance which exhibits this
P8737 phenomenon is so composed, for it may indicate a new kind of electric polarization of which a homogeneous substance may be capable, and this in some cases may perhaps resemble electro chemical polarization much more than dielectric polarization.
P8738 The object of the investigation is merely to point out the true mathematical character of the so-called electric absorption, and to shew how fundamentally it differs from the phenomena of heat which seem at first sight analogous.

P8844 The charges on the opposite sides of the dielectric remain in
P8845 sulated, but their difference of potential diminishes.
P8846 In fact, the difference of pressure on the two sides of the piston
P8847 sinks from 4# to 2 a during the passage of the fluid through Q.
P8848 If we now shut the stopcock Q and allow the piston P to move freely, it will come to equilibrium at a point P2 , and the discharge
P8849 will be apparently only half of the charge.
P8850 The level of the mercury in A and B will be ^a above its original level, and the level in the tubes C and D will be \a
P8851 below its original level. This is indicated by the levels A29 -Z?2 ,
P8852 If the piston is now fixed and the stopcock opened, mercury will
P8853 flow from B to C till the level in the two tubes is again at BQ and C . There will then be a difference of pressure = a on the two
P8854 sides of the piston P. If the stopcock is then closed and the piston
P8855 P left free to move, it will again come to equilibrium at a point P3 , half way between P2 and P . This corresponds to the residual
P8856 charge which is observed when a charged dielectric is first dis
P8857 charged and then left to itself. It gradually recovers part of its
P8858 charge, and if this is again discharged a third charge is formed, the
P8859 successive charges diminishing in quantity. In the case of the illustrative experiment each charge is half of the preceding, and the 334-] HYDROSTATICAL ILLUSTRATION. 387
P8860 discharges, which are J, ^, &c. of the original charge, form a series
P8861 whose sum is equal to the original charge. If, instead of opening and closing the stopcock, we had allowed it
P8862 to remain nearly, but not quite, closed during the whole experiment,
P8863 we should have had a case resembling that of the electrification of a dielectric which is a perfect insulator and yet exhibits the phe nomenon called * electric absorption/
P8864 To represent the case in which there is true conduction through the dielectric we must either make the piston leaky, or we must
P8865 establish a communication between the top of the tube A and the
P8866 In this way we may construct a mechanical illustration of the
P8867 properties of a dielectric of any kind, in which the two electricities are represented by two real fluids, and the electric potential is represented by fluid pressure. Charge and discharge are repre sented by the motion of the piston P, and electromotive force by the resultant force on the piston.
P8868 THE MEASUREMENT OF ELECTRIC RESISTANCE.
P8869 335.] IN the present state of electrical science, the determination
P8870 of the electric resistance of a conductor may be considered as the
P8871 cardinal operation in electricity, in the same sense that the deter mination of weight is the cardinal operation in chemistry.
P8872 The reason of this is that the determination in absolute measure
P8873 of other electrical magnitudes, such as quantities of electricity,
P8874 electromotive forces, currents, &c., requires in each case a com
P8875 plicated series of operations, involving- generally observations of
P8876 time, measurements of distances, and determinations of moments of inertia, and these operations, or at least some of them, must be repeated for every new determination, because it is impossible
P8877 to preserve a unit of electricity, or of electromotive force, or of current, in an unchangeable state, so as to be available for direct
P8878 But when the electric resistance of a properly shaped conductor
P8879 of a properly chosen material has been once determined, it is found that it always remains the same for the same temperature, so that
P8880 the conductor may be used as a standard of resistance, with which
P8881 that of other conductors can be compared, and the comparison of two resistances is an operation which admits of extreme accuracy.
P8882 When the unit of electrical resistance has been fixed on, material
P8883 copies of this unit, in the form of Resistance Coils, are prepared for the use of electricians, so that in every part of the world
P8884 electrical resistances may be expressed in terms of the same unit.
P8885 These unit resistance coils are at present the only examples of material electric standards which can be preserved, copied, and used for the purpose of measurement. Measures of electrical capacity, which are also of great importance, are still defective, on account of the disturbing influence of electric absorption.
P8886 336.] The unit of resistance may be an entirely arbitrary one,
P8887 as in the case of Jacobins Etalon, which was a certain copper wire of 22.4932 grammes weight, 7.61975 metres length, and 0.667 339-] STANDARDS OF RESISTANCE. 389
P8888 millimetres diameter. Copies of this have been made by Leyser of Leipsig, and are to be found in different places.
P8889 According- to another method the unit may be defined as the
P8890 resistance of a portion of a definite substance of definite dimensions. Thus, Siemens unit is defined as the resistance of a column of
P8891 mercury of one metre long-, and one square millimetre section, at the temperature 0C.
P8892 337.] Finally, the unit may be defined with reference to the
P8893 electrostatic or the electromagnetic system of units. In practice the electromagnetic system is used in all telegraphic operations, and therefore the only systematic units actually in use are those
P8894 In the electromagnetic system, as we shall shew at the proper place, a resistance is a quantity homogeneous with a velocity, and may therefore be expressed as a velocity. See Art. 628.
P8895 338.] The first actual measurements on this system were made by Weber, who employed as his unit one millimetre per second.
P8896 Sir W. Thomson afterwards used one foot per second as a unit, but a large number of electricians have now agreed to use the
P8897 unit of the British Association, which professes to represent a
P8898 resistance which, expressed as a velocity, is ten millions of metres
P8899 per second. The magnitude of this unit is more convenient than that of Weber s unit, which is too small. It is sometimes referred to as the B.A. unit, but in order to connect it with the name of the discoverer of the laws of resistance, it is called the Ohm.
P8900 339.] To recollect its value in absolute measure it is useful to know that ten millions of metres is professedly the distance from the pole to the equator, measured along the meridian of Paris.
P8901 A body, therefore, which in one second travels along a meridian
P8902 from the pole to the equator would have a velocity which, on the
P8903 electromagnetic system, is professedly represented by an Ohm. I say professedly, because, if more accurate researches should
P8904 prove that the Ohm, as constructed from the British Association s
P8905 material standards, is not really represented by this velocity, elec tricians would not alter their standards, but would apply a cor
P8906 rection. In the same way the metre is professedly one ten-millionth of a certain quadrantal arc, but though this is found not to be
P8907 exactly true, the length of the metre has not been altered, but the dimensions of the earth are expressed by a less simple number.
P8908 According to the system of the British Association, the absolute
P8909 value of the unit is originally chosen so as to represent as nearly 390 MEASUREMENT OF RESISTANCE. [340.
P8910 as possible a quantity derived from the electromagnetic absolute

P8915 tributed over all parts of the world, so that it is not likely that
P8916 any difficulty will be found in obtaining copies of them if the

P8918 But such units as that of Siemens can without very great

P8946 The electrodes or ends of the coil must be such that no appre

P8948 For resistances of considerable magnitude it is sufficient that the electrodes should be made of stout copper wire or rod well amal gamated with mercury at the ends, and that the ends should be made to press on flat amalgamated copper surfaces placed in mercury
P8949 For very great resistances it is sufficient that the electrodes should be thick pieces of brass, and that the connexions should be made by inserting a wedge of brass or copper into the interval between them. This method is found very convenient.
P8950 The resistance coil itself consists of a wire well covered with
P8951 silk, the ends of which are soldered permanently to the elec
P8952 The coil must be so arranged that its temperature may be easily
P8953 observed. For this purpose the wire is coiled on a tube and
P8954 covered with another tube, so that it may be placed in a vessel of water, and that the water may have access to the inside and the
P8955 outside of the coil.
P8956 To avoid the electromagnetic effects of the current in the coil the wire is first doubled back on itself and then coiled on the tube,
P8957 so that at every part of the coil there are equal and opposite currents in the adjacent parts of the wire.
P8958 When it is desired to keep two coils at the same temperature the
P8959 wires are sometimes placed side by side and coiled up together. This method is especially useful when it is more important to secure equality of resistance than to know the absolute value of the resistance, as in the case of the equal arms of Wheatstone s
P8960 AVhen measurements of resistance were first attempted, a resist ance coil, consisting of an uncovered wire coiled in a spiral groove round a cylinder of insulating material, was much used. It was called a Rheostat. The accuracy with which it was found possible to compare resistances was soon found to be inconsistent with the use of any instrument in which the contacts are not more perfect than can be obtained in the rheostat. P8962 Resistance coils are generally made of those metals whose resist ance is greatest and which vary least with temperature. German silver fulfils these conditions very well, but some specimens are found to change their properties during the lapse of years. Hence for standard coils, several pure metals, and also an alloy of platinum and silver, have been employed, and the relative resistance of these during several years has been found constant up to the limits of modern accuracy.
P8963 342.] For very great resistances, such as several millions of Ohms, the wire must be either very long or very thin, and the construction of the coil is expensive and difficult. Hence tellurium and selenium have been proposed as materials for constructing
P8964 standards of great resistance. A very ingenious and easy method
P8965 of construction has been lately proposed by Phillips *. On a piece
P8966 of ebonite or ground glass a fine pencil-line is drawn. The ends of this filament of plumbago are connected to metallic electrodes, and the whole is then covered with insulating varnish. If it should be found that the resistance of such a pencil-line remains constant, this will be the best method of obtaining a resistance of several millions of Ohms.

P9221 sensibly uniform. The student may examine for himself the
P9222 investigation and the diagrams of Art. 193, where a current is introduced into a strip of metal with parallel sides through one of the sides, but soon becomes itself parallel to the sides.

P9414 ON THE ELECTRIC RESISTANCE OF SUBSTANCES.
P9415 359.] THERE are three classes in which we may place different
P9416 substances in relation to the passage of electricity through them. The first class contains all the metals and their alloys, some
P9417 sulphurets, and other compounds containing metals, to which we must add carbon in the form of gas-coke, and selenium in the
P9418 In all these substances conduction takes place without any decomposition, or alteration of the chemical nature of the substance, either in its interior or where the current enters and leaves the body. In all of them the resistance increases as the temperature
P9419 The second class consists of substances which are called electro lytes, because the current is associated with a decomposition of the substance into two components which appear at the electrodes. As a rule a substance is an electrolyte only when in the liquid form, though certain colloid substances, such as glass at 100C, which are apparently solid, are electrolytes. It would appear from
P9420 the experiments of Sir B. C. Brodie that certain gases are capable of electrolysis by a powerful electromotive force.
P9421 In all substances which conduct by electrolysis the resistance diminishes as the temperature rises.
P9422 The third class consists of substances the resistance of which is so great that it is only by the most refined methods that the passage of electricity through them can be detected. These are called Dielectrics. To this class belong a considerable number of solid bodies, many of which are electrolytes when melted, some liquids, such as turpentine, naphtha, melted paraffin, &c., and all gases and vapours. Carbon in the form of diamond, and selenium in the amorphous form, belong to this class.
P9423 The resistance of this class of bodies is enormous compared with that of the metals. It diminishes as the temperature rises. It 416 RESISTANCE. [360.
P9424 is difficult, on account of the great resistance of these substances,
P9425 to determine whether the feeble current which we can force through them is or is not associated with electrolysis.
P9426 On the Electric Resistance of Metals.
P9427 360.] There is no part of electrical research in which more numerous or more accurate experiments have been made than in
P9428 the determination of the resistance of metals. It is of the utmost
P9429 importance in the electric telegraph that the metal of which the wires are made should have the smallest attainable resistance.
P9430 Measurements of resistance must therefore be made before selecting
P9431 the materials. When any fault occurs in the line, its position is
P9432 at once ascertained by measurements of resistance, and these mea surements, in which so many persons are now employed, require the use of resistance coils, made of metal the electrical properties of which have been carefully tested.
P9433 The electrical properties of metals and their alloys have been
P9434 studied with great care by MM. Matthiessen, Vogt, and Hockin, and by MM. Siemens, who have done so much to introduce exact
P9435 electrical measurements into practical work.
P9436 It appears from the researches of Dr. Matthiessen, that the effect of temperature on the resistance is nearly the same for a considerable
P9437 number of the pure metals, the resistance at 100C being to that
P9438 at 0C in the ratio of 1.414 to 1, or of 1 to 70.7. For pure iron
P9439 the ratio is 1.645, and for pure thallium 1.458. The resistance of metals has been observed by Dr. C.W. Siemens*&quot;
P9440 through a much wider range of temperature, extending from the freezing point to 350C, and in certain cases to 1000C. He finds
P9441 that the resistance increases as the temperature rises, but that the
P9442 rate of increase diminishes as the temperature rises. The formula, which he finds to agree very closely both with the resistances observed at low temperatures by Dr. Matthiessen and with his own observations through a range of 1000C, is
P9443 where T is the absolute temperature reckoned from 273C, and
P9444 a, /3, y are constants. Thus, for
P9445 Platinum ...... r = 0.0393697^+ 0.0021640777 0.2413, Copper ......... r = 0.0265777^+0. 0031443^0.22751, Iron ............ r= 0. 0725457^ + 0.0038 13377 1.23971.
P9446 * Proc. R. S., April 27, 1871. 361.] OF METALS. 417
P9447 From data of this kind the temperature of a furnace may be
P9448 determined by means of an observation of the resistance of a platinum wire placed in the furnace.
P9449 Dr. Matthiessen found that when two metals are combined to
P9450 form an alloy, the resistance of the alloy is in most cases greater than that calculated from the resistance of the component metals and their proportions. In the case of alloys of gold and silver, the resistance of the alloy is greater than that of either pure gold or pure silver, and, within certain limiting proportions of the con stituents, it varies very little with a slight alteration of the pro portions. For this reason Dr. Matthiessen recommended an alloy of two parts by weight of gold and one of silver as a material for reproducing the unit of resistance.
P9451 The effect of change of temperature on electric resistance is generally less in alloys than in pure metals.
P9452 Hence ordinary resistance coils are made of German silver, on account of its great resistance and its small variation with tem
P9453 An alloy of silver and platinum is also used for standard coils.
P9454 361.] The electric resistance of some metals changes when the metal is annealed ; and until a wire has been tested by being
P9455 repeatedly raised to a high temperature without permanently altering its resistance, it cannot be relied on as a measure of
P9456 resistance. Some wires alter in resistance in course of time without
P9457 having been exposed to changes of temperature. Hence it is important to ascertain the specific resistance of mercury, a metal which being fluid has always the same molecular structure, and which can be easily purified by distillation and treatment with nitric acid. Great care has been bestowed in determining the
P9458 resistance of this metal by W. and C. F. Siemens, who introduced
P9459 it as a standard. Their researches have been supplemented by those of Matthiessen and Hockin.
P9460 The specific resistance of mercury was deduced from the observed resistance of a tube of length / containing a weight 10 of mercury, in the following manner.

P9481 On the Electric Resistance of Electrolytes.
P9482 363.] The measurement of the electric resistance of electrolytes
P9483 is rendered difficult on account of the polarization of the electrodes,
P9484 which causes the observed difference of potentials of the metallic
P9485 electrodes to be greater than the electromotive force which actually
P9486 produces the current. In certain This difficulty can be overcome in various ways.
P9487 cases we can get rid of polarization by using electrodes of proper
P9488 material, as, for instance, zinc electrodes in a solution of sulphate
P9489 of zinc. By making the surface of the electrodes very large com
P9490 pared with the section of the part of the electrolyte whose resist
P9491 ance is to be measured, and by using only currents of short duration in opposite directions alternately, we can make the measurements
P9492 before any considerable intensity of polarization has been excited
P9493 by the passage of the current. Finally, by making two different experiments, in one of which
P9494 the path of the current through the electrolyte is much longer than
P9495 in the other, and so adjusting the electromotive force that the

P9541 On the Electrical Resistance of Dielectrics.
P9542 366.] A great number of determinations of the resistance of
P9543 gutta-percha, and other materials used as insulating- media, in the manufacture of telegraphic cables, have been, made in order to
P9544 ascertain the value of these materials as insulators.
P9545 The tests are generally applied to the material after it has been used to cover the conducting wire, the wire being used as one electrode, and the water of a tank, in which the cable is plunged, as the other. Thus the current is made to pass through a cylin drical coating of the insulator of great area and small thickness.
P9546 It is found that when the electromotive force begins to act, the current, as indicated by the galvanometer, is by no means constant. The first effect is of course a transient current of considerable
P9547 intensity, the total quantity of electricity being that required to charge the surfaces of the insulator with the superficial distribution of electricity corresponding to the electromotive force. This first current therefore is a measure not of the conductivity, but of the capacity of the insulating layer.
P9548 But even after this current has been allowed to subside the
P9549 residual current is not constant, and does not indicate the true conductivity of the substance. It is found that the current con tinues to decrease for at least half an hour, so that a determination 422 RESISTANCE. [366.
P9550 of the resistance deduced from the current will give a greater value if a certain time is allowed to elapse than if taken immediately after applying the battery.
P9551 Thus, with Hooper s insulating material the apparent resistance at the end of ten minutes was four times, and at the end of nineteen hours twenty-three times that observed at the end of
P9552 one minute. When the direction of the electromotive force is
P9553 reversed, the resistance falls as low or lower than at first and then
P9554 These phenomena seem to be due to a condition of the gutta percha, which, for want of a better name, we may call polarization, and which we may compare on the one hand with that of a series of Leyden jars charged by cascade, and, on the other, with Ritter s
P9555 secondary pile, Art. 271.
P9556 If a number of Leyden jars of great capacity are connected in series by means of conductors of great resistance (such as wet cotton threads in the experiments of M. Gaugain), then an electro motive force acting on the series will produce a current, as indicated by a galvanometer, which will gradually diminish till the jars are
P9557 The apparent resistance of such a series will increase, and if the dielectric of the jars is a perfect insulator it will increase without limit. If the electromotive force be removed and connexion made
P9558 between the ends of the series, a reverse current will be observed, the total quantity of which, in the case of perfect insulation, will be the same as that of the direct current. Similar effects are observed
P9559 in the case of the secondary pile, with the difference that the final insulation is not so good, and that the capacity per unit of surface
P9560 is immensely greater. In the case of the cable covered with gutta-percha, &c., it is found
P9561 that after applying the battery for half an hour, and then con necting the wire with the external electrode, a reverse current takes place, which goes on for some time, and gradually reduces the system to its original state.
P9562 These phenomena are of the same kind with those indicated by the residual discharge of the Leyden jar, except that the amount of the polarization is much greater in gutta-percha, &c. than in glass.
P9563 This state of polarization seems to be a directed property of the material, which requires for its production not only electromotive force, but the passage, by displacement or otherwise, of a con 368.] OF DIELECTRICS. 423
P9564 siderable quantity of electricity, and this passage requires a con
P9565 siderable time. When the polarized state has been set up, there
P9566 is an internal electromotive force 1 in the substance in the
P9567 reverse direction, which will continue till it has either produced
P9568 a reversed current equal in total quantity to the first, or till the
P9569 state of polarization has quietly subsided by means of true con
P9570 duction through the substance.
P9571 The whole theory of what has been called residual discharge,
P9572 absorption of electricity, electrification, or polarization,, deserves
P9573 a careful investigation, and will probably lead to important dis
P9574 coveries relating to the internal structure of bodies.
P9575 367.] The resistance of the greater number of dielectrics di
P9576 minishes as the temperature rises.
P9577 Thus the resistance of gutta-percha is about twenty times as great
P9578 at 0C as at 24C. Messrs. Bright and Clark have found that the
P9579 following formula gives results agreeing with their experiments.
P9580 If r is* the resistance of gutta-percha at temperature T centigrade,
P9581 then the resistance at temperature T+ 1 will be PC = r x 0.8878 ,
P9582 the number varies between 0.8878 and 0.9.
P9583 Mr. Hockin has verified the curious fact that it is not until some
P9584 hours after the gutta-percha has taken its temperature that the resistance reaches its corresponding value.
P9585 The effect of temperature on the resistance of india-rubber is not so great as on that of gutta-percha.
P9586 The resistance of gutta-percha increases considerably on the
P9587 application of pressure.

P9589 gutta-percha used in different cables is as follows *.

P9598 369.] Mr. C. F. * has recently investigated the conditions Varley
P9599 of the current through rarefied gases, and finds that the electro
P9600 motive force E is equal to a constant EQ together with a part
P9601 depending on the current according to Ohm s Law, thus
P9602 For instance, the electromotive force required to cause the current to begin in a certain tube was that of 323 Dani ell s cells, but an electromotive force of 304 cells was just sufficient to maintain the current. The intensity of the current, as measured by the galvanometer, was proportional to the number of cells above 304. Thus for 305 cells the deflexion was 2, for 306 it was 4,
P9603 for 307 it was 6, and so on up to 380, or 304 + 76 for which the
P9604 deflexion was 150, or 76 x 1.97.
P9605 From these experiments it appears that there is a kind of
P9606 polarization of the electrodes, the electromotive force of which is equal to that of 304 DanielFs cells, and that up to this electro motive force the battery is occupied in establishing this state of
P9607 polarization. When the maximum polarization is established, the
P9608 excess of electromotive force above that of 304 cells is devoted to
P9609 maintaining the current according to Ohm s Law.
P9610 The law of the current in a rarefied gas is therefore very similar to the law of the current through an electrolyte in which we have
P9611 to take account of the polarization of the electrodes.
P9612 In connexion with this subject we should study Thomson s results,
P9613 described in Art. 57, in which the electromotive force required to produce a spark in air was found to be proportional not to the distance, but to the distance together with a constant quantity. The electromotive force corresponding to this constant quantity
P9614 may be regarded as the intensity of polarization of the electrodes.
P9615 370.] MM. Wiedemann and Ruhlmann have recently f investi
P9616 * Proc. E. S., Jan. 12, 1871. f Serichte der Konigl. Sachs. Gesellschafl, Oct. 20, 1871. 370.] OF DIELECTRICS. 425
P9617 gated the passage of electricity through gases. The electric current was produced by Holtz s machine, and the discharge took place between spherical electrodes within a metallic vessel containing rarefied gas. The discharge was in general discontinuous, and the interval of time between successive discharges was measured by means of a mirror revolving along with the axis of Holtz s machine. The images of the series of discharges were observed by means of a heliometer with a divided object-glass, which was adjusted till one image of each discharge coincided with the other image of
P9618 the next discharge. By this method very consistent results were
P9619 obtained. It was found that the quantity of electricity in each discharge is independent of the strength of the current and of the material of the electrodes, and that it depends on the nature and density of the gas, and on the distance and form of the
P9620 These researches confirm the statement of Faraday* that the
P9621 electric tension (see Art. 48) required to cause a disruptive discharge to begin at the electrified surface of a conductor is a little less
P9622 when the electrification is negative than when it is positive, but that when a discharge does take place, much more electricity passes at each discharge when it begins at a positive surface. They also
P9623 tend to support the hypothesis stated in Art. 57, that the stratum of gas condensed on the surface of the electrode plays an important part in the phenomenon, and they indicate that this condensation is greatest at the positive electrode.
